# Daily Summary: 2025-06-02

### Measure gradients, not activations! Enhancing neuronal activity in deep reinforcement learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24061v1)
- **Authors**: Jiashun Liu, Zihao Wu, Johan Obando-Ceron, Pablo Samuel Castro, Aaron Courville, Ling Pan
- **Abstract**: Deep reinforcement learning (RL) agents frequently suffer from neuronal activity loss, which impairs their ability to adapt to new data and learn continually. A common method to quantify and address this issue is the tau-dormant neuron ratio, which uses activation statistics to measure the expressive ability of neurons. While effective for simple MLP-based agents, this approach loses statistical power in more complex architectures. To address this, we argue that in advanced RL agents, maintaining a neuron's learning capacity, its ability to adapt via gradient updates, is more critical than preserving its expressive ability. Based on this insight, we shift the statistical objective from activations to gradients, and introduce GraMa (Gradient Magnitude Neural Activity Metric), a lightweight, architecture-agnostic metric for quantifying neuron-level learning capacity. We show that GraMa effectively reveals persistent neuron inactivity across diverse architectures, including residual networks, diffusion models, and agents with varied activation functions. Moreover, resetting neurons guided by GraMa (ReGraMa) consistently improves learning performance across multiple deep RL algorithms and benchmarks, such as MuJoCo and the DeepMind Control Suite.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of neuronal activity loss in deep reinforcement learning (DRL) agents, which hinders their ability to learn and adapt to new environments.  It argues that existing methods for quantifying and mitigating this issue, which rely on activation-based metrics, become less effective in modern, complex DRL architectures. The authors propose a new metric called GraMa (Gradient Magnitude Neural Activity Metric), which uses the magnitude of gradients instead of activations to assess a neuron's learning capacity.  They then introduce ReGraMa, a neuron resetting mechanism guided by GraMa, and demonstrate that it consistently improves learning performance across various DRL algorithms and benchmarks compared to activation-based approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:**  The paper tackles a significant problem in DRL: neuronal activity loss and its impact on continual learning and adaptation. This is a recognized limitation of DRL agents.
    *   **Insight and Motivation:** The core idea of shifting from activation-based to gradient-based metrics for assessing neuronal health is insightful. It directly addresses the limitations of activation-based methods in complex architectures where activations may not accurately reflect learning capacity.
    *   **Technical Contribution:** GraMa appears to be a relatively simple and efficient metric to compute, requiring only gradient information readily available during training.  ReGraMa leverages this efficiency to provide a practical neuron resetting mechanism.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across diverse architectures (including residual networks, diffusion models) and benchmarks (MuJoCo, DeepMind Control Suite). The results consistently demonstrate the superiority of ReGraMa over activation-based methods like ReDo. The ablations controlling neuron proportions further strengthens their analysis.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides theoretical justification, especially the Theorem which suggests the gradient will be close to zero for a ReLu that doesn't activate and relates it to GraMa.
    *   **Well-written and clear:** The paper is well written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the shift from activations to gradients is insightful, the overall approach is still based on the well-established framework of neuron resetting. The novelty lies primarily in the metric used to *identify* which neurons to reset.
    *   **Limited theoretical Depth:** While the theorem helps show the relationship between reDo and GraMa, there is a lack of more theoretical justification about the convergence of GraMa or how to set the threshold parameter in a more principled way.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The effectiveness of ReGraMa relies on the threshold parameter (τ), and while the appendix indicates that it is robust to the parameter, there is still a need for an extra hyperparameter.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper offers a practical and effective solution to a recognized problem in DRL. The GraMa metric and ReGraMa resetting mechanism are easy to implement and can be readily adopted by the DRL community.
    *   The findings highlight the limitations of existing neuron-level analysis techniques in modern DRL architectures, encouraging further research into more robust and informative metrics.
    *   The improved performance achieved by ReGraMa can contribute to the development of more robust and adaptable DRL agents for real-world applications.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of DRL. The central insight of using gradients to assess neuron health is well-motivated and supported by strong empirical evidence. While the overall approach is incremental, building upon existing neuron resetting techniques, the improvement in performance and robustness justifies its value. The limitations are its somewhat limited scope and the need to tune the hyperparameter. Therefore a reasonable score is:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### TCM-Ladder: A Benchmark for Multimodal Question Answering on Traditional Chinese Medicine
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24063v1)
- **Authors**: Jiacheng Xie, Yang Yu, Ziyang Zhang, Shuai Zeng, Jiaxuan He, Ayush Vasireddy, Xiaoting Tang, Congyu Guo, Lening Zhao, Congcong Jing, Guanghui An, Dong Xu
- **Abstract**: Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM), as an effective alternative medicine, has been receiving increasing attention. In recent years, the rapid development of large language models (LLMs) tailored for TCM has underscored the need for an objective and comprehensive evaluation framework to assess their performance on real-world tasks. However, existing evaluation datasets are limited in scope and primarily text-based, lacking a unified and standardized multimodal question-answering (QA) benchmark. To address this issue, we introduce TCM-Ladder, the first multimodal QA dataset specifically designed for evaluating large TCM language models. The dataset spans multiple core disciplines of TCM, including fundamental theory, diagnostics, herbal formulas, internal medicine, surgery, pharmacognosy, and pediatrics. In addition to textual content, TCM-Ladder incorporates various modalities such as images and videos. The datasets were constructed using a combination of automated and manual filtering processes and comprise 52,000+ questions in total. These questions include single-choice, multiple-choice, fill-in-the-blank, diagnostic dialogue, and visual comprehension tasks. We trained a reasoning model on TCM-Ladder and conducted comparative experiments against 9 state-of-the-art general domain and 5 leading TCM-specific LLMs to evaluate their performance on the datasets. Moreover, we propose Ladder-Score, an evaluation method specifically designed for TCM question answering that effectively assesses answer quality regarding terminology usage and semantic expression. To our knowledge, this is the first work to evaluate mainstream general domain and TCM-specific LLMs on a unified multimodal benchmark. The datasets and leaderboard are publicly available at https://tcmladder.com or https://54.211.107.106 and will be continuously updated.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper abstract and related content, and here's a summary and critical evaluation.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TCM-Ladder, a new multimodal benchmark dataset for evaluating large language models (LLMs) in the domain of Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM). The dataset encompasses a broad range of TCM knowledge areas (fundamental theory, diagnostics, herbal formulas, etc.) and includes various modalities like text, images (herbal medicine, tongues), audio, and video. The dataset contains over 52,000 questions across different task formats (single-choice, multiple-choice, fill-in-the-blank, diagnostic dialogue, and visual comprehension). The authors trained reasoning models on TCM-Ladder and benchmarked several general-purpose and TCM-specific LLMs. They also propose a novel evaluation metric called `Ladder-Score`, designed to assess the quality of answers with respect to TCM terminology and semantic accuracy. The dataset and leaderboard are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a contribution by providing a more comprehensive and multi-modal benchmark dataset for TCM than prior ones. The move beyond solely text-based data is well-justified, as TCM diagnostic methods are intrinsically multimodal. The novel `Ladder-Score` also appears to be a helpful development, as traditional metrics may not properly capture the nuances of TCM. The creation of the dataset itself is also fairly significant, given how little curated TCM training data is available to the public.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key need in the field: the lack of standardized evaluation methods for TCM-focused LLMs. By providing a dataset and evaluation metric, the authors make a step towards enabling fair comparisons and driving further development of such models. A key weakness for LLMs has been access to high quality training data, particularly for specific professional fields such as medicine and law, so this paper makes an important contribution towards data access. The breadth and depth of its coverage, as well as the inclusion of multimodal components, makes TCM-Ladder a useful tool for evaluating models with a more realistic context of TCM applications. By benchmarking leading general domain and specialized LLMs, this paper provides the first step in setting performance comparisons and baselines, and creating the potential for advancements in LLMs tailored for TCM.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Multimodal nature:** Addresses a significant limitation of existing TCM datasets.
    *   **Comprehensive coverage:** Spans various TCM disciplines.
    *   **Task Diversity:** Offers multiple task formats for thorough model evaluation.
    *   **Public Availability:** Encourages community participation and further research.
    *   **Novel Metric:** The proposed Ladder-Score helps evaluate responses in TCM contexts.
    *   **Benchmarking:** Comparative analysis of general and TCM-specific LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited analysis of bias:** There is no discussion of potential biases present in the source materials or annotation process, which is an important consideration in any dataset, particularly in medicine.
    *   **Lack of focus on safety:** The paper does not focus on potential safety concerns associated with LLMs in TCM, such as the generation of incorrect or misleading diagnostic advice.
    *   **Missing Information:** Ladder-base is not equipped with image understanding at this stage. Their current design focuses on structured text-based TCM evaluation and does not support visual input.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the development and evaluation of LLMs for TCM. The multi-modal approach and comprehensive task structure are major strengths. The novel `Ladder-Score` is useful, but would need further validation by comparing to clinicians' assessments. Considering the weaknesses, the paper provides a strong advancement in data access and evaluation in TCM, however is a bit weak on broader discussion on safety and bias.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DSR-Bench: Evaluating the Structural Reasoning Abilities of LLMs via Data Structures
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24069v1)
- **Authors**: Yu He, Yingxi Li, Colin White, Ellen Vitercik
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed for real-world tasks that fundamentally involve data manipulation. A core requirement across these tasks is the ability to perform structural reasoning--that is, to understand and reason about data relationships. For example, customer requests require a temporal ordering, which can be represented by data structures such as queues. However, existing benchmarks primarily focus on high-level, application-driven evaluations without isolating this fundamental capability. To address this gap, we introduce DSR-Bench, a novel benchmark evaluating LLMs' structural reasoning capabilities through data structures, which provide interpretable representations of data relationships. DSR-Bench includes 20 data structures, 35 operations, and 4,140 problem instances, organized hierarchically for fine-grained analysis of reasoning limitations. Our evaluation pipeline is fully automated and deterministic, eliminating subjective human or model-based judgments. Its synthetic nature also ensures scalability and minimizes data contamination risks. We benchmark nine state-of-the-art LLMs. Our analysis shows that instruction-tuned models struggle with basic multi-attribute and multi-hop reasoning. Furthermore, while reasoning-oriented models perform better, they remain fragile on complex and hybrid structures, with the best model achieving an average score of only 47% on the challenge subset. Crucially, models often perform poorly on multi-dimensional data and natural language task descriptions, highlighting a critical gap for real-world deployment.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces DSR-Bench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the structural reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) using data structure manipulation tasks. The benchmark includes 20 data structures, 35 operations, and 4,140 problem instances, organized hierarchically to enable fine-grained analysis. The evaluation pipeline is fully automated and deterministic, using synthetic data to minimize contamination risks. The authors evaluate nine state-of-the-art LLMs, revealing that instruction-tuned models struggle with multi-attribute and multi-hop reasoning, and even reasoning-oriented models exhibit fragility on complex structures. Furthermore, performance degrades on multi-dimensional data and natural language task descriptions, highlighting a gap in real-world deployment potential. The authors release their code and data to encourage community engagement and further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its focus on isolating and evaluating a very specific aspect of LLM reasoning: structural reasoning as demonstrated via data structure manipulation. While previous benchmarks touch on reasoning, they often lack the granular control and deterministic evaluation offered by DSR-Bench. Defining this domain explicitly and creating a targeted benchmark is a definite contribution. The extension suites addressing spatial and natural language understanding enhance the novelty as well.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is moderate. Structural reasoning is indeed a crucial component in many real-world applications, and the insights gained from DSR-Bench can inform the development of more robust and reliable LLMs. The findings, particularly concerning the struggles with multi-attribute reasoning, complex spatial data, and natural language descriptions, are useful. The hierarchical organization is valuable for identifying specific failure points. However, the tasks remain inherently synthetic. The benchmark's practical impact hinges on whether performance on these simplified tasks reliably translates to improvements in real-world application scenarios. The data contamination argument is weak, given the vast pre-training datasets, and could be better explained.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined benchmark with clear tasks and deterministic evaluation.
    *   Hierarchical organization allows for fine-grained analysis.
    *   Synthetic data minimizes contamination risks.
    *   Identifies key limitations of LLMs in structural reasoning.
    *   Release of code and data promotes reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Tasks are synthetic and may not fully reflect real-world complexity.
    *   While contamination is reduced, the assumption that this is a major problem for data structures could be argued.
    *   Some explanations of behavior (e.g. edges instead of node-count) could be strengthened with ablation studies.
    *   Significance argument could be stronger with real-world correlation.
    *   Presentation, specifically the charts, could be improved.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by introducing a novel and well-defined benchmark targeting a specific reasoning ability of LLMs. While the tasks are somewhat synthetic and the long-term impact remains to be seen, the insights gained from the evaluation of state-of-the-art models, the hierarchical design of the benchmark, and the release of resources justify a rating above average. The paper needs real-world correlation to justify it as truly groundbreaking.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Principal Context-aware Diffusion Guided Data Augmentation for Fault Localization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24079v1)
- **Authors**: Shihao Fu, Yan Lei
- **Abstract**: Test cases are indispensable for conducting effective fault localization (FL). However, test cases in practice are severely class imbalanced, i.e. the number of failing test cases (i.e. minority class) is much less than that of passing ones (i.e. majority class). The severe class imbalance between failing and passing test cases have hindered the FL effectiveness. To address this issue, we propose PCD-DAug: a Principal Context-aware Diffusion guided Data Augmentation approach that generate synthesized failing test cases for improving FL. PCD-DAug first combines program slicing with principal component analysis to construct a principal context that shows how a set of statements influences the faulty output via statistical program dependencies. Then, PCD-DAug devises a conditional diffusion model to learn from principle contexts for generating synthesized failing test cases and acquiring a class balanced dataset for FL. We conducted large-scale experiments on six state-of-the-art FL approaches and compare PCD-DAug with six data augmentation baselines. The results show that PCD-DAug significantly improves FL effectiveness, e.g. achieving average improvements of 383.83%, 227.08%, and 224.19% in six FL approaches under the metrics Top-1, Top-3, and Top-5, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PCD-DAug, a novel data augmentation approach for improving fault localization (FL).  PCD-DAug addresses the challenge of class imbalance (fewer failing test cases than passing ones) which hinders the effectiveness of FL techniques. The approach combines dynamic program slicing and principal component analysis (PCA) to construct a "principal context" representing the semantic and statistical dependencies influencing faulty outputs. A conditional diffusion model then learns from this context to generate synthetic failing test cases, creating a more balanced dataset for FL.  The authors conduct extensive experiments across several benchmarks and demonstrate significant improvements in FL effectiveness compared to existing data augmentation techniques and state-of-the-art FL methods.  They utilize metrics like Top-K accuracy, Mean Average Rank (MAR), Mean First Rank (MFR), and Relative Improvement (RImp) to show the performance gains.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the synergistic combination of different techniques: Dynamic program slicing, a revised PCA, and a conditional diffusion model. While each of these components has been used individually or in different combinations in software engineering before, PCD-DAug's integration of them for *fault localization*, guided by both *semantic and statistical program dependencies*, is relatively novel. Traditional approaches often rely solely on semantic or statistical information separately or struggle with the complexities of GAN/VAE training.  The use of a diffusion model in this particular context offers a potentially more stable and efficient generation of augmented data compared to GANs and CVAEs. However, the novelty is incremental, building upon existing data augmentation approaches and FL techniques.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in addressing a long-standing challenge in FL: class imbalance.  By generating high-quality synthetic failing test cases, the approach demonstrably improves the accuracy and efficiency of both spectrum-based and deep learning-based FL techniques. The experimental results are compelling, showing substantial improvements across several benchmarks and metrics. This could have a practical impact by making FL more effective, reducing debugging time and effort for developers.  The detailed comparison with other data augmentation methods provides strong evidence for the superiority of the proposed approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:**  PCD-DAug's modular design allows for combining semantic (program slicing) and statistical (revised PCA) context which provides more meaningful guidance for data synthesis.
    *   **Strong Experimental Validation:** The large-scale experiments on multiple benchmarks and comparisons with several state-of-the-art techniques provide solid evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Addressing a Real Problem:** The paper tackles a practical problem (class imbalance in FL) that hinders the effectiveness of existing techniques.
    *   **Differentiable to other FL data augmentation methods** PCD-DAug is differentiable to other FL data augmentation methods for utilizing the deep learning generative model for synthesizing coverage information

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The approach involves several complex components (program slicing, PCA, diffusion models), potentially increasing the barrier to entry for practitioners wanting to adopt it.
    *   **Parameter Tuning:** While the authors claim robustness across datasets with a fixed set of hyperparameters, fine-tuning might be necessary for optimal performance in specific contexts.  The hyperparameter optimization process itself is not discussed in detail, potentially making the results harder to replicate perfectly.
    *   **Scalability:** While the diffusion model is claimed to be efficient, generating synthetic data for very large and complex systems might still pose a scalability challenge. The paper doesn't delve deep into analyzing the computational cost of PCD-DAug, particularly the data synthesis step.
    *   **Generalizability:** Though experimented on several benchmarks, the approach might not generalize equally well to all types of programs or fault scenarios. Some limited performance decreases are noticed when the hypeparameters were not adjusted.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in FL and data augmentation for software engineering. It demonstrates the benefits of combining different analysis techniques and using deep generative models for creating more realistic and balanced datasets.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of fault localization by addressing the challenging problem of class imbalance using a novel data augmentation approach. The strong experimental results and detailed comparisons with existing techniques demonstrate the effectiveness of PCD-DAug. While there are some limitations related to complexity and potential scalability challenges, the overall contribution is significant.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper's novelty and significance warrant a score of 7.5. The novelty is incremental (combining existing techniques in a new way), but the strong experimental results and practical impact justify the score. The paper effectively addresses a real problem in fault localization, providing a viable solution supported by solid evidence. The comprehensiveness of the experimental comparisons provides added value, which is why it edges a score slightly over 7. The complexity of the proposed method, which hinders adoption of the algorithm, keeps it from a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### ComposeAnything: Composite Object Priors for Text-to-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24086v1)
- **Authors**: Zeeshan Khan, Shizhe Chen, Cordelia Schmid
- **Abstract**: Generating images from text involving complex and novel object arrangements remains a significant challenge for current text-to-image (T2I) models. Although prior layout-based methods improve object arrangements using spatial constraints with 2D layouts, they often struggle to capture 3D positioning and sacrifice quality and coherence. In this work, we introduce ComposeAnything, a novel framework for improving compositional image generation without retraining existing T2I models. Our approach first leverages the chain-of-thought reasoning abilities of LLMs to produce 2.5D semantic layouts from text, consisting of 2D object bounding boxes enriched with depth information and detailed captions. Based on this layout, we generate a spatial and depth aware coarse composite of objects that captures the intended composition, serving as a strong and interpretable prior that replaces stochastic noise initialization in diffusion-based T2I models. This prior guides the denoising process through object prior reinforcement and spatial-controlled denoising, enabling seamless generation of compositional objects and coherent backgrounds, while allowing refinement of inaccurate priors. ComposeAnything outperforms state-of-the-art methods on the T2I-CompBench and NSR-1K benchmarks for prompts with 2D/3D spatial arrangements, high object counts, and surreal compositions. Human evaluations further demonstrate that our model generates high-quality images with compositions that faithfully reflect the text.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ComposeAnything: Composite Object Priors for Text-to-Image Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper "ComposeAnything" addresses the challenge of generating high-quality images from complex text descriptions involving intricate object arrangements.  It proposes a novel, training-free framework that enhances compositional image generation in existing text-to-image (T2I) models. The core idea is to leverage large language models (LLMs) to create 2.5D semantic layouts from text, which include object captions, bounding boxes, and depth information. These layouts are then used to synthesize a coarse composite image, acting as a "composite object prior" that replaces random noise initialization in diffusion-based T2I models. A "prior-guided diffusion" module refines the image through object prior reinforcement and spatial-controlled denoising. The framework is evaluated on challenging benchmarks, demonstrating improved compositional accuracy and image quality compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of techniques, rather than groundbreaking individual advancements. Using LLMs for layout generation and diffusion models for image synthesis are not new concepts in themselves. However, the specific way the authors integrate these elements is where the novelty lies. The chain-of-thought approach for extracting 2.5D semantic layouts and the subsequent use of these layouts to generate a composite object prior are a distinct contribution. The prior guided diffusion, combining prior reinforcement and spatial controlled denoising, is also a novel component of the work. This is a clever way to use existing T2I models without retraining them. However, the extent of novelty can be debated, as it is largely an engineering effort building upon existing techniques.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant limitation of current T2I models: generating images with complex and novel object arrangements. By improving compositional accuracy and visual quality, the paper takes a step towards more controllable and reliable T2I generation. The method's training-free nature and its compatibility with existing diffusion models are also significant advantages. This framework has potential real-world applications, allowing users to generate specific scenes from detailed textual descriptions with more confidence and better results. The performance on benchmark tasks and the supportive human evaluations further solidify the significance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper provides convincing quantitative and qualitative results demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
    *   **Well-defined framework:** The different components of the system (LLM planning, object prior generation, prior-guided diffusion) are clearly explained and well-integrated.
    *   **Training-free approach:** Enhancing existing T2I models without requiring retraining is a practical advantage, making the framework more readily accessible and applicable.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The evaluation includes both automatic metrics and human evaluations, providing a thorough assessment of the framework's performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLM quality:** The performance of the system heavily relies on the accuracy of the LLM-generated layouts. Errors in the layout can propagate through the pipeline and negatively affect the final image. This reliance on an external tool (LLM) introduces a dependency.
    *   **Complexity:** The framework involves several steps, which can make it more complex to implement and optimize.
    *   **Limited Ablation Study:** While some ablation is performed, there could be more investigation into the individual impact of the LLM planning step.

*   **Potential Influence:**  "ComposeAnything" can influence future research by offering a valuable approach to improve compositional image generation in T2I models. It can also serve as a foundation for further exploration of LLM-driven layout generation and prior-guided diffusion techniques. The benchmarks and evaluation metrics could also become standard practice in the field. The code release will enable other researchers to build on this work.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**  While the paper builds upon existing techniques, the novel combination of these techniques, the effectiveness of the proposed framework, and the detailed experimentation contribute significantly to the field of text-to-image generation. The approach of composing images from LLM-derived layouts, while conceptually accessible, required considerable engineering effort and presents practical benefits. The work is not a paradigm shift but a valuable and impactful improvement upon existing methods. The strong empirical validation and training-free nature make it a potentially influential contribution to the field. The dependency on the LLM and the complexity of the pipeline are minor weaknesses that prevent the score from being even higher.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SkyLB: A Locality-Aware Cross-Region Load Balancer for LLM Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24095v1)
- **Authors**: Tian Xia, Ziming Mao, Jamison Kerney, Ethan J. Jackson, Zhifei Li, Jiarong Xing, Scott Shenker, Ion Stoica
- **Abstract**: Serving Large Language Models (LLMs) efficiently in multi-region setups remains a challenge. Due to cost and GPU availability concerns, providers typically deploy LLMs in multiple regions using instance with long-term commitments, like reserved instances or on-premise clusters, which are often underutilized due to their region-local traffic handling and diurnal traffic variance. In this paper, we introduce SkyLB, a locality-aware multi-region load balancer for LLM inference that aggregates regional diurnal patterns through cross-region traffic handling. By doing so, SkyLB enables providers to reserve instances based on expected global demand, rather than peak demand in each individual region. Meanwhile, SkyLB preserves KV-Cache locality and a balanced load, ensuring cost efficiency without sacrificing performance. SkyLB achieves this with a cache-aware cross-region traffic handler and a selective pushing load balancing mechanism based on checking pending requests. Our evaluation on real-world workloads shows that it achieves 1.12-2.06x higher throughput and 1.74-6.30x lower latency compared to existing load balancers, while reducing total serving cost by 25%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SkyLB: A Locality-Aware Cross-Region Load Balancer for LLM Inference":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SkyLB, a locality-aware cross-region load balancer designed for efficient LLM inference in multi-region deployments.  SkyLB aims to address the underutilization of resources in these deployments caused by region-specific peak demand and diurnal traffic variations. It enables providers to reserve instances based on global demand rather than individual region peaks by allowing cross-region traffic handling while preserving KV-Cache locality. SkyLB achieves this through a cache-aware cross-region traffic handler and a selective pushing load balancing mechanism based on pending requests. Evaluations demonstrate improved throughput and lower latency compared to existing load balancers, alongside a reduction in total serving costs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper demonstrates reasonable novelty by tackling a practical and relevant problem in LLM serving: efficient resource utilization in multi-region deployments. While load balancing and cache-aware routing are not entirely new concepts, their adaptation and combination within the context of geo-distributed LLM inference, considering unique challenges like KV-Cache locality and unpredictable LLM request characteristics, constitutes a meaningful contribution.  The two-layer cross-region routing approach and selective pushing mechanism, specifically tailored to the unpredictable nature of LLM inference, are key innovations. The prefix tree maintenance across regions isn't groundbreaking, but the snapshot idea offers a practical compromise between full consistency and performance.

**Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the cost-effectiveness and scalability of LLM serving.  Reducing resource underutilization and enabling better capacity planning are critical as LLM adoption grows. The demonstrated improvements in throughput and latency directly translate to better user experiences and lower operational costs for LLM providers.  The evaluation using real-world workloads strengthens the practical relevance of the findings.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Problem:** The paper addresses a real and pressing challenge in LLM serving.
*   **Well-Defined Solution:** SkyLB is clearly described with well-motivated components.
*   **Effective Techniques:** The combination of techniques (cross-region routing, prefix-aware routing, selective pushing) is well-integrated and tailored to LLM characteristics.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The evaluation methodology is thorough, comparing SkyLB against several baselines and using real-world workloads.  The microbenchmarks provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of selective pushing.
*   **Cost-Benefit Analysis:** Quantifying the cost savings makes the contribution more compelling.
*   **Open Source Release:** The availability of the SkyLB codebase fosters further research and adoption.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:**  While the two-layer routing simplifies the system, deployment can still be complex given the need for coordination across regions.
*   **Regional Dependency:**  The analysis relies on the diurnal load pattern which may vary over time or depending on the application. In extreme cases where the load is distributed equally across all regions, the benefit might be limited.
*   **Limitations on KV Cache:**  SkyLB's cache strategy can still have potential cases which leads to cache misses due to heterogeneous use cases. This could be a focus of further study.
*   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation focuses primarily on one model size (Llama-3.1-8B). Performance and cost benefits might vary with different model architectures or sizes.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, the paper represents a **good** contribution to the field of LLM serving, with practical relevance and reasonable novelty. It is well-executed with solid experimental results and has a practical aspect through the provided open source release of SkyLB. The identified weaknesses suggest avenues for future research, but do not detract significantly from the paper's overall value.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Training LLMs for EHR-Based Reasoning Tasks via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24105v1)
- **Authors**: Jiacheng Lin, Zhenbang Wu, Jimeng Sun
- **Abstract**: We present EHRMIND, a practical recipe for adapting large language models (LLMs) to complex clinical reasoning tasks using reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). While RLVR has succeeded in mathematics and coding, its application to healthcare contexts presents unique challenges due to the specialized knowledge and reasoning required for electronic health record (EHR) interpretation. Our pilot study on the MEDCALC benchmark reveals two key failure modes: (1) misapplied knowledge, where models possess relevant medical knowledge but apply it incorrectly, and (2) missing knowledge, where models lack essential domain knowledge. To address these cases, EHRMIND applies a two-stage solution: a lightweight supervised fine-tuning (SFT) warm-up that injects missing domain knowledge, stabilizes subsequent training, and encourages structured, interpretable outputs; followed by RLVR, which reinforces outcome correctness and refines the model's decision-making. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method across diverse clinical applications, including medical calculations (MEDCALC), patient-trial matching (TREC CLINICAL TRIALS), and disease diagnosis (EHRSHOT). EHRMIND delivers consistent gains in accuracy, interpretability, and cross-task generalization. These findings offer practical guidance for applying RLVR to enhance LLM capabilities in healthcare settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EHRMIND, a practical approach for adapting large language models (LLMs) to complex clinical reasoning tasks using reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR).  The authors address challenges specific to healthcare applications, where specialized knowledge and reasoning over electronic health records (EHRs) are essential. EHRMIND employs a two-stage solution: a supervised fine-tuning (SFT) warm-up to inject missing domain knowledge and stabilize training, followed by RLVR to reinforce outcome correctness and refine decision-making.  The method is evaluated on medical calculations (MEDCALC), patient-trial matching (TREC CLINICAL TRIALS), and disease diagnosis (EHRSHOT), demonstrating improvements in accuracy, interpretability, and cross-task generalization. The authors also introduce "Pass@k" as a practical indicator to guide the use of the SFT warm-up.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of RLVR to complex EHR-based clinical reasoning tasks is reasonably novel. While RLVR has seen success in other domains, the complexities of EHR data and medical knowledge pose unique challenges. The identification of "misapplied knowledge" and "missing knowledge" as key failure modes is insightful. Introducing a lightweight SFT warm-up phase before RLVR to address "missing knowledge" and stabilize training appears to be a crucial contribution. The use of Pass@k as a trigger for employing the SFT approach further enhances the practical application of the framework.

*   **Significance:**  The work has significant potential impact for several reasons:

    *   **Practicality:**  The approach emphasizes practical application, providing a recipe for adapting LLMs to healthcare.  The focus on rule-based rewards simplifies the RL pipeline and makes it more robust.
    *   **Interpretability:**  The method aims to improve interpretability by guiding the model to generate structured, clinically meaningful reasoning paths. This is vital for trust and adoption in high-stakes medical domains.
    *   **Performance:**  The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on several benchmarks, even with relatively small (3B) models. This indicates the efficiency of the approach.
    *   **Generalizability:**  The method shows robust generalization across multiple clinical tasks, making it potentially applicable to a wider range of healthcare problems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of applying RLVR to EHR-based reasoning.
    *   **Effective solution:** The two-stage EHRMIND approach addresses these challenges effectively.
    *   **Strong empirical results:**  The paper provides extensive experimental results across multiple datasets, demonstrating the benefits of EHRMIND.
    *   **Practical insights:**  The use of Pass@k and analysis of failure modes provide valuable practical guidance.
    *   Clear and Concise Presentation: The approach, the results, and their significance is very clearly articulated throughout the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited model scale:** All model training is conducted on the 3B LLaMA-3 backbone. Although this reduces computational overheads and makes experiments possible on the resources available to the authors, it limits our ability to know if a higher-performing model can be achieved with the same training recipe.
    *   **Synthetic data for SFT:** While the study uses EHR data, the SFT datasets rely on LLM-generated explanations. The performance improvements seen during experimentation could be amplified by leveraging real data with manually generated reasoning chains.
    *   **Reliance on Rule-Based Rewards:** The effectiveness of RLVR depends heavily on the quality and availability of rule-based reward functions. While these offer advantages in stability, they might not be suitable for all clinical tasks, particularly those requiring more nuanced or subjective evaluations.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper provides a solid foundation for future work on applying RLVR to healthcare. It highlights the importance of domain knowledge injection and structured reasoning in this context. Future research could explore:

    *   Scalability:  Applying EHRMIND to larger LLMs and more complex clinical tasks.
    *   Reward function design:  Developing more sophisticated reward functions that capture multiple dimensions of clinical quality.
    *   Integration with real-world clinical workflows: Assessing the impact of EHRMIND on actual clinical decision-making.

**Score: 8.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a highly relevant, practical, and well-validated approach to adapting LLMs for clinical reasoning. The identified challenges and proposed solutions are insightful, and the empirical results demonstrate significant improvements. The introduction of Pass@k and the practical recipes make the work directly applicable to the field. The reliance on synthetic data for SFT dataset construction poses a limitation but is mitigated by the strong performance across multiple benchmarks. Overall, this work represents a substantial contribution with the potential to advance the use of LLMs in healthcare.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### R-KV: Redundancy-aware KV Cache Compression for Training-Free Reasoning Models Acceleration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24133v1)
- **Authors**: Zefan Cai, Wen Xiao, Hanshi Sun, Cheng Luo, Yikai Zhang, Ke Wan, Yucheng Li, Yeyang Zhou, Li-Wen Chang, Jiuxiang Gu, Zhen Dong, Anima Anandkumar, Abedelkadir Asi, Junjie Hu
- **Abstract**: Reasoning models have demonstrated impressive performance in self-reflection and chain-of-thought reasoning. However, they often produce excessively long outputs, leading to prohibitively large key-value (KV) caches during inference. While chain-of-thought inference significantly improves performance on complex reasoning tasks, it can also lead to reasoning failures when deployed with existing KV cache compression approaches. To address this, we propose Redundancy-aware KV Cache Compression for Reasoning models (R-KV), a novel method specifically targeting redundant tokens in reasoning models. Our method preserves nearly 100% of the full KV cache performance using only 10% of the KV cache, substantially outperforming existing KV cache baselines, which reach only 60% of the performance. Remarkably, R-KV even achieves 105% of full KV cache performance with 16% of the KV cache. This KV-cache reduction also leads to a 90% memory saving and a 6.6X throughput over standard chain-of-thought reasoning inference. Experimental results show that R-KV consistently outperforms existing KV cache compression baselines across two mathematical reasoning datasets.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a novelty and significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper "R-KV: Redundancy-aware KV Cache Compression for Training-Free Reasoning Models Acceleration" proposes a novel method for compressing the key-value (KV) cache in reasoning models. These models, while demonstrating impressive reasoning abilities, generate excessively long outputs, resulting in high memory demands during inference. The authors observe that these long outputs often contain significant redundancy.  R-KV addresses this redundancy by incorporating importance scoring of tokens (based on attention weights) along with a redundancy estimation mechanism (based on key vector similarity).  A joint selection strategy balances importance and non-redundancy during token eviction. Experiments on mathematical reasoning datasets (MATH-500 and AIME24) show that R-KV significantly reduces KV cache size while maintaining or even improving model performance, outperforming existing KV cache compression techniques. The paper demonstrates memory savings, throughput improvements, and offers a training-free, model-agnostic solution for accelerating reasoning models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty:** The paper's main strength lies in its explicit focus on redundancy within the KV cache of reasoning models.  While previous compression methods mainly targeted long input prompts and relied on attention, R-KV addresses the redundancy inherent in the *generated* outputs of these models. The combination of importance scoring *and* redundancy estimation in a joint selection strategy is a novel approach.
    * **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in deploying large reasoning models.  Reducing the KV cache size directly translates to lower memory requirements, enabling faster inference, and supporting larger batch sizes. The demonstrated throughput improvements are significant and practically valuable.  The fact that the method is training-free and model-agnostic greatly enhances its applicability and value to the community.  The paper provides a solid analysis of the limitations of existing methods when applied to reasoning models, clearly motivating the need for their approach.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results are compelling, showing substantial improvements over SnapKV (a well-known KV compression technique) across two popular datasets. The ablation studies, especially the sensitivity analysis of the lambda parameter, provide valuable insights.  The memory and throughput comparisons are well-documented.
    * **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly structured. The motivation, methodology, and results are presented logically. The figures effectively illustrate the proposed method and its benefits.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited scope of datasets:** The evaluation focuses on math reasoning tasks. It is a valid choice, but evaluating the method on other reasoning tasks such as commonsense reasoning, or question answering would further strengthen the findings.
    * **Complexity analysis:** While the computation overhead is analyzed, deeper analysis of the effects of various hyper-parameters (particularly similarity threshold τ and number of recent similar tokens β), and a discussion regarding the sensitivity for changes would provide more insight. The selection process needs a thorough performance analysis, to demonstrate the approach would scale and retain the efficiency gains for larger models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of KV cache compression for reasoning models. R-KV tackles a pressing deployment challenge with a clever and effective training-free approach, well supported by experiments. The improvements over existing methods are substantial, and the potential impact on the deployment of large reasoning models is significant.  While broader evaluation would further solidify the findings, the paper demonstrates a clear advance in the state-of-the-art. The paper would benefit with more extensive analysis of computational complexity for selection process.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### AMSbench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating MLLM Capabilities in AMS Circuits
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24138v1)
- **Authors**: Yichen Shi, Ze Zhang, Hongyang Wang, Zhuofu Tao, Zhongyi Li, Bingyu Chen, Yaxin Wang, Zhiping Yu, Ting-Jung Lin, Lei He
- **Abstract**: Analog/Mixed-Signal (AMS) circuits play a critical role in the integrated circuit (IC) industry. However, automating Analog/Mixed-Signal (AMS) circuit design has remained a longstanding challenge due to its difficulty and complexity. Recent advances in Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) offer promising potential for supporting AMS circuit analysis and design. However, current research typically evaluates MLLMs on isolated tasks within the domain, lacking a comprehensive benchmark that systematically assesses model capabilities across diverse AMS-related challenges. To address this gap, we introduce AMSbench, a benchmark suite designed to evaluate MLLM performance across critical tasks including circuit schematic perception, circuit analysis, and circuit design. AMSbench comprises approximately 8000 test questions spanning multiple difficulty levels and assesses eight prominent models, encompassing both open-source and proprietary solutions such as Qwen 2.5-VL and Gemini 2.5 Pro. Our evaluation highlights significant limitations in current MLLMs, particularly in complex multi-modal reasoning and sophisticated circuit design tasks. These results underscore the necessity of advancing MLLMs' understanding and effective application of circuit-specific knowledge, thereby narrowing the existing performance gap relative to human expertise and moving toward fully automated AMS circuit design workflows. Our data is released at https://huggingface.co/datasets/wwhhyy/AMSBench
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AMSbench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating MLLM Capabilities in AMS Circuits":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AMSbench, a new benchmark suite specifically designed to evaluate the capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in the context of Analog/Mixed-Signal (AMS) circuit design. AMSbench covers three core task areas: schematic perception (recognizing and interpreting circuit diagrams), circuit analysis (understanding circuit functionality and performance tradeoffs), and circuit design (synthesizing circuits and creating testbenches). The benchmark consists of approximately 8000 questions spanning various difficulty levels and circuit types, sourced from both academic publications and industry datasheets. The authors evaluate several prominent MLLMs, including closed-source (GPT-4o, Gemini-2.5-Pro) and open-source (Qwen2.5-VL, DeepSeek-R1) models, on AMSbench and highlight significant limitations in current MLLM performance, particularly in complex reasoning and circuit design tasks. They release the benchmark dataset to foster further research and development in this area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation and introduction of a comprehensive benchmark specifically tailored for evaluating MLLMs in AMS circuit design. While there have been previous efforts to apply ML/LLMs to EDA, and even some focusing on analog circuits, the comprehensive, multimodal, and task-oriented nature of AMSbench represents a significant advancement. The dataset is diverse, including schematics, text, tables, and charts, and it covers a wide range of AMS concepts and practical tasks. The decomposition of AMS challenges into the three levels (perception, analysis, and design) provides a structured and more granular evaluation approach. In addition, including a testbench generation part is not seen in existing benchmarks.

*   **Significance:** AMS circuit design is a challenging and historically difficult area to automate, so using MLLMs is an important direction. AMSbench addresses a critical gap by providing a standardized platform for evaluating and comparing MLLM performance in this domain. The paper's results highlight current limitations and underscore the need for further research focused on improving MLLM understanding of circuit-specific knowledge and their ability to perform complex reasoning. AMSbench has the potential to become a valuable tool for guiding future development in this field, accelerating the progress toward more automated AMS design workflows. By exposing the performance gap, it provides motivation for improving model architectures, training data, and prompting strategies. Releasing the dataset is also a positive move, as it promotes transparency, reproducibility, and community-driven research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive and well-structured benchmark covering diverse AMS tasks and difficulty levels.
    *   Multimodal data representation mirrors real-world AMS design challenges.
    *   Systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art MLLMs, both open-source and closed-source.
    *   Clear identification of current MLLM limitations in AMS design.
    *   Public release of the dataset promotes further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The design tasks, while present, are less represented.
    *   The evaluation metrics are adequate but could be improved. The introduction of a netlist edit distance (NED) is helpful, but more sophisticated metrics to assess circuit functionality and design quality could be beneficial. Automated circuit simulation metrics are also missing.
    *   The paper focuses on a limited set of MLLMs. Evaluating a broader range of models, including those specifically fine-tuned for EDA tasks, would provide a more comprehensive picture.
    *   The error analysis section, while helpful, is relatively brief. A more in-depth analysis of the types of errors made by MLLMs, particularly in the perception and design tasks, would offer valuable insights for future model development.
    *   The benchmark relies on manual question generation, which can be time-consuming and potentially introduce biases. Exploring automated question generation techniques could improve the scalability and objectivity of the benchmark.

*   **Potential Impact:** AMSbench can significantly impact the field by:

    *   Providing a common ground for comparing and evaluating different MLLM approaches for AMS design.
    *   Guiding future research efforts toward addressing the identified limitations of current models.
    *   Accelerating the development of more automated AMS design workflows.
    *   Encouraging the development of new MLLM architectures and training strategies specifically tailored for EDA tasks.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** AMSbench is a solid contribution to the field. The benchmark's comprehensiveness, multimodality, and practical relevance are its key strengths. While there are areas for improvement, particularly in the evaluation metrics, diversity of models evaluated, and depth of error analysis, the paper provides a valuable resource for researchers working on applying MLLMs to AMS circuit design. It will likely serve as a starting point and a reference for future work in this area, and the public release of the dataset will further promote progress. The score reflects both the substantial contribution and the identified weaknesses. The benchmark addresses a gap and can have a substantial effect, therefore the high rating.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### S4-Driver: Scalable Self-Supervised Driving Multimodal Large Language Modelwith Spatio-Temporal Visual Representation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24139v1)
- **Authors**: Yichen Xie, Runsheng Xu, Tong He, Jyh-Jing Hwang, Katie Luo, Jingwei Ji, Hubert Lin, Letian Chen, Yiren Lu, Zhaoqi Leng, Dragomir Anguelov, Mingxing Tan
- **Abstract**: The latest advancements in multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) have spurred a strong renewed interest in end-to-end motion planning approaches for autonomous driving. Many end-to-end approaches rely on human annotations to learn intermediate perception and prediction tasks, while purely self-supervised approaches--which directly learn from sensor inputs to generate planning trajectories without human annotations often underperform the state of the art. We observe a key gap in the input representation space: end-to-end approaches built on MLLMs are often pretrained with reasoning tasks in 2D image space rather than the native 3D space in which autonomous vehicles plan. To this end, we propose S4-Driver, a scalable self-supervised motion planning algorithm with spatio-temporal visual representation, based on the popular PaLI multimodal large language model. S4-Driver uses a novel sparse volume strategy to seamlessly transform the strong visual representation of MLLMs from perspective view to 3D space without the need to finetune the vision encoder. This representation aggregates multi-view and multi-frame visual inputs and enables better prediction of planning trajectories in 3D space. To validate our method, we run experiments on both nuScenes and Waymo Open Motion Dataset (with in-house camera data). Results show that S4-Driver performs favorably against existing supervised multi-task approaches while requiring no human annotations. It also demonstrates great scalability when pretrained on large volumes of unannotated driving logs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces S4-Driver, a self-supervised end-to-end motion planning algorithm for autonomous driving using multimodal large language models (MLLMs). S4-Driver addresses the limitations of applying MLLMs (pretrained on 2D image tasks) to 3D motion planning by proposing a novel sparse volume representation to transform perspective view visual features into a 3D space representation *without* finetuning the vision encoder. This representation aggregates multi-view and multi-frame visual inputs, enhancing the model's 3D spatio-temporal reasoning capabilities for better planning. The method is trained using only ego-vehicle trajectory supervision, eliminating the need for expensive human annotations. Experiments on nuScenes and a large-scale in-house Waymo Open Motion Dataset (WOMD) Planning-ADE benchmark show S4-Driver achieves state-of-the-art results and demonstrates scalability with pretraining on unannotated driving logs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies primarily in the integration of a sparse volume representation for end-to-end self-supervised motion planning in MLLMs, without requiring human annotations. Prior works typically relied on multi-task learning or supervised pretraining, limiting scalability and introducing annotation bottlenecks. The sparse volume approach is a clever way to bridge the 2D-3D gap without significantly disrupting pretrained MLLM weights. While the individual components (MLLMs, volume representations) are not entirely new, their combination in this context is a novel contribution. The use of a very large in-house dataset is also notable, though its inaccessibility limits reproducibility.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of end-to-end autonomous driving, particularly in addressing the limitations of relying on labeled data. By achieving state-of-the-art performance in a *self-supervised* manner, S4-Driver demonstrates the potential for leveraging large volumes of unlabeled driving data, which are far more abundant than labeled data. This significantly reduces the dependence on expensive and time-consuming human annotations, promoting scalability and broader applicability. The introduction of behavior-wise metrics for motion planning evaluation is a valuable addition.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Performance:** The results demonstrate the effectiveness of S4-Driver, achieving state-of-the-art or competitive performance on standard benchmarks.
    *   **Scalability:** The self-supervised approach and novel architectural choices enable pretraining on large datasets, demonstrating excellent scalability.
    *   **Effective Volume Representation:** The sparse volume representation effectively bridges the gap between 2D image features and 3D planning space.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes thorough ablation studies to validate the design choices and a new benchmark.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Accessibility:** The use of a proprietary Waymo dataset limits the reproducibility and accessibility of the research. This is a significant drawback as other researchers are not able to replicate the complete training process.
    *   **Complexity:** While the sparse volume representation is innovative, the combination of various components within the architecture might make it more challenging to analyze and understand the specific impact of each element.
    *   **Limited novelty of individual modules**: The framework is based on a combination of components that are not all entirely new.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's results will likely influence the direction of future research in end-to-end autonomous driving, encouraging more exploration of self-supervised learning methods and novel representations for bridging the 2D-3D gap. The behavior-wise metrics could also become a standard evaluation practice in the field.

*   **Overall Assessment:** While the reliance on a proprietary dataset somewhat diminishes its impact, the paper presents a compelling approach to self-supervised motion planning in MLLMs and makes significant progress towards reducing the reliance on human annotations.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach, but it is weighed down by the closed and proprietary nature of one of its primary evaluation datasets. The ideas are sound, and the performance is excellent, but the significance is somewhat hampered by limited reproducibility. The paper pushes the field forward in an important direction, but it is not ground-breaking enough to deserve a 9 or 10, and the proprietary dataset means its practical impact will likely be limited.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### CrossICL: Cross-Task In-Context Learning via Unsupervised Demonstration Transfer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24143v1)
- **Authors**: Jinglong Gao, Xiao Ding, Lingxiao Zou, Bing Qin, Ting Liu
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) enhances the performance of large language models (LLMs) with demonstrations. However, obtaining these demonstrations primarily relies on manual effort. In most real-world scenarios, users are often unwilling or unable to provide such demonstrations. Inspired by the human analogy, we explore a new ICL paradigm CrossICL to study how to utilize existing source task demonstrations in the ICL for target tasks, thereby obtaining reliable guidance without any additional manual effort. To explore this, we first design a two-stage alignment strategy to mitigate the interference caused by gaps across tasks, as the foundation for our experimental exploration. Based on it, we conduct comprehensive exploration of CrossICL, with 875 NLP tasks from the Super-NI benchmark and six types of LLMs, including GPT-4o. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of CrossICL and provide valuable insights on questions like the criteria for selecting cross-task demonstrations, as well as the types of task-gap-induced interference in CrossICL.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "CrossICL: Cross-Task In-Context Learning via Unsupervised Demonstration Transfer" introduces a novel paradigm called CrossICL for in-context learning (ICL) that leverages demonstrations from existing, but different, tasks to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs). This aims to alleviate the dependence on manual demonstration creation, which can be a significant barrier to practical ICL applications. CrossICL employs a two-stage alignment strategy: (1) Minimum Gap Selection to identify source tasks with minimal task differences and (2) Progressive Task Adaptation to transform source demonstrations into a format suitable for the target task. The paper provides a comprehensive experimental evaluation, utilizing 875 tasks from the Super-NI benchmark and six LLMs (including GPT-4o), to demonstrate the effectiveness of CrossICL and explore criteria for selecting cross-task demonstrations and potential sources of interference.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of transferring demonstrations across tasks in ICL is relatively novel.  While related research explores zero-shot ICL and demonstration generation, few works have explicitly examined using pre-existing demonstrations from other tasks in a systematic way. This is a useful and practical step toward generalizing LLMs. The two-stage alignment strategy (Minimum Gap Selection and Progressive Task Adaptation) shows potential for practical improvement.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the limitations of standard ICL. Manual demonstration creation is a bottleneck. CrossICL tackles this by enabling the use of existing datasets, opening up opportunities to leverage abundant resources without human intervention. The thorough experimental analysis, including the study of selection criteria, interference types, and the impacts of the number of demonstrations and temperature, provides valuable insights for future research. Furthermore, the experiments with GPT-4o highlights the practical performance of CrossICL.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of standard ICL and proposes a practical solution.
    *   **Well-defined methodology:** The two-stage alignment strategy is clearly described and easy to understand.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results across a large number of tasks and models, offering strong evidence for the effectiveness of CrossICL.
    *   **In-depth analysis:**  The analysis of demonstration selection criteria and interference types offers valuable insights for future research and practical implementation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs for adaptation:** The Progressive Task Adaptation stage heavily relies on the LLMs' capabilities for transforming and refining queries and generating labels. Although the approach seems to be working, the quality of adaptation is still tied to the reasoning ability of the LLMs.
    *   **Complexity of the framework:** While the concept is elegant, the implementation introduces complexity with multiple prompts and stages, making it harder to implement and reproduce than zero-shot ICL methods.
    *   **Limited exploration of the source tasks:** The study could benefit from further exploration of the source tasks used. Are there certain properties of the source task (beyond overall similarity) that make them particularly beneficial for CrossICL?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has a good potential to influence the field of ICL by opening a new research direction for cross-task demonstration transfer. The findings on demonstration selection and interference can inform the design of future ICL methods.

*Justification for the Score:*

While the paper introduces a novel and useful approach to ICL, the reliance on the LLMs' reasoning capabilities for the adaptation strategy and the complexity of implementation, combined with the unexplored qualities of source tasks, limit the impact. The analysis on demonstration selection and interference is very useful.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Autoregressive regularized score-based diffusion models for multi-scenarios fluid flow prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24145v1)
- **Authors**: Wilfried Genuist, Éric Savin, Filippo Gatti, Didier Clouteau
- **Abstract**: Building on recent advances in scientific machine learning and generative modeling for computational fluid dynamics, we propose a conditional score-based diffusion model designed for multi-scenarios fluid flow prediction. Our model integrates an energy constraint rooted in the statistical properties of turbulent flows, improving prediction quality with minimal training, while enabling efficient sampling at low cost. The method features a simple and general architecture that requires no problem-specific design, supports plug-and-play enhancements, and enables fast and flexible solution generation. It also demonstrates an efficient conditioning mechanism that simplifies training across different scenarios without demanding a redesign of existing models. We further explore various stochastic differential equation formulations to demonstrate how thoughtful design choices enhance performance. We validate the proposed methodology through extensive experiments on complex fluid dynamics datasets encompassing a variety of flow regimes and configurations. Results demonstrate that our model consistently achieves stable, robust, and physically faithful predictions, even under challenging turbulent conditions. With properly tuned parameters, it achieves accurate results across multiple scenarios while preserving key physical and statistical properties. We present a comprehensive analysis of stochastic differential equation impact and discuss our approach across diverse fluid mechanics tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an autoregressive, regularized score-based diffusion model for predicting multi-scenario fluid flow.  The model incorporates an energy constraint derived from the statistical properties of turbulent flows to enhance prediction quality while maintaining efficient sampling. A key contribution is a simple, general architecture that doesn't require problem-specific modifications and supports plug-and-play improvements. The paper validates the approach with extensive experiments on fluid dynamics datasets spanning various flow regimes, showing stable, robust, and physically faithful predictions even under challenging turbulent conditions. The study also presents a comprehensive analysis of the impact of different stochastic differential equation (SDE) formulations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of autoregressive modeling, score-based diffusion, and energy-based regularization for multi-scenario fluid flow prediction shows decent novelty. Previous works explored components of this approach, but the comprehensive integration is a significant step forward. The effort to create a generic model architecture instead of custom builds for specific problems is valuable. The SDE formulation analysis is a welcome addition as current methods typically favor standard SDE approaches. The innovation is in the specific combination and architectural choices.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: computationally efficient and accurate fluid flow prediction, especially in turbulent scenarios. The method has the potential to significantly impact areas like aerodynamic design, renewable energy optimization, and environmental modeling. The ability to handle multiple scenarios without retraining or redesigning the model is a valuable asset.  The demonstration of improved performance with a physically motivated regularization term is significant. The paper directly addresses known shortcomings of machine learning models in fluid flow: stability and interpretability.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** Extensive evaluation across diverse datasets and flow regimes provides solid evidence of the method's robustness.
    *   **Physically Informed Regularization:**  Integrating energy constraints improves prediction quality, making it a more principled approach.
    *   **Simple and General Architecture:** The focus on a simple, general architecture over complex, specialized ones is a significant advantage for practical applications.
    *   **Efficient Conditioning Mechanism:**  The proposed conditioning simplifies training across different scenarios.
    *   **SDE Analysis:** Provides a useful analysis and comparison of different SDE formulations.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the architecture is simpler, diffusion models themselves can be computationally expensive, especially for 3D and time-dependent simulations. The paper doesn't explicitly address the scaling of the method with increasing dimensionality and time horizons.  More detailed timing comparisons and resource consumption analysis would be helpful.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The random search optimization for hyperparameters suggests a degree of sensitivity to these settings, and a more robust method for hyperparameter optimization could strengthen the approach. Some hyperparameters need tuning case-by-case, hindering the "plug-and-play" feature.
    *   **Limited 3D Cases:** While the paper includes a 3D MHD simulation, more validation on diverse 3D datasets would increase the confidence in the model's generalizability to complex 3D flows. Also, the 3D results are a bit weaker in overall performance.
    *   **Post-Processing Dependence:** While the use of the Perona-Malik filter is a nice touch, the reliance on this post-processing step for cleaner results suggests that there is still room to improve the diffusion model's ability to directly generate coherent structures.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by promoting the use of score-based diffusion models with physically-motivated regularization and simpler architectures for computational fluid dynamics. The demonstration of improved performance by optimizing SDE formulation can lead to further research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-executed and valuable contribution to the field of scientific machine learning and computational fluid dynamics. The novelty lies in the comprehensive integration of autoregressive modeling, score-based diffusion, energy regularization, and SDE formulation analysis, showing significant promise in solving the challenging problem of turbulent flow prediction. The simple, general architecture makes the method practical for widespread adoption. However, the paper is not without limitations, particularly concerning the high computational cost and some degree of hyperparameter sensitivity and the dependence on post-processing filters. The impact score reflects the potential of this integrated approach and the demonstration of tangible results across different fluid mechanics datasets, while accounting for the limitations above. Further improvements in scalability and hyperparameter robustness would substantially increase the impact of this approach.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Don't Just Follow MLLM Plans: Robust and Efficient Planning for Open-world Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24157v1)
- **Authors**: Seungjoon Lee, Suhwan Kim, Minhyeon Oh, Youngsik Yoon, Jungseul Ok
- **Abstract**: Developing autonomous agents capable of mastering complex, multi-step tasks in unpredictable, interactive environments presents a significant challenge. While Large Language Models (LLMs) offer promise for planning, existing approaches often rely on problematic internal knowledge or make unrealistic environmental assumptions. Although recent work explores learning planning knowledge, they still retain limitations due to partial reliance on external knowledge or impractical setups. Indeed, prior research has largely overlooked developing agents capable of acquiring planning knowledge from scratch, directly in realistic settings. While realizing this capability is necessary, it presents significant challenges, primarily achieving robustness given the substantial risk of incorporating LLMs' inaccurate knowledge. Moreover, efficiency is crucial for practicality as learning can demand prohibitive exploration. In response, we introduce Robust and Efficient Planning for Open-world Agents (REPOA), a novel framework designed to tackle these issues. REPOA features three key components: adaptive dependency learning and fine-grained failure-aware operation memory to enhance robustness to knowledge inaccuracies, and difficulty-based exploration to improve learning efficiency. Our evaluation in two established open-world testbeds demonstrates REPOA's robust and efficient planning, showcasing its capability to successfully obtain challenging late-game items that were beyond the reach of prior approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces REPOA (Robust and Efficient Planning for Open-world Agents), a framework designed to enable autonomous agents to learn planning strategies in complex open-world environments like Minecraft.
