### STORK: Improving the Fidelity of Mid-NFE Sampling for Diffusion and Flow Matching Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24210v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng Tan, Weizhen Wang, Andrea L. Bertozzi, Ernest K. Ryu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models (DMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in high-fidelity image and video generation. Because high-quality generations with DMs typically require a large number of function evaluations (NFEs), resulting in slow sampling, there has been extensive research successfully reducing the NFE to a small range (<10) while maintaining acceptable image quality. However, many practical applications, such as those involving Stable Diffusion 3.5, FLUX, and SANA, commonly operate in the mid-NFE regime (20-50 NFE) to achieve superior results, and, despite the practical relevance, research on the effective sampling within this mid-NFE regime remains underexplored. In this work, we propose a novel, training-free, and structure-independent DM ODE solver called the Stabilized Taylor Orthogonal Runge--Kutta (STORK) method, based on a class of stiff ODE solvers with a Taylor expansion adaptation. Unlike prior work such as DPM-Solver, which is dependent on the semi-linear structure of the DM ODE, STORK is applicable to any DM sampling, including noise-based and flow matching-based models. Within the 20-50 NFE range, STORK achieves improved generation quality, as measured by FID scores, across unconditional pixel-level generation and conditional latent-space generation tasks using models like Stable Diffusion 3.5 and SANA. Code is available at https://github.com/ZT220501/STORK.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "STORK: Improving the Fidelity of Mid-NFE Sampling for Diffusion and Flow Matching Models" introduces a new training-free method, Stabilized Taylor Orthogonal Runge-Kutta (STORK), for accelerating sampling in diffusion models (DMs) and flow matching models. It focuses on the practically relevant "mid-NFE" regime (20-50 function evaluations), where existing fast sampling methods optimized for very low NFE (<10) are suboptimal. STORK leverages stabilized Runge-Kutta methods with Taylor expansion adaptations to achieve higher fidelity generation, as measured by FID scores, compared to state-of-the-art samplers like Flow-DPM-Solver within this mid-NFE range.  The method is structure-independent, allowing it to be used with both noise-based diffusion models and flow matching models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in addressing a specific gap in the diffusion model literature. While many works focus on extreme low-NFE sampling, the authors correctly identify that current, practically used DMs (e.g., Stable Diffusion 3.5, SANA, FLUX) often operate with a moderate number of NFEs. Applying stabilized Runge-Kutta methods (SRK) to DM sampling isn't completely new (prior works have explored Runge-Kutta methods), but the integration with Taylor expansion and the specific focus on the mid-NFE regime, and adaptation of SRK for general DM sampling, is a distinct contribution. This is particularly notable as the authors have adapted it to work with both noise-based diffusion models and flow-matching models.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from improving the efficiency and/or quality of widely used DM sampling techniques. The experimental results, showing improved FID scores over Flow-DPM-Solver in various unconditional and conditional generation tasks, suggests a concrete improvement. The method's structure-independence is also significant as it allows it to be deployed across a wider variety of generative modeling settings without significant modifications. This broader applicability will likely allow for increased future adoption of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a practical problem:** The paper focuses on a relevant problem in real-world DM applications.
    *   **Training-free:** Avoiding additional training is a major benefit, enabling easy integration into existing pipelines.
    *   **Structure-independent:** General applicability to both noise-based and flow-matching DMs.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** Consistent improvements in FID scores across multiple datasets and models.
    *   Well-written with clear explanations.
    *   Method is explained in sufficient detail and released as code.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental improvement:** While the gains are consistent, the improvement may be considered incremental in the overall context of the rapidly evolving field of diffusion models.
    *   **Limited comparison:** It would be beneficial to compare against more recent, very low-NFE techniques and evaluate how well the performance scales.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** The method introduces hyperparameters (number of sub-steps s, taylor expansion order) that require task specific tuning, potentially limiting ease of use.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the DM sampling literature by: (1) shifting focus to the mid-NFE regime, (2) providing a practical and effective training-free sampling method, and (3) highlighting the benefits of stabilized Runge-Kutta methods. Given the increased adoption of flow-matching models, the paper's general applicability is highly impactful.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a sound and practical solution to a relevant problem in diffusion model sampling.  It offers consistent empirical improvements over existing methods, along with the key benefits of being training-free and structure-independent.  While the improvements might be considered incremental and may require some hyperparameter tuning, the paper addresses a gap in the literature and offers broad applicability. This is a solid contribution to the field.

### Benchmarking Foundation Models for Zero-Shot Biometric Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24214v1)
- **Authors**: Redwan Sony, Parisa Farmanifard, Hamzeh Alzwairy, Nitish Shukla, Arun Ross
- **Abstract**: The advent of foundation models, particularly Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs), has redefined the frontiers of artificial intelligence, enabling remarkable generalization across diverse tasks with minimal or no supervision. Yet, their potential in biometric recognition and analysis remains relatively underexplored. In this work, we introduce a comprehensive benchmark that evaluates the zero-shot and few-shot performance of state-of-the-art publicly available VLMs and MLLMs across six biometric tasks spanning the face and iris modalities: face verification, soft biometric attribute prediction (gender and race), iris recognition, presentation attack detection (PAD), and face manipulation detection (morphs and deepfakes). A total of 41 VLMs were used in this evaluation. Experiments show that embeddings from these foundation models can be used for diverse biometric tasks with varying degrees of success. For example, in the case of face verification, a True Match Rate (TMR) of 96.77 percent was obtained at a False Match Rate (FMR) of 1 percent on the Labeled Face in the Wild (LFW) dataset, without any fine-tuning. In the case of iris recognition, the TMR at 1 percent FMR on the IITD-R-Full dataset was 97.55 percent without any fine-tuning. Further, we show that applying a simple classifier head to these embeddings can help perform DeepFake detection for faces, Presentation Attack Detection (PAD) for irides, and extract soft biometric attributes like gender and ethnicity from faces with reasonably high accuracy. This work reiterates the potential of pretrained models in achieving the long-term vision of Artificial General Intelligence.
- **Summary**: This paper presents a comprehensive benchmark evaluation of zero-shot and few-shot performance of 41 Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across six biometric tasks spanning face and iris modalities: face verification, soft biometric attribute prediction (gender and race), iris recognition, presentation attack detection (PAD), and face manipulation detection (morphs and deepfakes). The results show that embeddings from these models can be used for diverse biometric tasks with varying degrees of success, highlighting the potential of pre-trained models for achieving Artificial General Intelligence.

**Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's primary contribution lies in its **breadth and systematic evaluation** of a large suite of publicly available VLMs on a diverse range of biometric tasks.  While prior works have explored applying foundation models to specific biometric problems (e.g., iris segmentation, face recognition), this work provides a significantly broader benchmark. The effort to systematically evaluate many models against several important biometric challenges and datasets using zero-shot and few-shot protocols contributes valuable empirical data to the community.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive benchmark:** Evaluating 41 models across 6 tasks provides a much-needed overview of VLM capabilities in biometrics.
    * **Zero-shot focus:** Emphasis on zero-shot performance reveals the inherent transferability of these models without task-specific training.
    * **Diverse tasks:**  Including face verification, attribute prediction, PAD, and manipulation detection reveals the breadth of VLM applicability to biometrics.
    * **Real-world datasets:** Using established biometric datasets lends credibility to the benchmark results.
    * Provides a useful comparative view of the strengths and weaknesses of different foundation models for biometric tasks.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited analysis of failure modes:** The paper primarily reports aggregate performance metrics (TMR, accuracy). A deeper dive into specific failure cases and biases within the VLMs would have been highly valuable. For example, how do demographic characteristics affect the performance of soft biometric attribute predictions? What types of morphs are most difficult to detect?
    * **Relatively simple evaluation protocols:**  While the scale of evaluation is large, the protocols employed are relatively standard (e.g., cosine similarity for face verification, simple classifier heads for other tasks).  More sophisticated adaptation or fine-tuning techniques beyond lightweight classifier heads (e.g., prompt tuning, LoRA) could potentially reveal higher performance ceilings for these models, adding to the novelty of the approach.
    * **Lack of theoretical underpinning:** The paper is largely empirical. There's limited discussion about *why* certain models perform better than others for specific biometric tasks or the underlying factors that contribute to successful transfer learning in this domain.  The explanations provided are rather cursory.
    * **Incremental novelty in methodology:** The techniques are not particularly new, using pretrained models and existing benchmarks. The major novelty is applying existing methods across multiple new tasks and models.
    * **Limited focus on compute:** The paper doesn't clearly explain how much compute resources where used. This is an important consideration to judge the replicability and usefullness of the benchmark.

* **Significance:**
    * The benchmark results serve as a valuable resource for researchers entering the field of VLMs in biometrics.
    * It provides a baseline for future work exploring more sophisticated adaptation techniques or architectures.
    * It highlights promising directions for building scalable and adaptable biometric systems using pre-trained models.
    * The work could encourage more research into understanding and mitigating biases in VLMs for biometric applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a significant and useful contribution to the biometric and machine learning communities by providing a broad, systematic evaluation of publicly available VLMs on a diverse set of biometric tasks. While the methodologies are relatively straightforward, the **scale and scope of the evaluation** are its main strength. This systematic approach reveals promising areas for future research. However, there are several limitations. The limited analysis of failure modes, biases, as well as the relatively simple evaluation protocols prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. The major benefit is to the biometric community to understand state-of-the-art techniques in the area.

### Semi-structured LLM Reasoners Can Be Rigorously Audited
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24217v1)
- **Authors**: Jixuan Leng, Cassandra A. Cohen, Zhixian Zhang, Chenyan Xiong, William W. Cohen
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) become increasingly capable at reasoning, the problem of "faithfulness" persists: LLM "reasoning traces" can contain errors and omissions that are difficult to detect, and may obscure biases in model outputs. To address these limitations, we introduce Semi-Structured Reasoning Models (SSRMs), which internalize a semi-structured Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning format within the model. Our SSRMs generate reasoning traces in a Pythonic syntax. While SSRM traces are not executable, they adopt a restricted, task-specific vocabulary to name distinct reasoning steps, and to mark each step's inputs and outputs. Through extensive evaluation on ten benchmarks, SSRMs demonstrate strong performance and generality: they outperform comparably sized baselines by nearly ten percentage points on in-domain tasks while remaining competitive with specialized models on out-of-domain medical benchmarks. Furthermore, we show that semi-structured reasoning is more amenable to analysis: in particular, they can be automatically audited to identify reasoning flaws. We explore both hand-crafted structured audits, which detect task-specific problematic reasoning patterns, and learned typicality audits, which apply probabilistic models over reasoning patterns, and show that both audits can be used to effectively flag probable reasoning errors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Semi-Structured Reasoning Models (SSRMs), a novel approach to training Large Language Models (LLMs) for reasoning. SSRMs generate reasoning traces in a Pythonic syntax, with task-specific vocabulary and marked inputs/outputs for each step. This semi-structured format enables automated auditing for reasoning flaws. The authors demonstrate SSRMs' strong performance and generality across diverse benchmarks. They explore hand-crafted structured audits and learned typicality audits to identify reasoning errors, effectively flagging outcomes. The paper also demonstrates the feasibility of applying these audits to prompted models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central novelty lies in the combination of semi-structured reasoning output with automated audits (both rule-based and typicality). Existing work has explored structured outputs (e.g., program synthesis) and reasoning analysis, but the integration of a semi-structured trace specifically designed for efficient auditing is a significant step forward. Program Trace Prompting (PTP) is used for data generation, so the innovation is not in the overall training procedure but in the architecture which supports both PTP and automated audits.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses the crucial "faithfulness" problem in LLM reasoning. The ability to automatically detect reasoning flaws opens avenues for building more reliable and trustworthy LLM systems. This is particularly important in high-stakes domains like medicine. The study of reasoning pattern typicality also provides a new lens to understand and potentially improve the reasoning processes of LLMs. The analysis presented allows for better understanding of what LLMs are doing with their reasoning traces, but this novelty would be increased with an analysis of *why* the LLMs have these patterns.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** SSRMs consistently outperform baselines on a range of benchmarks.
    *   **Generalizability:** The approach is not tied to a specific task, as shown by performance across diverse domains. The extension of semi-structured audits to prompted models and its demonstration to be effective across different architectures and datasets increases the generalizability.
    *   **Clear Auditing Methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the structured and typicality audit methods, making them readily implementable.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The paper provides valuable insights into reasoning patterns and their correlation with accuracy.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scale:** The experiments are limited to a 7B model, and a single architecture, and the computational cost of larger-scale auditing (especially for typicality audits) could be significant. The scaling effect is only minimally discussed and should be explored.
    *   **Manual Audit Development:** The structured audits are hand-crafted, potentially limiting their scalability and adaptability to new tasks. Automating this process is left for future work, which decreases the significance of this approach as of now.
    *   **Typicality Audit Interpretation:** While the typicality analysis is interesting, the interpretation of typicality as indicative of correctness requires careful consideration. In some cases, highly typical patterns might reflect biases or incorrect heuristics that are common in the training data.
    *   **Limited Test Time Scaling:** Only preliminary experiments integrating test-time-scaling with audits were conducted and further utility during inference time would increase its usefulness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of more auditable and reliable LLM systems. The concept of semi-structured reasoning output and automated audits could become a standard practice in LLM development, particularly for safety-critical applications. The investigation into reasoning patterns may also inspire new techniques for improving LLM reasoning capabilities.

*Justification:*

The paper presents a novel approach with strong empirical results, but the current limitations relating to manual audit creation, scale, and typicality interpretation prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The significance of this paper would be increased with a more robust typicality audit and increased scalability. The findings presented could spur further research into this area, but some of the proposed next steps are too premature to increase the score.

### Unleashing High-Quality Image Generation in Diffusion Sampling Using Second-Order Levenberg-Marquardt-Langevin
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24222v1)
- **Authors**: Fangyikang Wang, Hubery Yin, Lei Qian, Yinan Li, Shaobin Zhuang, Huminhao Zhu, Yilin Zhang, Yanlong Tang, Chao Zhang, Hanbin Zhao, Hui Qian, Chen Li
- **Abstract**: The diffusion models (DMs) have demonstrated the remarkable capability of generating images via learning the noised score function of data distribution. Current DM sampling techniques typically rely on first-order Langevin dynamics at each noise level, with efforts concentrated on refining inter-level denoising strategies. While leveraging additional second-order Hessian geometry to enhance the sampling quality of Langevin is a common practice in Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC), the naive attempts to utilize Hessian geometry in high-dimensional DMs lead to quadratic-complexity computational costs, rendering them non-scalable. In this work, we introduce a novel Levenberg-Marquardt-Langevin (LML) method that approximates the diffusion Hessian geometry in a training-free manner, drawing inspiration from the celebrated Levenberg-Marquardt optimization algorithm. Our approach introduces two key innovations: (1) A low-rank approximation of the diffusion Hessian, leveraging the DMs' inherent structure and circumventing explicit quadratic-complexity computations; (2) A damping mechanism to stabilize the approximated Hessian. This LML approximated Hessian geometry enables the diffusion sampling to execute more accurate steps and improve the image generation quality. We further conduct a theoretical analysis to substantiate the approximation error bound of low-rank approximation and the convergence property of the damping mechanism. Extensive experiments across multiple pretrained DMs validate that the LML method significantly improves image generation quality, with negligible computational overhead.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel sampling method called Levenberg-Marquardt-Langevin (LML) for improving image generation quality in diffusion models (DMs). Current DM sampling techniques primarily rely on first-order Langevin dynamics, focusing on refining inter-level denoising strategies. The LML method leverages a second-order Hessian geometry to guide the Langevin update to take more accurate steps within each noise level. The core innovation lies in approximating the diffusion Hessian in a computationally efficient, training-free manner, drawing inspiration from the Levenberg-Marquardt optimization algorithm. LML uses a low-rank approximation of the Hessian and a damping mechanism to stabilize it. The authors provide theoretical analyses for the low-rank approximation and the damping mechanism's convergence properties. Experimental results across multiple pre-trained DMs demonstrate that LML improves image generation quality with negligible computational overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty is the adaptation of the Levenberg-Marquardt optimization technique to approximate the Hessian geometry within the context of diffusion model sampling. While the idea of using second-order information for Langevin dynamics isn't entirely new, the proposed method of efficiently approximating the Hessian *without* training auxiliary networks or maintaining large memory-intensive states is a significant contribution.  The specific way the low-rank approximation is derived and the use of a damping mechanism for stabilization appear to be unique.  This is particularly important given the high dimensionality of modern DMs.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in diffusion model sampling: enhancing the sampling quality while maintaining computational efficiency. By enabling more accurate Langevin steps without incurring prohibitive costs, the LML method has the potential to improve the accessibility and practical application of DMs. The experimental results validate this claim by showing improvements in image quality across several pre-trained models. The theoretical analysis also bolsters the significance, providing a solid foundation for the method's performance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Computationally efficient Hessian approximation that avoids naive quadratic complexity.
    *   Training-free and applicable to pre-trained, commercial-level DMs.
    *   Theoretical analysis providing error bounds and convergence guarantees.
    *   Empirical validation across multiple datasets and DMs.
    *   Clear and well-structured presentation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed comparison to other Hessian approximation techniques, particularly those used in related fields (e.g., reinforcement learning).  The authors acknowledge this gap.
    *   While the experimental results are impressive, the improvements in image quality are still somewhat subjective.  The authors utilize metrics like FID and CLIP scores which are standard, but an ablation study analyzing the specific impact of low-rank approximation vs. damping mechanism could be more in-depth.
    *   The method relies on tuning two hyperparameters, λ and κ. While the authors provide guidelines, more detailed analysis and automated tuning strategies might be beneficial.
    *   The scope focuses on improving the sampling quality. Further research could focus on adapting the proposed approach to reduce the amount of sampling steps.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The LML method could potentially influence the design of more efficient and higher-quality diffusion model samplers. The technique's training-free nature makes it readily adoptable for a wide range of pre-trained models. The successful adaptation of Levenberg-Marquardt optimization to diffusion models could inspire further research into using other optimization techniques for generative modeling.

*Rigorous Rationale for the Score*

The paper presents a novel adaptation of a well-known optimization technique (Levenberg-Marquardt) to a relevant problem in diffusion model sampling. The method is computationally efficient, training-free, and theoretically sound. The gains in image quality, while not revolutionary, are consistent across different models, and the approach directly addresses a key practical challenge.

The critical consideration comes from assessing the "leap" in innovation. While the concept of using second-order information for samplers exists, previous attempts have suffered quadratic-complexity issues.  The paper's low-rank approximation circumvents this and results in a significant computational saving. Overall, the paper balances theoretical rigor, practical applicability, and significant technical contribution.

### Automated Structured Radiology Report Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24223v1)
- **Authors**: Jean-Benoit Delbrouck, Justin Xu, Johannes Moll, Alois Thomas, Zhihong Chen, Sophie Ostmeier, Asfandyar Azhar, Kelvin Zhenghao Li, Andrew Johnston, Christian Bluethgen, Eduardo Reis, Mohamed Muneer, Maya Varma, Curtis Langlotz
- **Abstract**: Automated radiology report generation from chest X-ray (CXR) images has the potential to improve clinical efficiency and reduce radiologists' workload. However, most datasets, including the publicly available MIMIC-CXR and CheXpert Plus, consist entirely of free-form reports, which are inherently variable and unstructured. This variability poses challenges for both generation and evaluation: existing models struggle to produce consistent, clinically meaningful reports, and standard evaluation metrics fail to capture the nuances of radiological interpretation. To address this, we introduce Structured Radiology Report Generation (SRRG), a new task that reformulates free-text radiology reports into a standardized format, ensuring clarity, consistency, and structured clinical reporting. We create a novel dataset by restructuring reports using large language models (LLMs) following strict structured reporting desiderata. Additionally, we introduce SRR-BERT, a fine-grained disease classification model trained on 55 labels, enabling more precise and clinically informed evaluation of structured reports. To assess report quality, we propose F1-SRR-BERT, a metric that leverages SRR-BERT's hierarchical disease taxonomy to bridge the gap between free-text variability and structured clinical reporting. We validate our dataset through a reader study conducted by five board-certified radiologists and extensive benchmarking experiments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Automated Structured Radiology Report Generation":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a new task called Structured Radiology Report Generation (SRRG), which aims to convert free-text chest X-ray (CXR) reports into a standardized format.  The authors argue that existing datasets (MIMIC-CXR, CheXpert Plus) contain unstructured reports, leading to challenges in both generating consistent reports and evaluating them effectively. To address this, they create a new dataset by restructuring existing reports using large language models (LLMs) and defined a set of strict structural formatting principles.  They also introduce SRR-BERT, a fine-grained disease classification model trained on 55 labels, to enable more precise evaluation. Finally, they propose F1-SRR-BERT, a new metric that leverages SRR-BERT's hierarchical disease taxonomy to evaluate report quality.  The dataset is validated through a reader study by board-certified radiologists, and the task is benchmarked with various models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel and clinically relevant task: standardizing radiology reports. While the use of LLMs for data transformation is not inherently novel *per se*, the specific application to radiology reports and the construction of a structured dataset are noteworthy. The introduction of SRR-BERT with its fine-grained disease classification and the F1-SRR-BERT metric are additional contributions that address the limitations of existing methods.
*   **Significance:** The potential impact of SRRG is significant. Standardized reports can improve clinical efficiency by reducing radiologist workload and improving communication. A structured format can also facilitate the development of clinical decision support systems. The creation of a large structured dataset can promote further research in this area.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defines the problem and provides a convincing rationale for the SRRG task.
    *   Presents a well-defined set of desiderata for structured radiology reporting.
    *   Creates a valuable dataset, which is made publicly available to stimulate further research.
    *   Introduces SRR-BERT, a classifier with fine-grained labels specifically trained for this task.
    *   Provides a new evaluation metric, F1-SRR-BERT, tailored for structured reports.
    *   Validates the dataset using a reader study and benchmarks the task with existing models.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset generation relies heavily on LLMs (specifically GPT-4). While the authors claim to enforce strict desiderata, it remains a synthetic dataset and is inevitably influenced by the biases and limitations of the LLM.  A more thorough analysis of potential biases introduced during the LLM-driven reformulation process would be beneficial.
    *   While the reader study helps validate the dataset, a more extensive human review would strengthen the findings. The limited number of reviewed samples might not fully capture the complexity of the reports.
    *   The performance gains demonstrated in the benchmarking experiments are moderate.  Further improvements in model architecture and training strategies may be needed to achieve clinically relevant performance.
    *   The comparison of SRR-BERT to CheXbert has limitations. The mapping process inevitably introduces some degree of overlap and ambiguity, thereby making the comparative results less precise.
    *   The paper does not address the potential resistance of radiologists to adopt fully structured reporting in real-world clinical settings, even though it can be more efficient.
*   **Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research in radiology report generation. The introduction of SRRG and the associated dataset, model, and metric provide a solid foundation for building more effective and consistent systems. It will likely prompt researchers to investigate methods for generating more accurate and clinically meaningful structured reports.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of medical natural language processing. While some of the methods are not entirely novel, the combination of a clearly defined new task, the construction of a novel structured dataset, and the tailored evaluation metrics make it an important advance. The reliance on LLMs for dataset creation is a potential limitation, but this is mitigated by the reader study and the publicly available dataset. Considering the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, a score of 7 is assigned. It marks a noteworthy contribution with clear potential for further development and significant impact on the field of automated radiology reporting.

### Reasoning Can Hurt the Inductive Abilities of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24225v1)
- **Authors**: Haibo Jin, Peiyan Zhang, Man Luo, Haohan Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable progress across domains, yet their ability to perform inductive reasoning - inferring latent rules from sparse examples - remains limited. It is often assumed that chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting, as used in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), enhances such reasoning. We investigate this assumption with creating four controlled, diagnostic game-based tasks - chess, Texas Hold'em, dice games, and blackjack - with hidden human-defined rules. We find that CoT reasoning can degrade inductive performance, with LRMs often underperforming their non-reasoning counterparts. To explain this, we present a theoretical framework that reveals how reasoning steps can amplify error through three failure modes: incorrect sub-task decomposition, incorrect sub-task solving, and incorrect final answer summarization. Based on our theoretical and empirical analysis, we introduce structured interventions that adapt CoT generation according to our identified failure types. These interventions improve inductive accuracy without retraining. Our findings suggest that effective (CoT) reasoning depends not only on taking more steps but also on ensuring those steps are well-structured.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper challenges the assumption that chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning consistently enhances inductive abilities in Large Language Models (LLMs). It introduces a set of controlled, diagnostic game-based tasks (chess, Texas Hold'em, dice games, blackjack) with hidden human-defined rules.  The authors find that CoT reasoning can *degrade* inductive performance, with Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) often underperforming their non-reasoning counterparts.  They propose a theoretical framework explaining this degradation via three failure modes: incorrect sub-task decomposition, incorrect sub-task solving, and incorrect final answer summarization.  Based on this analysis, they introduce structured interventions that adapt CoT generation to mitigate these failures, demonstrating improved inductive accuracy without retraining.  The paper concludes that effective CoT reasoning requires not just more steps, but also well-structured steps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its counterintuitive finding that CoT reasoning can *hinder* inductive abilities. While some existing research has hinted at limitations of CoT with increasing depth, this paper goes further by demonstrating a *decrease* in performance compared to non-CoT approaches.  The controlled game environments, specifically designed with hidden rule components, also represent a valuable contribution, enabling a more focused and diagnostic evaluation of inductive reasoning. The theoretical framework outlining the failure modes of reasoning is well-defined and provides useful insights for future work.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for how we understand and apply CoT reasoning. It moves beyond the common assumption that more reasoning steps always equal better performance. The identification of the failure modes of CoT reasoning offers a more nuanced perspective on its effectiveness and can inform the development of better CoT strategies. The proposed interventions, based on error-guided adaptation of CoT, provide a practical approach to improve inductive accuracy and avoid the negative effects of unstructured reasoning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Evidence:** The use of controlled game environments provides a robust platform for evaluating inductive reasoning. The detailed error analysis and clear articulation of the failure modes are persuasive.

    *   **Theoretical Framework:** The formal model helps explain the empirical findings and provides a basis for designing interventions.

    *   **Practical Interventions:** The error-guided intervention method demonstrates how to mitigate the negative effects of CoT reasoning and improve inductive accuracy.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Game-Specific Focus:** The findings are primarily based on gameplay scenarios. While these scenarios are well-designed, it's important to consider how well the results generalize to other types of inductive reasoning tasks or real-world problems.

    *   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Evaluation:**  Using GPT-4 to judge the semantic alignment of rules, while practical, introduces a potential for bias or subjectivity. The paper mitigates this by using multiple queries, but it remains a factor.

    *   **Model-Specific Tuning:** The interventions are tuned for the specific models and tasks. Future work should explore the robustness and transferability of these interventions across different models and tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper can influence the direction of research on reasoning in LLMs, shifting the focus from simply increasing reasoning depth to improving the *structure* and *reliability* of reasoning steps. It also provides a roadmap for future work on diagnosing and mitigating the failure modes of CoT.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable, counterintuitive finding with solid theoretical and empirical support. The analysis of CoT failure modes is insightful, and the proposed interventions offer a practical approach to improve inductive accuracy. While the study is primarily focused on game-based tasks, the findings have broader implications for the field of LLM research and can guide the development of more effective reasoning strategies. Therefore, the work is considered high impact and novel.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### E^2GraphRAG: Streamlining Graph-based RAG for High Efficiency and Effectiveness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24226v1)
- **Authors**: Yibo Zhao, Jiapeng Zhu, Ye Guo, Kangkang He, Xiang Li
- **Abstract**: Graph-based RAG methods like GraphRAG have shown promising global understanding of the knowledge base by constructing hierarchical entity graphs. However, they often suffer from inefficiency and rely on manually pre-defined query modes, limiting practical use. In this paper, we propose E^2GraphRAG, a streamlined graph-based RAG framework that improves both Efficiency and Effectiveness. During the indexing stage, E^2GraphRAG constructs a summary tree with large language models and an entity graph with SpaCy based on document chunks. We then construct bidirectional indexes between entities and chunks to capture their many-to-many relationships, enabling fast lookup during both local and global retrieval. For the retrieval stage, we design an adaptive retrieval strategy that leverages the graph structure to retrieve and select between local and global modes. Experiments show that E^2GraphRAG achieves up to 10 times faster indexing than GraphRAG and 100 times speedup over LightRAG in retrieval while maintaining competitive QA performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the E2GraphRAG paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces E2GraphRAG, a streamlined graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework designed for improved efficiency and effectiveness. It addresses the limitations of existing graph-based RAG methods, which often suffer from inefficiency and inflexible query modes. The key components of E2GraphRAG include: (1) A summary tree constructed using large language models (LLMs) for multi-granularity summarization of document chunks, (2) A concise entity graph created using SpaCy for extracting and relating entities within chunks, and (3) Bidirectional indexes between entities and chunks for fast lookup during retrieval.  The retrieval stage uses an adaptive strategy that dynamically selects between local and global query modes based on entity graph connectivity.  Experiments demonstrate that E2GraphRAG achieves significant speedups in both indexing and retrieval compared to existing methods like GraphRAG and LightRAG while maintaining competitive question-answering performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Improved Efficiency:** The paper's primary strength lies in its significant improvements in efficiency. E2GraphRAG addresses a key bottleneck in graph-based RAG (the high indexing cost) by substituting LLM-based entity extraction with SpaCy and simplifying the graph construction process. The adaptive retrieval strategy is also efficient as it avoid unnecessary LLM calls in choosing retrieval mode. This efficiency makes graph-based RAG more practical for real-world applications.
    * **Adaptive Retrieval Strategy:** The adaptive retrieval strategy is a good idea because it leverages the underlying graph structure to decide how best to retrieve knowledge. This avoids the need for manual setting of query modes, which reduces the amount of work needed from users of the system.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a well-designed set of experiments, comparing E2GraphRAG to strong baselines on multiple datasets. The ablation study provides insights into the contribution of each component.
    * **Practicality:** The choice of using smaller, open-source LLMs makes the model more accessible.
* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Novelty:** While the combination of a summary tree and an entity graph isn't entirely novel, the use of SpaCy for efficient entity graph creation and the adaptive retrieval strategy are the key contributions. The general concept of using graph structure for retrieval is explored quite heavily in literature. It is an incremental improvement in an existing line of work, but not a radical shift in perspective.
    * **Performance Reliance on SpaCy:** The performance of the entity graph construction is limited by SpaCy's ability to accurately identify entities. While SpaCy is efficient, it may not extract entities and relationships as effectively as LLM-based approaches in certain scenarios.
    * **Limited Scope of Experiments:** While the authors evaluate their method and compare it to other popular alternatives, all evaluations are done on the question-answering task. It would be interesting to also benchmark the various methods in some other common RAG applications such as summarization, document translation, and/or creative text generation.
    * **Intuitive Retrieval Design:** The paper admits that its retrieval design is intuitive. The effectiveness of the proposed adaptive retrieval strategy would be more convincing if it were compared to different and/or more sophisticated alternatives.

* **Significance:**

    The paper makes a valuable contribution by improving the practicality of graph-based RAG.  The efficiency gains achieved by E2GraphRAG make it more feasible to deploy graph-based RAG in resource-constrained environments and for large-scale datasets. The adaptive retrieval strategy offers a more flexible approach to query processing. While not a groundbreaking theoretical advancement, the paper's focus on practical improvements makes it relevant to the RAG research and development community.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 6 to this paper. While the efficiency improvements and adaptive retrieval strategy are valuable, the paper's novelty is somewhat limited. The method builds upon existing techniques, and the dependence on SpaCy for entity extraction could limit its performance in some cases. In addition, the work could use a more thorough evaluation by including other common RAG applications besides question answering. However, the paper provides a well-engineered and empirically sound contribution to the field, making a practical graph-based RAG more usable in real-world settings.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### ProofNet++: A Neuro-Symbolic System for Formal Proof Verification with Self-Correction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24230v1)
- **Authors**: Murari Ambati
- **Abstract**: We propose ProofNet++, a neuro-symbolic framework that enhances automated theorem proving by combining large language models (LLMs) with formal proof verification and self-correction mechanisms. Current LLM-based systems suffer from hallucinated logical steps and unverifiable reasoning. ProofNet++ mitigates these limitations by integrating symbolic proof tree supervision, a reinforcement learning loop using verifiers as reward functions, and an iterative self-correction module. Our experiments on miniF2F, Lean's mathlib, and HOL Light show that ProofNet++ significantly improves proof accuracy, correctness, and formal verifiability over prior models. We provide theoretical analysis of the convergence and stability of the verifier-guided RL framework and release our datasets and codebase for future research.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ProofNet++ paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ProofNet++, a neuro-symbolic framework designed to enhance automated theorem proving by integrating large language models (LLMs) with formal proof verification and self-correction mechanisms.  The core idea is to overcome the limitations of LLMs, such as hallucinated steps and unverifiable reasoning, by incorporating: 1) verifier-in-the-loop reinforcement learning, where a formal verifier provides rewards; 2) curriculum learning over structured proof trees from formal libraries; and 3) a self-correction loop that diagnoses errors and proposes repairs.  The system is evaluated on miniF2F, Lean's mathlib, and HOL Light, demonstrating improvements in proof accuracy, correctness, and verifiability compared to prior models.  The paper also presents a theoretical analysis of the verifier-guided RL framework and releases the datasets and codebase.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination and integration of several existing techniques. The idea of using a verifier in a loop with an LLM isn't entirely new, nor is reinforcement learning or self-correction in general. However, the comprehensive and cohesive architecture integrating all these elements, specifically tailored for formal theorem proving with a detailed focus on addressing hallucination and correctness, is a distinct contribution. Prior works often focus on a subset of these techniques or lack the tight integration crucial for formal verification.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a critical gap in the application of LLMs to formal domains. LLMs have shown promise in reasoning and code generation, but their unreliability in formal settings has limited their applicability. By creating a system that prioritizes formal correctness through tight integration with a verifier and self-correction, ProofNet++ makes a step towards bridging the gap between the flexibility of LLMs and the rigor of formal methods. This can have implications for AI safety, verified program synthesis, and automated theorem proving. The demonstration of empirical improvements and the provided datasets are also valuable contributions to the research community.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong empirical results:** The reported performance gains across multiple datasets provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of the ProofNet++ architecture.
    * **Comprehensive approach:** The integration of verifier-guided RL, curriculum learning, and self-correction creates a robust and well-rounded system.
    * **Detailed analysis:** The error analysis and statistical modeling offer valuable insights into the system's behavior and the challenges of formal theorem proving with LLMs.
    * **Open source contribution:** Releasing the datasets and codebase promotes reproducibility and encourages further research in this area.
    * **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies and addresses the limitations of existing LLM-based systems in formal theorem proving.
    * **Sound theoretical foundations:** The inclusion of a theoretical analysis of the convergence and stability of the verifier-guided RL framework adds rigor to the work.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational cost:**  The paper acknowledges the computational expense of verifier-guided RL, which may limit its scalability. The optimizations implemented only provide a marginal improvement relative to the resource requirement itself. This raises concerns about the practical applicability of the approach to more complex or larger-scale problems without substantial resource investment.
    * **Reliance on existing tools:** The system relies heavily on existing formal verifiers (Lean, HOL Light).  While this is understandable, it limits the system's portability to other formalisms or domains without compatible verifiers.
    * **Incremental Novelty:**  While the combination of techniques is novel and effective, the individual components are based on existing work.  The system feels more like an excellent *engineering* contribution than a groundbreaking theoretical advance.
    * **Limited ablation studies:** While the paper includes an error analysis, a more detailed ablation study isolating the impact of each component (verifier-guided RL, curriculum learning, self-correction) would strengthen the claims about their individual contributions.
    * **Lack of comparison with other hybrid approaches:** The related work section could be improved by comparing against similar hybrid approaches in formal verification. The paper does not rigorously compare results to or contrast the approach with other neuro-symbolic methods.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in automated theorem proving and verified program synthesis. It demonstrates the feasibility of using LLMs in conjunction with formal methods to achieve higher levels of correctness and reliability. The insights gained from the error analysis and the released datasets can also inform the development of more robust and scalable neuro-symbolic systems.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of 7 out of 10 is appropriate. The paper makes a solid contribution by effectively integrating several existing techniques to address a critical problem in the application of LLMs to formal domains. The empirical results are convincing, and the analysis provides valuable insights. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, the reliance on existing verifiers, and the computational cost limit the overall impact and warrant a slightly lower score. Further, the lack of rigorous comparison against other neuro-symbolic method lessens the impact of the results somewhat. A score of 7 reflects a well-executed piece of engineering research with practical value, but acknowledges that the underlying ideas build on prior work and scalability concerns linger.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### MIRAGE: Assessing Hallucination in Multimodal Reasoning Chains of MLLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24238v1)
- **Authors**: Bowen Dong, Minheng Ni, Zitong Huang, Guanglei Yang, Wangmeng Zuo, Lei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal hallucination in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) restricts the correctness of MLLMs. However, multimodal hallucinations are multi-sourced and arise from diverse causes. Existing benchmarks fail to adequately distinguish between perception-induced hallucinations and reasoning-induced hallucinations. This failure constitutes a significant issue and hinders the diagnosis of multimodal reasoning failures within MLLMs. To address this, we propose the {\dataset} benchmark, which isolates reasoning hallucinations by constructing questions where input images are correctly perceived by MLLMs yet reasoning errors persist. {\dataset} introduces multi-granular evaluation metrics: accuracy, factuality, and LLMs hallucination score for hallucination quantification. Our analysis reveals that (1) the model scale, data scale, and training stages significantly affect the degree of logical, fabrication, and factual hallucinations; (2) current MLLMs show no effective improvement on spatial hallucinations caused by misinterpreted spatial relationships, indicating their limited visual reasoning capabilities; and (3) question types correlate with distinct hallucination patterns, highlighting targeted challenges and potential mitigation strategies. To address these challenges, we propose {\method}, a method that combines curriculum reinforcement fine-tuning to encourage models to generate logic-consistent reasoning chains by stepwise reducing learning difficulty, and collaborative hint inference to reduce reasoning complexity. {\method} establishes a baseline on {\dataset}, and reduces the logical hallucinations in original base models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MIRAGE, a new benchmark for evaluating multimodal hallucination in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  MIRAGE focuses specifically on *reasoning-induced* hallucinations by constructing questions where the input images are correctly perceived by MLLMs, but reasoning errors still occur.  The benchmark features multi-granular evaluation metrics (accuracy, factuality, and a novel LLM-based hallucination score).  The paper analyzes the performance of several MLLMs on MIRAGE, revealing insights into the factors affecting hallucination (model scale, data scale, training stages) and the types of hallucinations prevalent in different question types.  Finally, the paper proposes a method called Logos, which combines curriculum reinforcement fine-tuning and collaborative hint inference, as a way to mitigate logical hallucinations, establishing a baseline on the MIRAGE benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is the MIRAGE benchmark.  The critical distinction between perception-induced and reasoning-induced hallucination is a valuable one, as it directly addresses a major shortcoming in existing MLLM evaluation. Prior benchmarks often conflate these two error sources, making it difficult to diagnose the root cause of failures. The multi-granular evaluation approach is also a plus. Logos is a reasonable, albeit incremental, method for mitigating the specific problem targeted by MIRAGE.
*   **Significance:** Addressing hallucination is crucial for building trustworthy MLLMs. By isolating reasoning-induced hallucinations, MIRAGE enables more targeted development and debugging efforts. The benchmark can serve as a valuable tool for researchers to analyze the strengths and weaknesses of different MLLMs and develop more effective mitigation strategies. The identified correlations between question types and hallucination patterns are useful insights. Logos provides a baseline and demonstrates that targeted training can improve performance on MIRAGE.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of distinguishing between hallucination types and the limitations of existing benchmarks.
    *   **Well-Designed Benchmark:** MIRAGE is designed to isolate and measure reasoning-induced hallucinations, a critical aspect of MLLM evaluation. The data structure including reasoning chains is well organized.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough analysis of several MLLMs on MIRAGE, providing valuable insights into the factors contributing to hallucination.
    *   **Mitigation Strategy:** Logos offers a concrete method for mitigating logical hallucinations and demonstrates its effectiveness on MIRAGE.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:**  While Logos is a useful contribution, the mitigation method itself isn't groundbreaking.  Curriculum learning and collaborative inference are established techniques.
    *   **Scope of Mirage:** The focus on reasoning-induced hallucinations might limit the benchmark's overall applicability. In real-world scenarios, both perception and reasoning contribute to hallucinations, and their interaction might be complex.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper focuses primarily on empirical evaluation and doesn't delve deeply into the theoretical reasons *why* certain MLLMs are more prone to specific types of hallucinations.
    *   **Generality of Results:** While several models are evaluated, some results are specific to the Qwen models.  Broader testing with diverse model architectures is needed to demonstrate the generality of findings.
*   **Potential Influence:** MIRAGE has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating reasoning capabilities in MLLMs. This will enable more targeted improvements in model architectures, training methods, and mitigation strategies. Logos offers a reasonable starting point to overcome logical hallucination.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant contribution (MIRAGE) to the field of MLLM evaluation, directly addressing a clear and important problem (distinguishing hallucination types).  The benchmark is well-designed and the empirical analysis provides valuable insights. However, the proposed mitigation method (Logos) is incremental and the scope of MIRAGE is limited to reasoning-induced hallucinations.  A deeper theoretical analysis and broader model evaluation would further strengthen the paper. Thus, the score of 7.5 reflects the solid foundation, strong empirical contributions, and potential for the work to influence future MLLM development, while acknowledging its limitations in scope and theoretical depth.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Advantageous Parameter Expansion Training Makes Better Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24241v1)
- **Authors**: Naibin Gu, Yilong Chen, Zhenyu Zhang, Peng Fu, Zheng Lin, Shuohuan Wang, Yu Sun, Hua Wu, Weiping Wang, Haifeng Wang
- **Abstract**: Although scaling up the number of trainable parameters in both pre-training and fine-tuning can effectively improve the performance of large language models, it also leads to increased computational overhead. When delving into the parameter difference, we find that a subset of parameters, termed advantageous parameters, plays a crucial role in determining model performance. Further analysis reveals that stronger models tend to possess more such parameters. In this paper, we propose Advantageous Parameter EXpansion Training (APEX), a method that progressively expands advantageous parameters into the space of disadvantageous ones, thereby increasing their proportion and enhancing training effectiveness. Further theoretical analysis from the perspective of matrix effective rank explains the performance gains of APEX. Extensive experiments on both instruction tuning and continued pre-training demonstrate that, in instruction tuning, APEX outperforms full-parameter tuning while using only 52% of the trainable parameters. In continued pre-training, APEX achieves the same perplexity level as conventional training with just 33% of the training data, and yields significant improvements on downstream tasks.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Advantageous Parameter EXpansion Training (APEX), a novel training strategy that aims to improve the performance of large language models (LLMs) without scaling up the number of parameters. The method identifies a subset of "advantageous" parameters, those with high activations, and progressively expands their influence into the space of less impactful parameters. This is achieved through expansion operators applied to the multi-head attention (MHA) and feed-forward network (FFN) modules, followed by theoretical justification based on matrix effective rank. Experiments demonstrate that APEX achieves comparable or better performance than full-parameter tuning while using significantly fewer trainable parameters, and also demonstrates faster convergence in continual pre-training scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a compelling idea – focusing training efforts on a smaller, more impactful subset of parameters within an LLM. The concept of "advantageous parameters" is intuitively appealing and backed by empirical observations on activation distributions.

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of expanding the influence of advantageous parameters rather than simply pruning less important ones is relatively novel. While parameter-efficient tuning methods and model expansion techniques exist, APEX combines these concepts in a unique way, aiming to increase the "concentration" of influential parameters within a fixed architecture. However, the expansion operators draw heavily on existing model expansion methods, which reduces the novelty. The theoretical analysis, while providing some justification, is rather high-level and relies on several assumptions that might not always hold true in practice.

*   **Significance:** If the results hold up, APEX could have a significant impact by reducing the computational cost associated with training and fine-tuning LLMs. The reported gains in parameter efficiency and convergence speed are substantial. However, the approach comes with certain limitations. First, assessment of "advantageous" parameters must be done with some computation. In a truly massively large language model this could be expensive. Also, it's only shown in limited settings.

*   **Strengths:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology and experimental setup. The empirical results appear promising and are compared against a comprehensive set of baselines. Theoretical analysis provides some justification for the results, though the practical contribution is more significant.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The theoretical analysis is somewhat weak and relies on assumptions that might not always hold.
    *   The reliance on tracking activations introduces a computational overhead, and while the authors claim this is minimal, it needs to be carefully considered, especially for very large models. How this applies to transfer learning scenarios is also not fully elucidated.
    *   The method involves several hyperparameters (KMHA, KFFN, number of stages) that require tuning, and the sensitivity of the results to these parameters is not fully explored.
    *   The results are primarily demonstrated on LLaMA-2 7B and LLaMA 3-8B, and TinyLLaMA-v1-1.1B, and it's unclear how well the method would generalize to significantly larger or architecturally different models.
    *   Certain implementation details such as the selection of thresholds and data volume used for initial assessment remain unclear.

*   **Potential Influence:** APEX has the potential to influence future research in parameter-efficient LLM training and could be a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners working with limited computational resources.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** While APEX presents a novel combination of existing techniques with encouraging empirical results, the theoretical grounding is limited, and the reliance on activation tracking could pose a practical challenge for extremely large models. The impact hinges heavily on its generalizability to much larger and different models and its scalability. While the performance increase is statistically significant and promising, the paper could have been strengthened by ablations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mamba Knockout for Unraveling Factual Information Flow
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24244v1)
- **Authors**: Nir Endy, Idan Daniel Grosbard, Yuval Ran-Milo, Yonatan Slutzky, Itay Tshuva, Raja Giryes
- **Abstract**: This paper investigates the flow of factual information in Mamba State-Space Model (SSM)-based language models. We rely on theoretical and empirical connections to Transformer-based architectures and their attention mechanisms. Exploiting this relationship, we adapt attentional interpretability techniques originally developed for Transformers--specifically, the Attention Knockout methodology--to both Mamba-1 and Mamba-2. Using them we trace how information is transmitted and localized across tokens and layers, revealing patterns of subject-token information emergence and layer-wise dynamics. Notably, some phenomena vary between mamba models and Transformer based models, while others appear universally across all models inspected--hinting that these may be inherent to LLMs in general. By further leveraging Mamba's structured factorization, we disentangle how distinct "features" either enable token-to-token information exchange or enrich individual tokens, thus offering a unified lens to understand Mamba internal operations.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

This paper extends interpretability techniques, specifically Attention Knockout, originally developed for Transformer-based language models, to Mamba-based state-space models (SSMs). The authors adapt and apply Attention Knockout to Mamba-1 and Mamba-2, analyzing the flow of factual information across tokens and layers. The analysis reveals shared patterns and distinct differences in information flow between Mamba models and Transformers.  A novel feature knockout mechanism is introduced to disentangle how different feature types contribute to token-to-token communication and individual token enrichment within Mamba. This offers a unified perspective on Mamba's internal operations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in applying Attention Knockout, a well-established Transformer interpretability technique, to Mamba-based models.  While connections between SSMs and attention have been suggested theoretically, empirically demonstrating the applicability of Attention Knockout is a useful step. The introduction of the 'feature knockout' mechanism, leveraging the unique structure of SSMs, offers more targeted intervention and, thus, more nuanced interpretability than just applying a "knockout". However, the core technique is adapted rather than fundamentally new. The validation of results across various model sizes and families strengthens findings, but the scope remains within the realm of adapting an existing technique.

*   **Significance:** Understanding the inner workings of SSMs like Mamba is crucial as they emerge as competitive alternatives to Transformers. By demonstrating the relevance (and limitations) of Attention Knockout in this context, the paper contributes to a more unified understanding of factual information processing in language models, regardless of the underlying architecture. This accelerates the development of better explainability tools for the whole language model space. Also, identifying areas where Mamba differs from Transformers adds to our understanding of the specific inductive biases offered by each architecture.  The feature knockout mechanism provides a novel way to analyze SSMs, which could potentially lead to insights into the role of different feature types in other SSM-based models as well.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Adaptation of a useful technique from one model family to another.
    *   Provides empirical evidence of shared and distinct properties in how Transformers and Mamba models handle factual information.
    *   Introduction of a novel "feature knockout" mechanism for more fine-grained analysis of SSMs.
    *   Validation of results across different model sizes and architectures.
    *   Code availability, increasing reproducibility.
    *   Direct comparison of behaviors across Mamba-1, Mamba-2, GPT-2, Llama3, and Mistral is a plus.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The core technique (Attention Knockout) is not novel.
    *   The analysis primarily focuses on *correlational* relationships, which can make inferences to causation harder, and potentially limits the implications from the analysis.
    *   While the feature knockout is more targeted, it's still limited to binary (on/off) interventions. More sophisticated approaches may be needed.
    *   The analyzed dataset is limited. Though they provide analyses on slightly different data, a more robust exploration of datasets could increase generalizability.
    * The limitations section acknowledges several points, such as use of continuous layer windows, and limited explanation for token-independent operations (e.g. gating), further solidifying the lack of "deep" novel findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by:

    *   Encouraging the development of new interpretability tools tailored to the specific structure of SSMs.
    *   Guiding training and fine-tuning strategies for Mamba-based models.
    *   Providing a framework for comparing information flow patterns across different language model architectures.

* **Rigorous Rationale:** Though the work is well-executed and has potential influence in encouraging better techniques and tools for interpreting SSMs, the lack of deep novel techniques or findings and an over reliance on correlation limits a higher score. The adapted tools are valuable, and the finding of consistent patterns in diverse models is great, but other limitations reduce its "ground-breaking-ness."

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### LTM3D: Bridging Token Spaces for Conditional 3D Generation with Auto-Regressive Diffusion Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24245v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Kang, Zihan Zheng, Lei Chu, Yue Gao, Jiahao Li, Hao Pan, Xuejin Chen, Yan Lu
- **Abstract**: We present LTM3D, a Latent Token space Modeling framework for conditional 3D shape generation that integrates the strengths of diffusion and auto-regressive (AR) models. While diffusion-based methods effectively model continuous latent spaces and AR models excel at capturing inter-token dependencies, combining these paradigms for 3D shape generation remains a challenge. To address this, LTM3D features a Conditional Distribution Modeling backbone, leveraging a masked autoencoder and a diffusion model to enhance token dependency learning. Additionally, we introduce Prefix Learning, which aligns condition tokens with shape latent tokens during generation, improving flexibility across modalities. We further propose a Latent Token Reconstruction module with Reconstruction-Guided Sampling to reduce uncertainty and enhance structural fidelity in generated shapes. Our approach operates in token space, enabling support for multiple 3D representations, including signed distance fields, point clouds, meshes, and 3D Gaussian Splatting. Extensive experiments on image- and text-conditioned shape generation tasks demonstrate that LTM3D outperforms existing methods in prompt fidelity and structural accuracy while offering a generalizable framework for multi-modal, multi-representation 3D generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LTM3D, a Latent Token space Modeling framework designed for conditional 3D shape generation. LTM3D combines the strengths of diffusion models (for continuous latent space modeling) and auto-regressive (AR) models (for capturing inter-token dependencies). The framework incorporates a Conditional Distribution Modeling backbone (Masked Autoencoder and diffusion model), Prefix Learning (aligns condition tokens with shape latent tokens during generation), and a Latent Token Reconstruction module with Reconstruction-Guided Sampling (reduces uncertainty and enhances structural fidelity). LTM3D supports multiple 3D data representations like SDFs, point clouds, meshes, and 3D Gaussian Splatting. The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art results on image- and text-conditioned shape generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novel Combination:** The key novelty lies in the integration of diffusion and auto-regressive models within a latent token space for 3D generation. This addresses the limitations of each approach individually.
    * **Prefix Learning:** The Prefix Learning module offers a flexible way to align condition tokens with shape latent tokens, decoupling the encoding and generation processes. This is a significant improvement over existing methods that align during the encoding stage, increasing the method’s adaptability.
    * **Reconstruction-Guided Sampling:** This module effectively reduces the uncertainty in generated tokens, leading to improved structural fidelity and alignment with input conditions, a crucial aspect for conditional generation tasks.
    * **Multi-Representation Support:** LTM3D's ability to handle multiple 3D representations within a single framework is a valuable contribution, eliminating the need for separate generation backbones for each data type.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on image- and text-conditioned shape generation tasks, validated by multiple metrics and qualitative results. The ablation studies further support the effectiveness of the proposed modules.
    * **Clear and Well-Organized:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it easy to understand the proposed framework and its components.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** The architecture is quite involved, with multiple modules and components. This complexity may make it challenging to implement and train.
    * **Domain Gap Still Present:** While Prefix Learning aims to mitigate the domain gap between condition tokens and shape latent space, this area could still benefit from improvement. The reliance on pre-trained models like DinoV2 and CLIP also introduces potential biases and limitations.
    * **Limited Diversity:** The text mentions a potential decrease in diversity when conditioned on the same input prompts. This is a common trade-off in conditional generation but needs further investigation and potential mitigation strategies. The relatively low P-IS metric (relative to other methods like Michelangelo in Table 2, where it is much higher) hints at some diversity limits of LTM3D.
    * **Dependency on Shape Encoders:** LTM3D, as a generation framework, relies on existing shape encoders. The overall performance is therefore bottlenecked by the quality of the encoders it uses.
    * **Lack of Ablation on all components:** While ablation studies are performed, they are focused on only Prefix Learning and Reconstruction Guided Sampling. Ablation on the Masked Autoencoder, DenoiseNet and the overall Conditional Distribution Modeling might reveal the critical components.
    * **Joint Training limitations:** The joint training experiment of multiple representations highlight challenges that LTM3D can't resolve fully.

* **Significance and Impact:**
    * LTM3D advances the field of conditional 3D shape generation by effectively integrating diffusion and AR models within a latent token space.
    * The framework's flexibility and support for multiple 3D representations make it a valuable tool for various applications, including content creation, virtual reality, and robotics.
    * The proposed Prefix Learning and Reconstruction-Guided Sampling modules offer novel solutions to address key challenges in conditional generation, potentially inspiring further research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7.5 seems appropriate. The paper presents a novel combination of existing techniques, achieves state-of-the-art results, and addresses several challenges in conditional 3D generation. However, the architectural complexity, dependence on pre-trained models and shape encoders, and some potential limitations on diversity prevent it from achieving a higher score. Further improvements in the joint training of multiple 3D representations will significantly enhance LTM3D's capabilities.
