To address these issues, we propose HiCaM, a Hierarchical-Causal Modification framework that operates through a hierarchical summary tree and a causal graph. Furthermore, to evaluate HiCaM, we derive a multi-domain dataset from various benchmarks, providing a resource for assessing its effectiveness. Comprehensive evaluations on the dataset demonstrate significant improvements over strong LLMs, with our method achieving up to a 79.50\% win rate. These results highlight the comprehensiveness of our approach, showing consistent performance improvements across multiple models and domains.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "HiCaM: A Hierarchical-Causal Modification Framework for Long-Form Text Modification":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HiCaM, a hierarchical-causal modification framework designed to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in long-form text modification (LTM) tasks. The framework addresses two primary challenges: 1) preventing undesired modifications by inappropriately altering irrelevant content, and 2) ensuring necessary modifications are made to implicitly related passages to maintain document coherence. HiCaM operates through a hierarchical summary tree and an entity-specific causal graph, guiding LLMs to perform modifications that maintain both coherence and completeness. The authors also introduce a new multi-domain dataset derived from existing benchmarks to evaluate HiCaM. Experimental results demonstrate significant improvements over strong LLMs, with HiCaM achieving up to a 79.50% win rate.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining a hierarchical summary tree with a causal graph for LTM is moderately novel. While both hierarchical summarization and causal reasoning have been used in NLP previously, their joint application within a structured framework designed *specifically* for long-form text modification is a valuable contribution. The concept of entity-centric analysis is particularly insightful, addressing a key limitation in many existing LTM approaches that often struggle with maintaining context across long documents.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and increasingly important problem – how to effectively modify long-form texts using LLMs while preserving coherence and avoiding unintended alterations. The problem is crucial for applications in various domains, including storytelling, scientific writing, and document revision. The framework developed tackles the two challenges of unintended and missing modifications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of long-form text modification and identifies specific challenges that LLMs face.
    *   **Well-Designed Framework:** HiCaM provides a structured and intuitive approach to LTM, leveraging hierarchical summarization and causal reasoning to guide LLMs.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation includes a diverse multi-domain dataset and comparisons against strong LLM baselines. The win rate metrics provide a clear indication of HiCaM's effectiveness.
    *   **Analysis of Results:** The paper provides insightful analyses of the results, examining the impact of different components of HiCaM and the performance across various datasets. The discussion of output length is particularly valuable for understanding the framework's behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The increased computational cost (2-3x) compared to standard generation is a significant drawback, potentially limiting its practical applicability in resource-constrained settings. The authors suggest parallelization as a mitigation strategy, which is a useful avenue to explore further.
    *   **Limited Language Coverage:** The limited evaluation on Chinese datasets (only 1 out of 8) raises concerns about the generalizability of the framework to other languages. The framework's architecture should be tested on more low-resource languages.
    *   **Evaluation Details:** The evaluation relies on GPT-4 to evaluate outputs. While this offers an objective measure, it also includes a risk of length and verbosity bias which is only superficially addressed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in long-form text generation and modification. The HiCaM framework provides a solid foundation for developing more sophisticated LTM approaches, and the multi-domain dataset will be a valuable resource for the community.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a moderately novel and significant contribution to the field of natural language generation. HiCaM provides a structured and effective approach to long-form text modification, addressing key challenges related to coherence and completeness. The strengths of the paper include a well-defined problem, a well-designed framework, comprehensive evaluation, and insightful analysis. However, the increased computational cost and limited language coverage are notable limitations. The paper also misses a more in-depth analysis into the evaluation strategies themselves, which would have improved the overall quality. On balance, the paper represents a worthwhile advance in LTM, but some limitations prevent a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SwiftEval: Developing a Language-Specific Benchmark for LLM-generated Code Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24324v1)
- **Authors**: Ivan Petrukha, Yana Kurliak, Nataliia Stulova
- **Abstract**: In recent years, large language models (LLMs) have showcased significant advancements in code generation. However, most evaluation benchmarks are primarily oriented towards Python, making it difficult to evaluate other programming languages, such as Swift, with high quality. By examining widely established multilingual benchmarks like HumanEval-XL and MultiPL-E, we identified critical issues specific to their Swift components, making them insufficient or even irrelevant for assessing LLM coding capabilities on Swift. Unlike these existing approaches, which prioritize rapid scaling and generalization by automatically translating Python-centric benchmarks with LLMs, we adopt a quality-over-quantity methodology. We present SwiftEval, the first Swift-oriented benchmark consisting of 28 carefully hand-crafted problems, and evaluate 44 popular Code LLMs on it. Our results show significant LLM scores drop for problems requiring language-specific features, most noticeable in the models of smaller sizes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SwiftEval, a new benchmark designed specifically for evaluating the code generation capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the Swift programming language.  The authors argue that existing multilingual benchmarks, often based on translations from Python, are insufficient for Swift due to its unique language features (static typing, protocols, etc.).  They created a Swift-native benchmark consisting of 28 hand-crafted problems covering both function-level and class-level tasks, including real-world scenarios. The paper then presents an evaluation of 44 popular code LLMs using both HumanEval (Python) and SwiftEval, demonstrating that language-specific features in Swift lead to significant performance drops in LLMs, especially smaller ones. The authors emphasize the importance of tailored benchmarks for each language to ensure high-quality development of multilingual Code LLMs. They show that even a small language-tailored benchmark offers more insightful results than large, but general-purpose benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in creating a dedicated benchmark for Swift. While multilingual benchmarks exist, the paper makes a strong case that direct translations from Python fail to capture the nuances and unique characteristics of Swift. The authors convincingly demonstrate the shortcomings of applying generic benchmarks to specific languages. The decision to hand-craft problems, rather than relying on LLM-translated tasks, is a strength, as it ensures that the problems genuinely test Swift-specific skills. However, the core idea of needing language-specific benchmarks is not entirely new; others have voiced similar concerns for different programming languages. The novelty is in providing concrete evidence and a tailored solution for Swift.

* **Significance:** The paper has important implications for the field of code generation with LLMs. It highlights a critical limitation of current multilingual benchmarking practices. By showing significant performance variations between HumanEval and SwiftEval, the authors clearly demonstrate that evaluation results are not directly transferable across programming languages. This suggests that developers need to be cautious when interpreting benchmark results and that language-specific benchmarks are crucial for developing LLMs capable of producing high-quality code in diverse languages. The paper encourages the creation of similar benchmarks for other under-represented languages, contributing to a more nuanced and accurate assessment of LLM code generation abilities. The analysis of why existing benchmarks fail (e.g., incorrect use of AnyHashable, failure to leverage optionals correctly) provides valuable insights for future benchmark design. The paper's findings could influence the direction of research in this area, encouraging a shift towards more targeted and language-aware evaluation methodologies.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem with existing multilingual benchmarks in the context of Swift.
    * **Convincing Empirical Evidence:** The experimental results demonstrate a significant performance gap between HumanEval and SwiftEval, providing strong support for the authors' claims.
    * **Detailed Error Analysis:** The paper includes a detailed discussion of the specific issues encountered with translated benchmarks, providing valuable insights into the challenges of evaluating code generation across languages.
    * **Well-Designed Benchmark:** SwiftEval is designed to assess key Swift features, going beyond basic algorithmic tasks.
    * **Practical Implications:** The paper's findings have practical implications for developers and researchers working on code generation with LLMs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scale of SwiftEval:** With only 28 problems, SwiftEval is relatively small compared to benchmarks like HumanEval. This raises concerns about the generalizability of the results, though the thorough manual curation helps to mitigate this.
    * **Focus on Correctness Only:** The benchmark primarily focuses on functional correctness (pass@k). It doesn't explicitly evaluate other important aspects of code quality, such as style, efficiency, or security. While the authors acknowledge this and propose future directions to address security and efficiency, it remains a limitation.

* **Overall:** The paper makes a solid contribution by addressing the limitations of existing multilingual benchmarks and providing a language-specific solution for Swift. The creation and evaluation of SwiftEval constitutes a valuable addition to the landscape of code generation benchmarks. While the benchmark's size is a limitation, the careful design and clear demonstration of the need for language-tailored evaluation make the work significant. The detailed analysis of failure cases in current benchmarks is especially helpful.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper is novel and important, but not transformative. The novelty lies in the specific application to Swift, and the creation of a targeted benchmark. The significance stems from highlighting a critical weakness in current evaluation practices and providing a pathway for more accurate assessment of code-generating LLMs. The relatively small size of SwiftEval, and the lack of metrics beyond functional correctness, hold the score back from being higher. If SwiftEval was more extensive and included dimensions of code quality beyond functional correctness, the score would have been higher. The work's impact will be seen if others adopt the principle of language-specific benchmarking, and develop high quality benchmarks of their own. The .5 is added because of the valuable error analysis of problems encountered with existing approaches.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### DisTime: Distribution-based Time Representation for Video Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24329v1)
- **Authors**: Yingsen Zeng, Zepeng Huang, Yujie Zhong, Chengjian Feng, Jie Hu, Lin Ma, Yang Liu
- **Abstract**: Despite advances in general video understanding, Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs) face challenges in precise temporal localization due to discrete time representations and limited temporally aware datasets. Existing methods for temporal expression either conflate time with text-based numerical values, add a series of dedicated temporal tokens, or regress time using specialized temporal grounding heads. To address these issues, we introduce DisTime, a lightweight framework designed to enhance temporal comprehension in Video-LLMs. DisTime employs a learnable token to create a continuous temporal embedding space and incorporates a Distribution-based Time Decoder that generates temporal probability distributions, effectively mitigating boundary ambiguities and maintaining temporal continuity. Additionally, the Distribution-based Time Encoder re-encodes timestamps to provide time markers for Video-LLMs. To overcome temporal granularity limitations in existing datasets, we propose an automated annotation paradigm that combines the captioning capabilities of Video-LLMs with the localization expertise of dedicated temporal models. This leads to the creation of InternVid-TG, a substantial dataset with 1.25M temporally grounded events across 179k videos, surpassing ActivityNet-Caption by 55 times. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DisTime achieves state-of-the-art performance across benchmarks in three time-sensitive tasks while maintaining competitive performance in Video QA tasks. Code and data are released at https://github.com/josephzpng/DisTime.
- **Summary**: The paper "DisTime: Distribution-based Time Representation for Video Large Language Models" introduces a novel framework to enhance temporal comprehension in Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs). The approach, called DisTime, uses a learnable token to create a continuous temporal embedding space and employs a Distribution-based Time Decoder to generate temporal probability distributions, aiming to mitigate boundary ambiguities and maintain temporal continuity. Additionally, a Distribution-based Time Encoder re-encodes timestamps to provide time markers. To address the lack of large-scale temporally aware datasets, the authors propose an automated annotation paradigm combining Video-LLM captioning with the localization expertise of dedicated temporal models, resulting in the InternVid-TG dataset containing 1.25M temporally grounded events across 179k videos. Extensive experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on time-sensitive tasks while maintaining competitive performance in Video QA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates some degree of novelty through its combined contributions. While the idea of using a continuous time representation isn't entirely new, the combination of a single learnable token, distribution-based time decoder and encoder, and an automated annotation pipeline provides a distinct approach. The automated generation of temporal annotations using LLMs and dedicated temporal models for the creation of a large dataset also adds to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key limitation of current Video-LLMs: precise temporal localization. The DisTime framework and the InternVid-TG dataset directly tackle this issue, showing improved performance on time-sensitive tasks. The scale of the generated dataset is also significant, exceeding existing datasets by a large margin. If the dataset is truly high-quality, it has the potential to serve as a valuable resource for the community. The practical implications of improved temporal understanding in videos are substantial, especially in applications like video editing, surveillance, and instructional learning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a Clear Limitation:** The paper identifies and directly addresses the limitations of existing Video-LLMs in handling temporal information.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** DisTime is a well-defined and technically sound framework with a clear rationale for its components.
    *   **Large-Scale Dataset:** The creation of InternVid-TG addresses the scarcity of temporally aware video datasets.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on several benchmarks, showcasing the effectiveness of DisTime.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on LLM and Temporal Models:** The dataset generation heavily relies on the quality of the initial LLM and the dedicated temporal models used. Any biases or limitations in these models could propagate to the generated dataset. More analysis of the noise introduced in the annotation process should have been provided.
    *   **Evaluation of Dataset Quality:** While the scale of InternVid-TG is impressive, its quality is crucial. The 1k subset isn't sufficiently large to demonstrate quality beyond doubt. It would be ideal to compare to human annotations to further validate the quality of the labels.
    *   **Limited Ablation Study:** While the ablation experiments provide some insights, a more in-depth analysis of the individual components and their interactions would be beneficial. For example, a breakdown of the performance gain from each component on various benchmarks would provide a clearer picture of the contributions.
    *   **Reproducibility:** While the authors state the code and data will be released, the ability to reproduce the impressive results from the dataset creation pipeline is not guaranteed and may be limited by computational resources.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the paper's strengths and weaknesses, I would assign it a score of **7**. The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of Video-LLMs by addressing the important problem of temporal understanding. The DisTime framework is innovative and technically sound, and the creation of the InternVid-TG dataset is a significant achievement. The improvements shown across benchmarks also demonstrate a tangible advance over existing approaches.

However, the paper is not without its limitations. The concerns surrounding dataset quality and the lack of further evaluation provide some skepticism. I suggest the authors continue to expand the annotation benchmark and conduct experiments in-the-loop with human evaluation. Furthermore, some of the claims could be presented more tentatively, and the details of the method could be described with more clarity. With these limitations in mind, I believe a score of 7 accurately reflects the paper's contribution to the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pangu DeepDiver: Adaptive Search Intensity Scaling via Open-Web Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24332v1)
- **Authors**: Wenxuan Shi, Haochen Tan, Chuqiao Kuang, Xiaoguang Li, Xiaozhe Ren, Chen Zhang, Hanting Chen, Yasheng Wang, Lifeng Shang, Fisher Yu, Yunhe Wang
- **Abstract**: Information seeking demands iterative evidence gathering and reflective reasoning, yet large language models (LLMs) still struggle with it in open-web question answering. Existing methods rely on static prompting rules or training with Wikipedia-based corpora and retrieval environments, limiting adaptability to the real-world web environment where ambiguity, conflicting evidence, and noise are prevalent. These constrained training settings hinder LLMs from learning to dynamically decide when and where to search, and how to adjust search depth and frequency based on informational demands. We define this missing capacity as Search Intensity Scaling (SIS)--the emergent skill to intensify search efforts under ambiguous or conflicting conditions, rather than settling on overconfident, under-verification answers. To study SIS, we introduce WebPuzzle, the first dataset designed to foster information-seeking behavior in open-world internet environments. WebPuzzle consists of 24K training instances and 275 test questions spanning both wiki-based and open-web queries. Building on this dataset, we propose DeepDiver, a Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework that promotes SIS by encouraging adaptive search policies through exploration under a real-world open-web environment. Experimental results show that Pangu-7B-Reasoner empowered by DeepDiver achieve performance on real-web tasks comparable to the 671B-parameter DeepSeek-R1. We detail DeepDiver's training curriculum from cold-start supervised fine-tuning to a carefully designed RL phase, and present that its capability of SIS generalizes from closed-form QA to open-ended tasks such as long-form writing. Our contributions advance adaptive information seeking in LLMs and provide a valuable benchmark and dataset for future research.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and score for the provided technical report on "Pangu DeepDiver: Adaptive Search Intensity Scaling via Open-Web Reinforcement Learning."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to improve information-seeking capabilities in Large Language Models (LLMs) by addressing the challenge of navigating the complex and noisy open web. They define "Search Intensity Scaling" (SIS) as the ability to dynamically adjust search frequency and depth based on informational needs. To facilitate research in this area, they present WebPuzzle, a new dataset specifically designed to evaluate information-seeking skills in a real-world internet environment, containing 24k training and 275 test instances, that spans both Wiki-based and open-web queries. Building upon WebPuzzle, they propose DeepDiver, a Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework that encourages adaptive search policies by allowing the LLM to interact with real-world search engines during training. The training curriculum involves supervised fine-tuning followed by a carefully designed RL phase with specific reward assignments and scheduling mechanisms. Experimental results demonstrate that a 7B parameter LLM trained with DeepDiver can achieve performance comparable to a significantly larger (671B) model on real-web tasks, and show that the capability of SIS generalizes from closed-form QA to open-ended tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects. First, the explicit focus on Search Intensity Scaling (SIS) as a distinct and important capability for LLMs is a valuable contribution. The creation of WebPuzzle directly addresses the limitations of existing datasets by providing a more realistic and challenging environment for training and evaluating information-seeking LLMs, which is especially important as many existing datasets don't force retrieval or handle noisy results. The RL framework, DeepDiver, combines supervised fine-tuning with RL in a novel way within an open-web setting, and the training curriculum (loose and strict rewards) is a reasonable approach.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is in addressing a key weakness of LLMs: their struggle with information seeking in unstructured and noisy environments. By developing a framework that promotes adaptive search, the paper contributes to making LLMs more reliable and useful for real-world applications. The results, showing comparable performance to a much larger model, suggest that this approach is promising for improving the efficiency of LLMs, as well as improving their ability to handle tasks requiring multiple search and reasoning iterations. The generalisability from closed-ended to open-ended tasks also enhances the relevance of this work.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of limited adaptability in LLMs when dealing with real-world, open-web environments and proposes a specific capability (SIS) to address it.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The introduction of WebPuzzle provides a valuable resource for future research in this area, with proper validation to ensure it requires external retrieval.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental results are thoroughly presented, with comparisons against strong baselines and ablation studies to analyze the impact of different components.
    *   **Generalization:** The demonstration that DeepDiver can generalize from closed-ended to open-ended tasks significantly enhances the value of the proposed approach.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Constraints:** The study acknowledges that their training and evaluation are limited to a 7B model due to computational constraints, which might limit the generalizability of the results to larger models.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** While WebPuzzle is a valuable contribution, the authors also mention that their curation process heavily relies on DeepSeek-R1, which could be introducing bias to the testing process.
    *   **Human Evaluation:** While the paper mentions human evaluation of the test set, details are somewhat limited. A more comprehensive description of the evaluation criteria and process would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Over-Searching:** The paper also notes that they see issues of "overthinking" and over-searching behavior in the DeepDiver model. Further analysis of this behavior and potential solutions would improve the paper.
*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence future research by:
    *   Focusing attention on the importance of adaptive search intensity in LLMs.
    *   Providing a benchmark dataset (WebPuzzle) for evaluating information-seeking capabilities.
    *   Offering a practical framework (DeepDiver) for training LLMs to effectively navigate the open web.

Score: 8

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LLMs by addressing a practical and challenging problem: effective information-seeking in real-world, open-web environments. The introduction of WebPuzzle and DeepDiver represents a significant step forward in enabling LLMs to dynamically adapt their search intensity based on informational demands. While the study does have some limitations, the novelty, significance, and potential influence of the work justify a strong score. The limitations are clearly stated and do not fundamentally undermine the main findings of the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Two failure modes of deep transformers and how to avoid them: a unified theory of signal propagation at initialisation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24333v1)
- **Authors**: Alessio Giorlandino, Sebastian Goldt
- **Abstract**: Finding the right initialisation for neural networks is crucial to ensure smooth training and good performance. In transformers, the wrong initialisation can lead to one of two failure modes of self-attention layers: rank collapse, where all tokens collapse into similar representations, and entropy collapse, where highly concentrated attention scores lead to training instability. While the right initialisation has been extensively studied in feed-forward networks, an exact description of signal propagation through a full transformer block has so far been lacking. Here, we provide an analytical theory of signal propagation through vanilla transformer blocks with self-attention layers, layer normalisation, skip connections and ReLU MLP. To treat the self-attention layer, we draw on a formal parallel with the Random Energy Model from statistical physics. We identify and characterise two regimes governed by the variance of the query and key initialisations: a low-variance regime, where we recover the known rank collapse behaviour; and a previously unexplored high-variance regime, where signal is preserved but \textit{entropy collapse} occurs. In the low-variance regime, we calculate the critical strength for the residual connection to ensure signal propagation. Our theory yields trainability diagrams that identify the correct choice of initialisation hyper-parameters for a given architecture. Experiments with BERT-style models trained on TinyStories validate our predictions. Our theoretical framework gives a unified perspective on the two failure modes of self-attention and gives quantitative predictions on the scale of both weights and residual connections that guarantees smooth training.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and can provide a summary and critical evaluation.

**Summary:**

The paper presents a unified theoretical framework for understanding signal propagation at initialization in deep transformer networks. It identifies two key failure modes of self-attention layers: rank collapse and entropy collapse. Rank collapse happens when tokens collapse into similar representations, while entropy collapse occurs when attention scores become highly concentrated, leading to training instability. The paper derives an analytical theory based on an analogy to the Random Energy Model from statistical physics to describe signal propagation through transformer blocks, incorporating self-attention, layer normalization, skip connections, and ReLU MLPs.  The theory predicts the existence of different regimes based on the variance of the query/key initializations and the strength of residual connections, and it yields trainability diagrams that guide the choice of initialization hyperparameters.  The authors validate their theoretical predictions through experiments using BERT-style models trained on the TinyStories dataset.  Ultimately, the paper provides a quantitative understanding of how to choose weights and residual connections to ensure smooth training and avoid failure modes in deep transformers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies in several aspects.  First, it provides a *complete and unified theoretical analysis* of signal propagation in transformers at initialization, including self-attention, skip connections, and MLPs. Previous work often focused on parts of the transformer or made simplifying assumptions.  Second, the paper identifies and characterizes a previously unexplored *high-variance regime* where entropy collapse occurs. Prior research mainly concentrated on rank collapse. Third, by drawing on the *Random Energy Model from statistical physics*, the authors provide a novel perspective for understanding self-attention. Fourth, the paper offers *quantitative predictions* for the scale of weights and residual connections needed to guarantee smooth training, expressed as trainability diagrams, rather than just qualitative prescriptions. It explicitly models and calculates finite size corrections. These are more precise than many existing guidelines.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of this paper is that it provides a practical and theoretically grounded framework for initializing deep transformers.  The ability to *predict and avoid failure modes* at initialization is crucial for training very deep models efficiently. The *trainability diagrams* are useful tools for practitioners who are dealing with the vanishing gradient problem or the difficulty of training very deep transformers.  The validation on the TinyStories dataset, although somewhat limited, demonstrates the practical relevance of the theory. By providing a deeper understanding of the interplay between weight initialization, residual connections, and network depth, the paper helps bridge the gap between theoretical insights and practical implementations of transformer models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed and well-justified theoretical derivation using techniques from statistical physics.
    *   **Unified Framework:** It successfully unifies the understanding of rank collapse and entropy collapse into a single framework.
    *   **Quantitative Predictions:** The theory provides quantitative predictions that can be used to guide the initialization of transformers.
    *   **Clear Trainability Diagrams:** The paper presents trainability diagrams that are easy to understand and use for practical applications.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The theoretical predictions are validated by experiments on the TinyStories dataset.
    *   **Identification of Finite-Size effects:** The work quantifies and analyzes the limitations of theoretical predictions regarding finite-size effects.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Experimental Validation:** The experimental validation is primarily on the TinyStories dataset. While useful as a proof of concept, it would be beneficial to validate the theory on more complex and realistic datasets (e.g., larger language modeling benchmarks or downstream tasks) to prove the general applicability and scalability of the results.
    *   **Simplifying Assumptions:** The theory relies on certain simplifying assumptions, such as the assumption that the attention scores have Gaussian distributions. While justified, it's important to acknowledge that deviations from these assumptions may affect the accuracy of the predictions.
    *   **Practical Tuning:** While the trainability diagrams provide guidelines, choosing the *exact* optimal parameters likely still requires some tuning, even with the diagrams' guidance. It would be useful to provide more specific guidance on how to perform this tuning in practice.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of deep learning, particularly in the area of transformer models. The theoretical framework can be used to develop better initialization schemes, design more efficient training strategies, and train deeper and more powerful transformers. It could serve as the foundation for future research aimed at further refining our understanding of signal propagation in complex neural networks.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper makes a strong contribution to the theoretical understanding of signal propagation in transformers, unifying several previously disparate phenomena and providing quantitative guidelines for initialization. The use of the Random Energy Model analogy is a novel and insightful approach.  While the experimental validation is somewhat limited in scope, the theoretical rigor, the practical relevance of the trainability diagrams, and the potential impact on future research justify a high score. The weaknesses mainly revolve around the need for more extensive empirical validation and potential refinements to account for deviations from simplifying assumptions. Overall, the paper presents a significant advance in the theoretical understanding of deep transformers and has the potential to influence future research and practical applications.

- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 8/10

### Exploring Multimodal Challenges in Toxic Chinese Detection: Taxonomy, Benchmark, and Findings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24341v1)
- **Authors**: Shujian Yang, Shiyao Cui, Chuanrui Hu, Haicheng Wang, Tianwei Zhang, Minlie Huang, Jialiang Lu, Han Qiu
- **Abstract**: Detecting toxic content using language models is important but challenging. While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance in understanding Chinese, recent studies show that simple character substitutions in toxic Chinese text can easily confuse the state-of-the-art (SOTA) LLMs. In this paper, we highlight the multimodal nature of Chinese language as a key challenge for deploying LLMs in toxic Chinese detection. First, we propose a taxonomy of 3 perturbation strategies and 8 specific approaches in toxic Chinese content. Then, we curate a dataset based on this taxonomy, and benchmark 9 SOTA LLMs (from both the US and China) to assess if they can detect perturbed toxic Chinese text. Additionally, we explore cost-effective enhancement solutions like in-context learning (ICL) and supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Our results reveal two important findings. (1) LLMs are less capable of detecting perturbed multimodal Chinese toxic contents. (2) ICL or SFT with a small number of perturbed examples may cause the LLMs "overcorrect'': misidentify many normal Chinese contents as toxic.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper you provided:

**Summary**

The paper "Exploring Multimodal Challenges in Toxic Chinese Detection: Taxonomy, Benchmark, and Findings" tackles the problem of detecting toxic content in Chinese, specifically focusing on how language models (LLMs) can be bypassed using various perturbation techniques that exploit the multimodal nature of the language (glyphic, phonetic, and semantic aspects). The authors make the following contributions:

1.  **Taxonomy:** They propose a comprehensive taxonomy of Chinese toxicity perturbation methods, categorizing them into three strategies (glyph-based, phonetic ambiguity-based, and semantic flexibility-based) and eight specific techniques.
2.  **Dataset:** They create a large-scale dataset (CNTP) based on this taxonomy, containing perturbed toxic Chinese content, and a benchmark for the same.
3.  **Evaluation:** They benchmark several state-of-the-art LLMs (from both the US and China) using this dataset, finding that these models are significantly less effective in detecting perturbed toxic content compared to the original, unperturbed text. They also explore cost-effective mitigation strategies like in-context learning (ICL) and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) but find that these can lead to "overcorrection" and misclassification of normal content as toxic.

The paper highlights the unique challenges posed by the Chinese language in toxic content detection and the need for more robust models that can handle various perturbation techniques.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty stems from its specific focus on the multimodal nature of the Chinese language as a key challenge in toxic content detection. While adversarial attacks and perturbation techniques are known issues in NLP, their application to Chinese and the systematic categorization of modality-specific perturbations are novel. The CNTP dataset is also a significant contribution, providing a resource for future research.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's findings have important implications.  The demonstration that current LLMs, including those specifically designed for Chinese, are vulnerable to relatively simple multimodal perturbations highlights a critical weakness in their ability to detect toxic content in a real-world setting.  The "overcorrection" issue with mitigation strategies is also significant, underscoring the need for more nuanced approaches that do not simply increase false positives. The paper also contributes to more efficient language models capable of detecting toxic content.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem and its importance.
*   **Comprehensive Taxonomy:** The proposed taxonomy is well-structured and provides a useful framework for understanding different perturbation techniques.
*   **Dataset Contribution:** The CNTP dataset is a valuable resource for researchers in this area.
*   **Systematic Evaluation:** The evaluation is thorough, benchmarking multiple LLMs and exploring different mitigation strategies.
*   **Interesting Findings:** The paper's findings are significant and raise important questions about the robustness of current LLMs in Chinese toxic content detection.
*   **Clearly written:** The article clearly explains the different terms of the project.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Mitigation Exploration:** While the paper explores ICL and SFT, it doesn't delve into other more advanced mitigation techniques, such as adversarial training or more sophisticated data augmentation methods. The exploration of a single mitigation technique and only up to a very limited number of trials weakens this portion of the research.
*   **Dataset Scope:** The dataset focuses primarily on textual perturbations. As the authors acknowledge, it could be expanded to include other multimodal aspects, such as the interplay between text and images, which is increasingly relevant in online platforms.
*   **Overcorrection explanation:** More information about the "Overcorrection" issue could be explored. While they discuss that the small samples cause the issue, further exploration into the root cause may improve the research.
*   **Real-World Relevance:** While the perturbations are "widely used and comprehensible on Chinese social platforms" and "have high ratios to confuse LLMs", further information to back that claim up may strengthen that claim and provide more real-world relevance.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence future research in Chinese toxic content detection and multilingual NLP more broadly.  The CNTP dataset could become a standard benchmark, and the identified challenges will likely motivate the development of more robust and nuanced models. The study may also stimulate work on more effective mitigation strategies that avoid the "overcorrection" problem.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper. It makes a novel contribution by highlighting the modality-specific challenges of toxic Chinese detection and provides a valuable dataset and benchmark. The research provides compelling findings and identifies areas for future investigation. However, the limited exploration of mitigation strategies and the scope of the dataset prevent it from achieving a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Fewer Hallucinations, More Verification: A Three-Stage LLM-Based Framework for ASR Error Correction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24347v1)
- **Authors**: Yangui Fang, Baixu Cheng, Jing Peng, Xu Li, Yu Xi, Chengwei Zhang, Guohui Zhong
- **Abstract**: Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) error correction aims to correct recognition errors while preserving accurate text. Although traditional approaches demonstrate moderate effectiveness, LLMs offer a paradigm that eliminates the need for training and labeled data. However, directly using LLMs will encounter hallucinations problem, which may lead to the modification of the correct text. To address this problem, we propose the Reliable LLM Correction Framework (RLLM-CF), which consists of three stages: (1) error pre-detection, (2) chain-of-thought sub-tasks iterative correction, and (3) reasoning process verification. The advantage of our method is that it does not require additional information or fine-tuning of the model, and ensures the correctness of the LLM correction under multi-pass programming. Experiments on AISHELL-1, AISHELL-2, and Librispeech show that the GPT-4o model enhanced by our framework achieves 21%, 11%, 9%, and 11.4% relative reductions in CER/WER.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Fewer Hallucinations, More Verification: A Three-Stage LLM-Based Framework for ASR Error Correction" proposes a novel framework (RLLM-CF) for correcting errors in Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) output using Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework aims to mitigate the hallucination problem that often arises when directly applying LLMs to this task, where the LLM modifies correct text unnecessarily. RLLM-CF consists of three stages: (1) error pre-detection, (2) chain-of-thought sub-tasks iterative correction, and (3) reasoning process verification. The key innovation is the structured approach that guides the LLM's correction process and verifies its outputs, ensuring higher accuracy and reliability. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their framework on AISHELL-1, AISHELL-2, and Librispeech datasets, showing significant reductions in CER/WER when using GPT-4o with RLLM-CF. Unlike other LLM-based ASR correction methods, RLLM-CF does not require fine-tuning or additional information, leveraging the general knowledge of the LLM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the structured, three-stage approach to LLM-based ASR error correction. While using LLMs for ASR correction is not entirely new, the RLLM-CF framework addresses a critical problem (hallucinations) with a systematic solution that combines error pre-detection, iterative refinement through chain-of-thought, and verification of the LLM's reasoning. This contrasts with previous approaches that often rely on direct LLM application or fine-tuning, which can be resource-intensive or domain-specific. The emphasis on not fine-tuning and relying on the general LLM knowledge is also a key aspect of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in applying LLMs to ASR error correction. Hallucinations can severely limit the usefulness of LLMs in this domain. The proposed RLLM-CF offers a promising solution that can make LLMs more reliable for ASR error correction in real-world scenarios.  The experimental results, particularly the substantial CER/WER reductions achieved across multiple datasets, support the practical significance of the work.  Moreover, the ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of each component of the RLLM-CF framework. This contributes to the understanding of how LLMs can be effectively used for this specific application. The framework also facilitates the use of a general LLM without resource-intensive fine-tuning for different tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the hallucination problem in LLM-based ASR error correction.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** RLLM-CF is well-structured and easy to understand.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental results are strong and cover diverse datasets (Chinese and English).
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study effectively demonstrates the contribution of each component of the framework.
    *   **No Fine-tuning Needed:**  A key advantage is the avoidance of fine-tuning, making the approach more practical and widely applicable.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation of the Model:** The analysis of noun recall provides additional evidence of the model's efficacy beyond simple CER/WER metrics.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Cost Analysis:** While the paper mentions cost tokens, a more in-depth analysis of the computational cost associated with the framework (e.g., latency) would be beneficial. This is particularly relevant considering the iterative nature of the correction process.  This part should be emphasized and compared with other methods for future research.
    *   **Limited LLM Comparison:** The primary focus is on GPT-4o and DeepSeek v2. Expanding the comparison to include other LLMs would further strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Error Analysis:** While the framework addresses hallucinations, a detailed error analysis, categorizing the types of errors that RLLM-CF still struggles with, would offer more insights and guide future improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM-based ASR error correction. The RLLM-CF framework provides a solid foundation for developing more robust and reliable systems. Other researchers can build upon this work by exploring different LLMs, optimizing the chain-of-thought prompts, or incorporating additional verification mechanisms. The insights from the ablation study can also inform the design of future error correction frameworks.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to LLM-based ASR error correction. The RLLM-CF framework effectively addresses the hallucination problem, achieving impressive results without relying on fine-tuning. While there are some limitations related to cost analysis and LLM comparisons, the strengths of the paper outweigh these weaknesses. The clear problem definition, well-defined framework, comprehensive evaluation, and potential influence on future research justify a high score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Unifying Language Agent Algorithms with Graph-based Orchestration Engine for Reproducible Agent Research
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24354v1)
- **Authors**: Qianqian Zhang, Jiajia Liao, Heting Ying, Yibo Ma, Haozhan Shen, Jingcheng Li, Peng Liu, Lu Zhang, Chunxin Fang, Kyusong Lee, Ruochen Xu, Tiancheng Zhao
- **Abstract**: Language agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in understanding, reasoning, and executing complex tasks. However, developing robust agents presents significant challenges: substantial engineering overhead, lack of standardized components, and insufficient evaluation frameworks for fair comparison. We introduce Agent Graph-based Orchestration for Reasoning and Assessment (AGORA), a flexible and extensible framework that addresses these challenges through three key contributions: (1) a modular architecture with a graph-based workflow engine, efficient memory management, and clean component abstraction; (2) a comprehensive suite of reusable agent algorithms implementing state-of-the-art reasoning approaches; and (3) a rigorous evaluation framework enabling systematic comparison across multiple dimensions. Through extensive experiments on mathematical reasoning and multimodal tasks, we evaluate various agent algorithms across different LLMs, revealing important insights about their relative strengths and applicability. Our results demonstrate that while sophisticated reasoning approaches can enhance agent capabilities, simpler methods like Chain-of-Thought often exhibit robust performance with significantly lower computational overhead. AGORA not only simplifies language agent development but also establishes a foundation for reproducible agent research through standardized evaluation protocols.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AGORA, a framework designed to unify language agent algorithms through a graph-based orchestration engine. It aims to simplify the development and evaluation of language agents by providing a modular architecture, a suite of reusable agent algorithms, and a rigorous evaluation framework. The paper evaluates different agent algorithms (CoT, SC-CoT, ReAct, etc.) with various LLMs on mathematical and multimodal reasoning tasks. The results suggest that simpler methods like Chain-of-Thought (CoT) often perform robustly with lower computational overhead and that the effectiveness of different agent algorithms varies depending on model size.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** AGORA tackles key challenges in language agent research: engineering complexity, lack of standardized components, and inconsistent evaluation. Providing a unified environment for development, orchestration, and assessment is valuable.
    *   **Modular Architecture:** The graph-based workflow engine and modular agent algorithm support enhance reusability, extensibility, and experimentation. This makes it easier for researchers to compare and contrast different approaches.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation of various agent algorithms across different LLMs and tasks, revealing important insights about their strengths and applicability. The comparison metrics are well-defined (accuracy, cost, token usage, pass rate).
    *   **Reproducibility:** The framework and evaluation setup promote reproducible research, addressing a crucial need in the field.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The findings regarding the effectiveness of simple vs. complex methods, the importance of prompt design, and the influence of model size provide actionable guidance for researchers and practitioners.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Individual Algorithms:** While the *framework* is novel, the *algorithms* implemented within it (CoT, ReAct, ToT) are not. The paper enhances a few of the existing algorithms but does not fundamentally introduce completely new reasoning methods. The focus is on *integrating* and *evaluating* existing algorithms within a unified platform.
    *   **Dependency on Existing LLMs:** The framework's performance is inevitably tied to the performance of the underlying LLMs. While the paper explores multiple LLMs, the dependence remains a limitation.
    *   **Evaluation Scope:** While the evaluation covers mathematical and multimodal tasks, expanding the evaluation to real-world tasks and more complex scenarios would be beneficial.
    *   **Cost Metric Imperfections**: The cost analysis is limited by only considering direct token consumption costs and not computational cost.
    *   **Description Clarity:** Certain sections, such as V* parameter settings, could benefit from further clarification for better reproducibility.

*   **Significance:**
    *   AGORA contributes to the field by providing a standardized platform to accelerate language agent research. Its ability to compare different reasoning strategies can greatly help to identify more efficient and performant approaches.
    *   The insights about simpler agent algorithms (CoT) outperforming more complex algorithms in many situations are significant, challenging the assumption that complexity always translates to better performance. This can encourage a more resource-conscious approach to agent design.
    *   The evaluation metrics offer a template for standardized comparison. The framework provides a solid foundation for future research and development in language agent technology.

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty resides in the **integrated framework** and the **systematic evaluation methodology**. The individual algorithms are not new, but their combination, standardization, and rigorous comparative analysis within AGORA represent a novel contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

A score of 7.5 is given because while AGORA doesn't introduce radically new *algorithms*, it provides a *crucial platform* for systematically comparing and contrasting existing ones. The framework addresses a significant need in the field for reproducible agent research, provides valuable insights, and promotes efficient development.  The modular design and comprehensive evaluation are clear strengths. The main drawbacks stem from dependency on existing LLMs and the lack of fundamentally new agent algorithms. The significance stems from AGORA's potential to streamline research, reduce engineering overhead, and guide the development of more effective language agent technologies, promoting both accessible and replicable results.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReCalKV: Low-Rank KV Cache Compression via Head Reordering and Offline Calibration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24357v1)
- **Authors**: Xianglong Yan, Zhiteng Li, Tianao Zhang, Linghe Kong, Yulun Zhang, Xiaokang Yang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable performance, yet their capability on long-context reasoning is often constrained by the excessive memory required to store the Key-Value (KV) cache. This makes KV cache compression an essential step toward enabling efficient long-context reasoning. Recent methods have explored reducing the hidden dimensions of the KV cache, but many introduce additional computation through projection layers or suffer from significant performance degradation under high compression ratios. To address these challenges, we propose ReCalKV, a post-training KV cache compression method that reduces the hidden dimensions of the KV cache. We develop distinct compression strategies for Keys and Values based on their different roles and varying importance in the attention mechanism. For Keys, we propose Head-wise Similarity-aware Reordering (HSR), which clusters similar heads and applies grouped SVD to the key projection matrix, reducing additional computation while preserving accuracy. For Values, we propose Offline Calibration and Matrix Fusion (OCMF) to preserve accuracy without extra computational overhead. Experiments show that ReCalKV outperforms existing low-rank compression methods, achieving high compression ratios with minimal performance loss. Code is available at: https://github.com/XIANGLONGYAN/ReCalKV.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ReCalKV paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ReCalKV, a post-training method for compressing the Key-Value (KV) cache in Large Language Models (LLMs) to reduce memory footprint during long-context inference. The approach distinguishes between Keys and Values, applying different compression strategies based on their roles in the attention mechanism.  For Keys, it uses Head-wise Similarity-aware Reordering (HSR) to group similar attention heads and then applies grouped SVD. For Values, it employs Offline Calibration and Matrix Fusion (OCMF), which involves SVD, calibration with a small dataset, and fusing the right factor with the output projection matrix to eliminate runtime reconstruction overhead. Experiments demonstrate that ReCalKV achieves good compression ratios with minimal performance loss and can be combined with quantization techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a critical problem:** KV cache compression is crucial for enabling efficient long-context reasoning in LLMs, making this work highly relevant.
    *   **Differentiated approach:**  The key novelty lies in recognizing and exploiting the asymmetry between Keys and Values in the attention mechanism, leading to tailored compression strategies. This is a significant step beyond previous low-rank compression methods.
    *   **HSR for Keys:** Grouping attention heads based on similarity before applying SVD seems like a good engineering solution for keys, reducing reconstruction overhead, and better capturing shared subspace structures.
    *   **OCMF for Values:** Using offline calibration to preserve the accuracy of Values while eliminating runtime overhead via Matrix Fusion is another strong point. OCMF intelligently minimizes approximation error.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance across different LLM families and datasets, surpassing existing low-rank compression methods under various evaluation metrics.
    *   **Orthogonality:** The fact that it's orthogonal to quantization allows further gains in compression.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and provides sufficient detail to understand the proposed methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental novelty:**  While the combination and tailored application are novel, the individual components (SVD, CKA, offline calibration) are well-established techniques.
    *   **Limited scope of social impact discussion:** While the authors touch on the potential for broader access and reduced energy consumption, the social impact section could be more thoroughly developed. It lacks specific quantification of benefits like cost savings and energy reductions, making it feel somewhat generic.
    *   **Reliance on a small calibration dataset:** While efficient, offline calibration still requires a dataset. The sensitivity of ReCalKV's performance to the size and characteristics of this dataset could be explored more thoroughly. The whitening procedure and randomly selecting WikiText-2 dataset samples as calibration data is likely sub-optimal and requires further optimization.
    *   **Limited analysis of head reordering impact:** While the paper visually demonstrates that head reordering results in adjacent heads exhibiting higher similarity, a more in-depth analysis into the impact of the head reordering (for different configurations) on the final performance/compression ratio would be beneficial. It would be more rigorous to quantify the impact of head reordering in the ablation studies with different reordering configurations.
    *   **Lack of theoretical justification:** The paper is heavily empirical and lacks any theoretical guarantees or formal analysis of the proposed methods.

*   **Significance and potential influence:**

    *   ReCalKV provides a practical and effective solution for KV cache compression. This will enable efficient deployment of LLMs, especially for long-context applications.
    *   The differentiated approach to Key and Value compression could influence future research in this area.
    *   The techniques are relatively easy to implement and integrate with existing LLM frameworks.
    *   The focus on minimizing reconstruction overhead addresses a key bottleneck in existing compression methods.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LLM compression. While the individual components of ReCalKV aren't revolutionary, the way they are combined and adapted to the specific characteristics of Keys and Values in LLMs is novel and effective. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating that ReCalKV consistently outperforms existing methods.

The weaknesses, such as the incremental novelty and reliance on a small calibration dataset, prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. Also, a lot of the benefits come from engineering wins. The scope of the social impact discussion is weak.

Therefore, a score of 7 reflects a valuable contribution with practical significance and the potential to influence future research in KV cache compression.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Interpreting Large Text-to-Image Diffusion Models with Dictionary Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24360v1)
- **Authors**: Stepan Shabalin, Ayush Panda, Dmitrii Kharlapenko, Abdur Raheem Ali, Yixiong Hao, Arthur Conmy
- **Abstract**: Sparse autoencoders are a promising new approach for decomposing language model activations for interpretation and control. They have been applied successfully to vision transformer image encoders and to small-scale diffusion models. Inference-Time Decomposition of Activations (ITDA) is a recently proposed variant of dictionary learning that takes the dictionary to be a set of data points from the activation distribution and reconstructs them with gradient pursuit. We apply Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) and ITDA to a large text-to-image diffusion model, Flux 1, and consider the interpretability of embeddings of both by introducing a visual automated interpretation pipeline. We find that SAEs accurately reconstruct residual stream embeddings and beat MLP neurons on interpretability. We are able to use SAE features to steer image generation through activation addition. We find that ITDA has comparable interpretability to SAEs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the interpretability of large text-to-image diffusion models (specifically FLUX.1) using sparse autoencoders (SAEs) and a new dictionary learning technique called Inference-Time Decomposition of Activations (ITDA). The authors evaluate the feasibility and scalability of these methods for decomposing and interpreting hidden representations within the model. They train SAEs and ITDAs on different layers of FLUX.1, develop a visual auto-interpretation pipeline, and demonstrate steering of image generation by manipulating SAE features.  The key findings are that SAEs and ITDAs can effectively reconstruct residual stream embeddings, they outperform MLP neurons in terms of interpretability, and SAE features can be used to steer image generation. ITDA offers comparable interpretability to SAEs with potential efficiency advantages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The application of SAEs and ITDA to large-scale text-to-image diffusion models like FLUX.1 represents a significant step forward.  While SAEs have been used in other contexts (language models, smaller diffusion models), scaling them to this setting and exploring their use with the specific architecture of FLUX.1 presents new challenges and insights. The visual auto-interpretation pipeline, while built upon existing ideas, is adapted for the unique characteristics of diffusion models. Comparing ITDA to SAEs is also a novel contribution.
* **Significance:**  Improving the interpretability of text-to-image models is crucial for understanding their internal representations, controlling their behavior, and addressing issues like memorization and bias. The ability to steer image generation through feature manipulation has potential for creative applications and for improving model transparency. The finding that localized feature spaces constrain steering is an important empirical observation. Also, they have identified important differences in the qualities of SAE vs. ITDA learned features with manual examination (SAE is more interpretable and concrete) that goes beyond automated metrics.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Methodology:** The paper outlines a well-defined methodology for training and evaluating SAEs and ITDAs.
    * **Thorough Experiments:** The authors conduct a range of experiments, comparing different settings of k and d/n, exploring various layers of the network, and evaluating reconstruction quality and interpretability.
    * **Visualizations:** The paper includes numerous visualizations (e.g., maximum activating examples, spectral analysis) that aid in understanding the results.
    * **Addressing Challenges:** The authors identify and address challenges specific to the MMDiT architecture of FLUX.1, such as the importance of PCA normalization.
    * **Discussion of Limitations:**  The paper acknowledges limitations, such as the reliance on automated interpretability metrics and the potential for biases in the auto-interpretation pipeline. The paper also explicitly mentions biases of the autointerp pipeline, for example, simple characteristics like color.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Automated Metrics:** While the automated interpretability pipeline is valuable, the reliance on these metrics can be problematic. As the authors acknowledge, the metrics may not fully capture the nuances of interpretability or distinguish between abstract and semantic concepts.
    * **Constrained Steering:**  The constrained nature of steering, requiring the initial prompt to align with the steered features, limits the practical applicability of this technique. Although this limitation is important for understanding of the latent space.
    * **Limited Comparison:** The paper shows ITDA results on reconstruction quality, but does not provide ITDA comparisons on max activating examples.
    * **Complexity of FLUX.1:** While a strength is the applicability of this technique to a state-of-the-art model, the specifics of FLUX.1 architecture might make the findings difficult to generalize to other diffusion models.  The MMDiT architecture with AdaLN may introduce unique challenges.
* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    * **Inspiring further work on interpreting diffusion models:** The paper demonstrates a promising approach for decomposing and understanding the internal representations of these models.
    * **Guiding the development of steering techniques:** The insights into localized feature spaces can inform the design of more effective steering methods.
    * **Motivating the development of improved interpretability metrics:** The limitations of existing metrics highlight the need for more sophisticated measures of interpretability.
    * **Providing a benchmark for future research:** The paper provides a strong baseline for future work on interpreting FLUX.1 and similar models.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by scaling SAEs and introducing ITDA to a large-scale text-to-image diffusion model. The techniques offer promise for improving the interpretability and controllability of these models. However, limitations regarding the interpretability metrics, constrained steering capabilities, the limited comparative results for ITDA and potential generalizability issues prevent a higher score. The experiments are thorough, and the paper addresses relevant challenges. The findings provide a solid foundation for future research in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Knowing Before Saying: LLM Representations Encode Information About Chain-of-Thought Success Before Completion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24362v1)
- **Authors**: Anum Afzal, Florian Matthes, Gal Chechik, Yftah Ziser
- **Abstract**: We investigate whether the success of a zero-shot Chain-of-Thought (CoT) process can be predicted before completion. We discover that a probing classifier, based on LLM representations, performs well \emph{even before a single token is generated}, suggesting that crucial information about the reasoning process is already present in the initial steps representations. In contrast, a strong BERT-based baseline, which relies solely on the generated tokens, performs worse, likely because it depends on shallow linguistic cues rather than deeper reasoning dynamics. Surprisingly, using later reasoning steps does not always improve classification. When additional context is unhelpful, earlier representations resemble later ones more, suggesting LLMs encode key information early. This implies reasoning can often stop early without loss. To test this, we conduct early stopping experiments, showing that truncating CoT reasoning still improves performance over not using CoT at all, though a gap remains compared to full reasoning. However, approaches like supervised learning or reinforcement learning designed to shorten CoT chains could leverage our classifier's guidance to identify when early stopping is effective. Our findings provide insights that may support such methods, helping to optimize CoT's efficiency while preserving its benefits.\footnote{Code and data is available at \href{https://github.com/anum94/CoTpred}{\texttt{github.com/anum94/CoTpred}}.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, assigning a novelty/significance score with detailed justification.

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether it's possible to predict the success of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning *before* the LLM completes the entire reasoning process. The central idea is that LLMs might "know" if they'll reach the correct answer early on. The authors train probing classifiers on internal LLM representations (hidden states) at different points during CoT generation. They find that these probes can predict CoT success even *before* any tokens are generated, significantly outperforming a BERT-based baseline that only uses the generated text.  Further experiments explore how prediction accuracy changes as the CoT process unfolds and attempt zero-shot early stopping to save computation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of predicting CoT success from LLM internals *before* token generation is reasonably novel. While probing LLMs is a well-established technique, applying it specifically to CoT success prediction and focusing on the earliest stages of generation provides a unique angle. The comparison to a BERT baseline is also a good way to show that the *internal states* of the LLM are capturing something beyond what's explicitly present in the text.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential for several significant implications:

    *   **Efficiency:** If CoT success can be predicted early, it opens avenues for early stopping, reducing computational costs. The paper takes initial steps in this direction but, zero-shot early-stopping interventions only show marginal improvement. More sophisticated learning methods that leverage the classifier are needed to truly unlock this.
    *   **Understanding CoT:** The findings shed light on how LLMs perform CoT reasoning. The fact that prediction is possible before token generation suggests that the initial prompt activates relevant knowledge and reasoning processes within the LLM itself, rather than solely relying on the sequential generation of steps.
    *   **Annotation Improvement:**  Knowing when CoT is likely to fail from the start, could also focus annotation efforts on specifically fixing prompts where success is most needed.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Research Question:** The paper focuses on a well-defined and important question.
    *   **Solid Methodology:** The use of probing classifiers, comparison to BERT, and experiments with early stopping provides a strong methodological framework. The inclusion of SVCCA also helps corroborate the probing results.
    *   **Extensive Datasets:** Using a variety of math problem datasets gives the findings more generalizability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Zero-shot Early Stopping Limitations:** The zero-shot early stopping experiments are the weakest part of the paper. The fact that merely prompting the LLM to stop and provide an answer doesn't consistently improve performance points to the need for more sophisticated methods of leveraging the success prediction.
    *   **Limited Explanations:** While the paper identifies *that* LLM representations encode information, it doesn't delve deeply into *what* specific information is encoded. More analysis of which neurons or features are most predictive would be valuable.
    *   **Human vs. Machine annotation:** the lack of human annotation across all datasets, and reliance on LLM-generated correctness labels for training, while acknowledged by the authors, is also a limitation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to stimulate further research in several directions:

    *   Development of more effective early-stopping strategies for CoT reasoning, potentially using reinforcement learning or supervised learning.
    *   Deeper investigation into the knowledge representation and reasoning processes within LLMs during CoT.
    *   Use of the success prediction signal to guide prompt engineering or fine-tuning efforts.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a novel and interesting finding about the predictive power of LLM representations in CoT reasoning. While the initial early-stopping experiments have limitations, the overall methodology is strong, and the results have the potential to significantly impact future research. The paper's strength lies in identifying an intriguing phenomenon and providing a solid foundation for further exploration.

**Score: 7**

**Rigorous Rationale:**

I assigned a score of 7 because, while the paper isn't a *groundbreaking* discovery that fundamentally shifts the field, it offers a valuable and novel contribution.  The core idea and the initial experimental results are compelling, but limitations prevent a higher score. The zero-shot stopping experiments, while a logical next step, don't yield particularly strong results.  Greater exploration of *why* the LLM can predict success or failure and what specific knowledge is being encoded at the various layers would also have strengthened the paper significantly and would've driven the score higher. I am not giving it a higher score because of the weaknesses highlighted above.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM Inference Enhanced by External Knowledge: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24377v1)
- **Authors**: Yu-Hsuan Lin, Qian-Hui Chen, Yi-Jie Cheng, Jia-Ren Zhang, Yi-Hung Liu, Liang-Yu Hsia, Yun-Nung Chen
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have enhanced natural-language reasoning. However, their limited parametric memory and susceptibility to hallucination present persistent challenges for tasks requiring accurate, context-based inference. To overcome these limitations, an increasing number of studies have proposed leveraging external knowledge to enhance LLMs. This study offers a systematic exploration of strategies for using external knowledge to enhance LLMs, beginning with a taxonomy that categorizes external knowledge into unstructured and structured data. We then focus on structured knowledge, presenting distinct taxonomies for tables and knowledge graphs (KGs), detailing their integration paradigms with LLMs, and reviewing representative methods. Our comparative analysis further highlights the trade-offs among interpretability, scalability, and performance, providing insights for developing trustworthy and generalizable knowledge-enhanced LLMs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the survey paper "LLM Inference Enhanced by External Knowledge: A Survey."

**Summary:**

This survey paper presents a systematic exploration of strategies for enhancing Large Language Model (LLM) inference through the integration of external knowledge. The paper categorizes external knowledge into unstructured and structured data, with a particular focus on structured data sources like tables and knowledge graphs (KGs). It discusses different integration paradigms, including symbolic, neural, and hybrid reasoning for tables, and loose and tight coupling strategies for KGs. The paper concludes by comparing these approaches based on their strengths, limitations, and trade-offs, aiming to provide insights for developing trustworthy and generalizable knowledge-enhanced LLMs. The paper focuses on methods that enhance LLMs during inference, emphasizing dynamic integration rather than pre-training or fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively standard survey format, which means the novelty lies primarily in the specific organization and synthesis of existing research. The taxonomy of knowledge sources (structured vs. unstructured) and the detailed breakdown of integration strategies for tables and KGs are helpful. The focus on inference-time integration is a distinguishing characteristic.

However, similar surveys on RAG and knowledge-augmented LLMs exist. The novelty of this paper depends on whether its specific angle (inference-time, structured data, and the coupling strategies presented) provides a significantly different or more detailed perspective than existing work. It doesn't appear to introduce fundamentally new concepts or techniques. The authors claim of comprehensive categorization and analysis might be an overstatement.

**Significance:** The significance stems from the practical importance of the surveyed topic. Improving LLM reasoning, reducing hallucinations, and enhancing interpretability are crucial for real-world applications.
