- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Bench4KE: Benchmarking Automated Competency Question Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24554v1)
- **Authors**: Anna Sofia Lippolis, Minh Davide Ragagni, Paolo Ciancarini, Andrea Giovanni Nuzzolese, Valentina Presutti
- **Abstract**: The availability of Large Language Models (LLMs) presents a unique opportunity to reinvigorate research on Knowledge Engineering (KE) automation, a trend already evident in recent efforts developing LLM-based methods and tools for the automatic generation of Competency Questions (CQs). However, the evaluation of these tools lacks standardisation. This undermines the methodological rigour and hinders the replication and comparison of results. To address this gap, we introduce Bench4KE, an extensible API-based benchmarking system for KE automation. Its first release focuses on evaluating tools that generate CQs automatically. CQs are natural language questions used by ontology engineers to define the functional requirements of an ontology. Bench4KE provides a curated gold standard consisting of CQ datasets from four real-world ontology projects. It uses a suite of similarity metrics to assess the quality of the CQs generated. We present a comparative analysis of four recent CQ generation systems, which are based on LLMs, establishing a baseline for future research. Bench4KE is also designed to accommodate additional KE automation tasks, such as SPARQL query generation, ontology testing and drafting. Code and datasets are publicly available under the Apache 2.0 license.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Bench4KE, an API-based benchmarking system for evaluating Knowledge Engineering (KE) automation tasks, specifically focusing on competency question (CQ) generation. The system provides a curated gold standard of CQ datasets from four real-world ontology projects and uses a suite of similarity metrics (Cosine, Jaccard, BLEU, ROUGE-L, sBERT, and LLM-based scoring) to assess the quality of generated CQs. The authors present a comparative analysis of four existing LLM-based CQ generation systems using Bench4KE, establishing a baseline for future research. The system is designed to be extensible, accommodating additional KE automation tasks like SPARQL query generation and ontology testing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of a dedicated and extensible benchmarking system specifically for KE automation tasks, with an initial focus on CQ generation. While individual components (similarity metrics, LLMs for evaluation) are not entirely novel, their integration into a comprehensive, readily usable, and reproducible framework represents a significant step forward. The curated dataset of CQs from real-world projects is also a valuable contribution. However, some existing benchmarks already tackle similar goals in broader contexts. The paper needs to emphasize better what makes this system uniquely advantageous for this specific domain.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the lack of standardization in evaluating LLM-based CQ generation tools.  The paper argues convincingly that the absence of standard evaluation methods hinders progress by undermining methodological rigor and hindering result comparison. Bench4KE offers a solution by providing a common platform and standardized metrics, promoting reproducibility and fair comparison of different approaches. This should accelerate research in the field of KE automation. It provides a clear baseline against which new tools can be measured. The adoption of LLM-based evaluation adds a semantic dimension that complements traditional metrics. The authors show real results from using their framework to test existing tools in the KE domain.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive framework:** Bench4KE integrates multiple evaluation metrics, datasets, and a modular architecture, enabling a thorough assessment of CQ generation systems.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The API-centric design, accompanied by clear documentation and a step-by-step tutorial, promotes reproducibility of experiments.
    *   **Extensibility:** The framework is designed to be extended to support additional KE automation tasks and datasets.
    *   **Real-world datasets:** The curated datasets are based on real-world ontology projects, ensuring relevance and practical applicability.
    *   **LLM integration:** The integration of LLM-based semantic scoring adds a layer of semantic understanding to the evaluation process.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited datasets:** The number of datasets and their format diversity (particularly concerning the raw data inputs) could be expanded.
    *   **LLM dependency:** Relying solely on OpenAI's GPT-4 for LLM-based scoring makes the system dependent on a specific commercial service. Support for open-source LLMs needs to be added.
    *   **Absolute Value Metrics:** The absolute similarity metrics are relatively low. More analysis on why they are that low would be beneficial. Can this be attributed to CQ generation as a hard task, to bad tool implementation or to the metrics not being appropriate?
    *   **Missing evaluation for Novelty:** The authors mention that an important point that the community requested was a score for novelty. It has not been added to the current benchmark.

*   **Potential Influence:** Bench4KE has the potential to become a valuable resource for researchers in the field of KE automation, enabling the development and evaluation of more effective and reliable tools. The system's extensibility suggests that it could evolve into a comprehensive platform for benchmarking various KE tasks. Its design promotes community contribution and development, which is crucial for its long-term success. By addressing the need for standardization, Bench4KE can accelerate the progress of KE automation research and facilitate the development of better tools for ontology engineering.

**Rationale for Score:**

Bench4KE represents a significant contribution to the field by addressing a critical need for standardization and offering a comprehensive, extensible benchmarking system for KE automation tasks. The authors convincingly demonstrate its value through a comparative analysis of existing systems and clearly outline its strengths and potential. While there are some weaknesses related to dataset size and LLM dependency, the overall impact of Bench4KE on the community is expected to be positive. Therefore, a score of 7 is assigned.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mixture-of-Experts for Personalized and Semantic-Aware Next Location Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24597v1)
- **Authors**: Shuai Liu, Ning Cao, Yile Chen, Yue Jiang, Gao Cong
- **Abstract**: Next location prediction plays a critical role in understanding human mobility patterns. However, existing approaches face two core limitations: (1) they fall short in capturing the complex, multi-functional semantics of real-world locations; and (2) they lack the capacity to model heterogeneous behavioral dynamics across diverse user groups. To tackle these challenges, we introduce NextLocMoE, a novel framework built upon large language models (LLMs) and structured around a dual-level Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) design. Our architecture comprises two specialized modules: a Location Semantics MoE that operates at the embedding level to encode rich functional semantics of locations, and a Personalized MoE embedded within the Transformer backbone to dynamically adapt to individual user mobility patterns. In addition, we incorporate a history-aware routing mechanism that leverages long-term trajectory data to enhance expert selection and ensure prediction stability. Empirical evaluations across several real-world urban datasets show that NextLocMoE achieves superior performance in terms of predictive accuracy, cross-domain generalization, and interpretability
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NextLocMoE, a novel framework for next location prediction that addresses limitations in existing approaches related to capturing complex location semantics and user behavioral heterogeneity.  NextLocMoE utilizes a dual-level Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) design, incorporating a Location Semantics MoE (to encode functional semantics of locations using LLMs and function-specific experts) and a Personalized MoE (embedded within a Transformer backbone to adapt to individual user mobility patterns). A history-aware routing mechanism is also included to enhance expert selection and prediction stability.  The authors evaluate NextLocMoE across multiple real-world urban datasets, demonstrating improvements in predictive accuracy, cross-domain generalization, and interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its dual-level MoE architecture specifically tailored for next location prediction.  While MoE is not a new concept, its application to simultaneously model location semantics *and* user behavior within an LLM framework is innovative. The history-aware routing mechanism also contributes a distinct element. Furthermore, the incorporation of LLMs both for initializing function-specific experts and as a backbone is a clever approach.
*   **Significance:** The problem of next location prediction is of practical importance in various domains. Improving accuracy and generalizability could significantly enhance applications like intelligent transportation, personalized services, and urban management. NextLocMoE's ability to handle complex location semantics and heterogeneous user behaviors represents a tangible step forward. The demonstrated cross-domain generalization is particularly valuable. The interpretability aspects, while not deeply explored in the main paper, suggest potential for understanding underlying mobility patterns, which can lead to further improvements and insights.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive experimental validation across diverse datasets.
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Incorporation of recent advancements in LLMs and MoE.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in accuracy, generalization, and efficiency (inference time).
    *   The attention to interpretability through the LLM-initialized location function categories.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper mentions significant training-time memory costs, which is a practical limitation.  More details on these costs, and potentially, a detailed analysis of scalability, would be beneficial. The paper mentions future work involves compression techniques.
    *   The interpretability analysis (case study) is limited. A more systematic approach to evaluating the interpretability of the Location Semantics MoE and Personalized MoE (e.g., through user studies or more detailed analysis of expert activations) would strengthen the paper.
    *   While improvements are shown, the absolute gains over the best baselines (e.g., Llama-Mob) are not always overwhelming, suggesting that the problem is already being tackled relatively well.
    *   The paper's reliance on pre-defined user groups and location categories could limit its adaptability to entirely novel contexts.  Exploring techniques for automatically discovering these groups/categories would be a valuable extension.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by demonstrating the effectiveness of MoE architectures for modeling complex aspects of human mobility. It could spur further research into:
    *   More sophisticated methods for expert specialization and routing in mobility prediction.
    *   Techniques for reducing the memory footprint of MoE-based LLMs.
    *   Automated methods for learning user groups and location function categories.
    *   Applications of NextLocMoE in real-world systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to next location prediction. The improvements in accuracy, generalizability, and inference time, combined with the potential for enhanced interpretability, make it a valuable contribution. While the memory cost and limited interpretability analysis are notable weaknesses, the overall significance and potential influence of the work warrant a positive assessment.

### Harnessing Large Language Models for Scientific Novelty Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24615v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Liu, Zonglin Yang, Soujanya Poria, Thanh-Son Nguyen, Erik Cambria
- **Abstract**: In an era of exponential scientific growth, identifying novel research ideas is crucial and challenging in academia. Despite potential, the lack of an appropriate benchmark dataset hinders the research of novelty detection. More importantly, simply adopting existing NLP technologies, e.g., retrieving and then cross-checking, is not a one-size-fits-all solution due to the gap between textual similarity and idea conception. In this paper, we propose to harness large language models (LLMs) for scientific novelty detection (ND), associated with two new datasets in marketing and NLP domains. To construct the considerate datasets for ND, we propose to extract closure sets of papers based on their relationship, and then summarize their main ideas based on LLMs. To capture idea conception, we propose to train a lightweight retriever by distilling the idea-level knowledge from LLMs to align ideas with similar conception, enabling efficient and accurate idea retrieval for LLM novelty detection. Experiments show our method consistently outperforms others on the proposed benchmark datasets for idea retrieval and ND tasks. Codes and data are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/NoveltyDetection-10FB/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, based on its contents:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of scientific novelty detection (ND) in the face of exponential growth in academic literature and a paradoxically declining trend in disruptive research. It identifies the lack of suitable benchmark datasets and the limitations of relying solely on text similarity as key hurdles. The authors propose harnessing large language models (LLMs) for ND and introduce two new benchmark datasets in the marketing and NLP domains, constructed with topological closure and compactness in mind. They develop an LLM-based knowledge distillation (KD) framework to train an idea retriever, aiming to bridge the gap between textual similarity and idea conception. The framework utilizes synthesized idea pairs (rephrased, partial, and incremental) generated by LLMs to align ideas with similar conceptions. Finally, they employ a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) approach for ND, using the idea retriever to identify relevant ideas and LLMs to cross-check and assess novelty.  Experiments demonstrate the method's superior performance compared to other approaches in both idea retrieval and ND tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses an Important Problem:** The paper tackles a relevant and pressing issue in modern research – the difficulty of identifying truly novel ideas amidst a vast and ever-growing body of literature. The identified paradox of declining disruptiveness, despite increased research output, adds urgency to the problem.
    *   **Novel Dataset Construction:** The paper introduces a novel approach to dataset construction, emphasizing both topological closure (completeness of related papers) and compactness (structured summaries of research ideas). The construction based on topological closure is a significant step toward ensuring completeness in the benchmarking process.
    *   **LLM-based Knowledge Distillation:** The core methodological contribution, using LLM knowledge distillation to train an idea retriever, is innovative.  Synthesizing idea pairs to capture conceptual similarity, beyond textual similarity, is a clever approach.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes extensive experiments on both idea retrieval and ND tasks, using a variety of baselines and LLM backbones.  The analysis of different types of synthesized ideas (rephrased, partial, and incremental) provides valuable insights.
    *   **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly structured. The problem formulation is precise, and the methodology is explained in detail.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependency and Potential Bias:** The reliance on LLMs for both dataset generation (synthesized ideas) and novelty detection introduces potential biases. The authors acknowledge that the LLM-generated content is not always perfect. The inherent biases in the LLM training data can subtly influence the perceived novelty of ideas.
    *   **Binary Novelty Classification:** Framing ND as a binary classification task (novel/non-novel) is a simplification of reality. Novelty is often a continuous spectrum.  While acknowledged as a limitation, this simplification could limit the practical utility of the approach. The subjective nature of "novelty" isn't thoroughly addressed.
    *   **Scalability and Generalizability:** While the paper demonstrates improvements over existing methods, questions remain about the scalability of the approach to even larger datasets and its generalizability to different research domains. The results depend on the quality of reference list.
    *   **Limited Baselines in ND:** The baselines in the Novelty Detection task, while including some notable methods, may not fully represent the most advanced approaches in related areas like content duplication detection and semantic similarity analysis in large document corpora.
    *   **The Impact on Real-World is unknown.** Even though the authors show some positive findings, they did not conduct experiments that measure the impact of this tool on real-world innovation.

*   **Significance and Potential Impact:**

    *   The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in the progress of scientific research.  If successful, methods for efficient and accurate novelty detection could significantly accelerate innovation.
    *   The LLM-based KD framework for idea retrieval offers a promising avenue for improving information retrieval systems, enabling them to capture conceptual similarity more effectively.
    *   The new benchmark datasets provide a valuable resource for researchers in the field, facilitating the development and evaluation of new ND methods.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of scientific novelty detection by tackling key challenges and proposing innovative solutions. The LLM-based KD framework and the new benchmark datasets are valuable contributions. However, the limitations related to LLM dependency, binary classification, and scalability need to be considered.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Eye of Judgement: Dissecting the Evaluation of Russian-speaking LLMs with POLLUX
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24616v1)
- **Authors**: Nikita Martynov, Anastasia Mordasheva, Dmitriy Gorbetskiy, Danil Astafurov, Ulyana Isaeva, Elina Basyrova, Sergey Skachkov, Victoria Berestova, Nikolay Ivanov, Valeriia Zanina, Alena Fenogenova
- **Abstract**: We introduce POLLUX, a comprehensive open-source benchmark designed to evaluate the generative capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in Russian. Our main contribution is a novel evaluation methodology that enhances the interpretability of LLM assessment. For each task type, we define a set of detailed criteria and develop a scoring protocol where models evaluate responses and provide justifications for their ratings. This enables transparent, criteria-driven evaluation beyond traditional resource-consuming, side-by-side human comparisons. POLLUX includes a detailed, fine-grained taxonomy of 35 task types covering diverse generative domains such as code generation, creative writing, and practical assistant use cases, totaling 2,100 manually crafted and professionally authored prompts. Each task is categorized by difficulty (easy/medium/hard), with experts constructing the dataset entirely from scratch. We also release a family of LLM-as-a-Judge (7B and 32B) evaluators trained for nuanced assessment of generative outputs. This approach provides scalable, interpretable evaluation and annotation tools for model development, effectively replacing costly and less precise human judgments.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper introduces POLLUX, a new open-source benchmark for evaluating large language models (LLMs) in Russian. It emphasizes a criteria-driven evaluation methodology, utilizing a detailed taxonomy of 35 tasks, a fine-grained scoring protocol, and trained LLM-as-a-Judge models to assess generative outputs. The goal is to enhance interpretability and scalability in LLM evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation and Novelty:**

While the paper presents a comprehensive resource for evaluating Russian-speaking LLMs, several aspects diminish its novelty and overall impact:

*   **Incremental Contribution:** The methodology builds upon existing LLM evaluation strategies (LLM-as-a-Judge), primarily adapting them to the Russian language. The core concepts of criteria-driven evaluation, detailed taxonomies, and LLM-based assessment are not fundamentally new.

*   **Limited Justification for Task Selection:** Although the paper mentions logs of LLM usage, the process of deriving the 35 task types from these logs lacks a clear, rigorous justification. What specific criteria were used to identify and categorize the tasks? How were biases in user queries addressed? Without this, the task selection appears somewhat arbitrary.

*   **Potential Data Contamination:** The paper acknowledges the importance of preventing data leakage but only mentions manual crafting and expert verification. A more detailed discussion of the steps taken to ensure that the LLM-as-a-Judge models are not inadvertently exposed to similar examples would significantly strengthen the results.

*   **Evaluation of LLM-as-a-Judge:** While the paper trains and evaluates LLM-as-a-Judge models, it does not provide sufficient evidence to show a substantial improvement over existing methods. The reported correlation coefficients (Spearman's rank correlation) require further analysis. Are the improvements statistically significant? What is the performance relative to other existing LLM-as-a-Judge methods, especially those already trained on multiple languages? What specific advantages does the POLLUX framework provide in terms of cost-effectiveness, speed, or reduced bias compared to human evaluations?

*   **Limited Discussion of LLM-as-a-Judge Biases:** The paper mentions addressing LLM biases but does not provide detailed strategies used to mitigate such biases during training and evaluation of the LLM-as-a-Judge models. How was fairness ensured in the assessment of diverse generative outputs? How was the training data balanced to prevent unfair advantages for certain tasks or model architectures?

Despite these limitations, the paper makes several valuable contributions:

*   **Resource for Russian Language LLM Evaluation:** The POLLUX benchmark provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners focused on Russian-speaking LLMs.

*   **Detailed Taxonomy and Scoring Protocol:** The fine-grained taxonomy of tasks and criteria offers a structured approach to LLM evaluation, which could be adapted to other languages.

*   **Open-Source LLM-as-a-Judge Models:** Releasing the trained LLM-as-a-Judge models is a valuable step towards democratizing LLM evaluation, although the discussion needs to include limitations and specific use cases.

**Potential Influence on the Field:**

The paper's impact on the field is likely to be moderate. While it provides a useful benchmark, its incremental nature and lack of substantial improvements over existing methods limit its significance. To increase the paper's impact, future work could focus on:

*   Providing stronger justifications for task selection.
*   Addressing concerns about data contamination in more detail.
*   Conducting more comprehensive evaluations of the LLM-as-a-Judge models, including comparisons with existing methods.
*   Mitigating biases in LLM-as-a-Judge models.
*   Demonstrating the cost-effectiveness and scalability of the POLLUX framework.

**Score: 6/10**

The paper provides a valuable resource for the Russian language LLM community but lacks significant novelty and requires further validation and refinement to achieve a substantial impact on the broader field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Benchmarking Large Language Models for Cryptanalysis and Mismatched-Generalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24621v1)
- **Authors**: Utsav Maskey, Chencheng Zhu, Usman Naseem
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed natural language understanding and generation, leading to extensive benchmarking across diverse tasks. However, cryptanalysis a critical area for data security and encryption has not yet been thoroughly explored in LLM evaluations. To address this gap, we evaluate cryptanalytic potential of state of the art LLMs on encrypted texts generated using a range of cryptographic algorithms. We introduce a novel benchmark dataset comprising diverse plain texts spanning various domains, lengths, writing styles, and topics paired with their encrypted versions. Using zero-shot and few shot settings, we assess multiple LLMs for decryption accuracy and semantic comprehension across different encryption schemes. Our findings reveal key insights into the strengths and limitations of LLMs in side-channel communication while raising concerns about their susceptibility to jailbreaking attacks. This research highlights the dual-use nature of LLMs in security contexts and contributes to the ongoing discussion on AI safety and security.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel benchmark dataset and methodology for evaluating the cryptanalysis capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The benchmark consists of diverse plain texts spanning various domains, lengths, writing styles, and topics, paired with their encrypted versions using a range of cryptographic algorithms (Caesar, Atbash, Morse, Bacon, Rail Fence, Vigenere, Playfair, RSA, AES). The authors conduct zero-shot and few-shot experiments on several state-of-the-art LLMs to assess their decryption accuracy and semantic comprehension across different encryption schemes and text complexities. The research reveals insights into the strengths and limitations of LLMs in side-channel communication and raises concerns about their susceptibility to jailbreaking attacks. The authors emphasize the dual-use nature of LLMs in security contexts and contribute to the ongoing discussion on AI safety and security. The study assesses LLM cryptanalysis abilities, focusing on vulnerabilities and susceptibility to jailbreaking attacks, and offers valuable insights into the decryption accuracy and semantic comprehension of encrypted texts across different encryption schemes.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty of the Benchmark:** The creation of a dedicated benchmark dataset for LLM cryptanalysis is a significant contribution.  Prior work in LLM evaluation has largely neglected this area, focusing instead on more general NLP tasks. The dataset's diversity, encompassing various domains, lengths, and writing styles, makes it valuable for a comprehensive evaluation of LLMs' cryptanalytic potential.
*   **Thorough Experimentation:**  The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation using a range of LLMs and encryption algorithms. The inclusion of both zero-shot and few-shot settings provides insights into the LLMs' inherent capabilities and their ability to learn from examples.
*   **Addressing a Security Gap:**  The paper directly addresses a critical security gap in the LLM evaluation landscape. As LLMs become more powerful, understanding their potential misuse for malicious activities like cryptanalysis and jailbreaking is paramount.
*   **Dual-Use Discussion:** The acknowledgement of the dual-use nature of LLMs is crucial. The paper effectively highlights the potential benefits of LLMs in security (e.g., vulnerability detection) while also acknowledging the risks of their misuse for malicious purposes.
*   **Focus on Side-Channel Mismatched Generalization:** The paper specifically targets the side-channel mismatched generalization attack, which is a relevant and potent attack vector for LLMs.
*   **Detailed Analysis of Model Performance:** The paper provides detailed explanations for the model performance in the result section, and provides insightful recommendations to avoid jailbreaking attacks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Encryption Algorithms:** While the paper includes a range of encryption algorithms, the selection is primarily limited to classical and symmetric algorithms.  The inclusion of more advanced and modern cryptographic schemes (e.g., elliptic curve cryptography, homomorphic encryption) would further strengthen the benchmark.
*   **Lack of a "Jailbreaking" Attack Section:**  While the paper mentions jailbreaking implications, there is a lack of a dedicated section in the evaluation for actual jailbreaking attempts. A demonstration of how partial comprehension of encrypted texts could be exploited for jailbreaking would significantly enhance the paper's impact.
*   **Potential Bias in Generated Texts:** The use of LLMs to generate some of the plain texts could introduce biases. Although, the authors take precaution by using two separate human-written text to avoid bias. It would be more compelling if human-generated and curated datasets were used as plain texts.
*   **Limited analysis for token inflation mitigation techniques:** There isn't further information on what can be done to overcome the token inflation, which causes LLMs to fail in decryption.
*   **Limited consideration for real-world applications:** There is limited mention on how the developed method can be leveraged in real-world application of cyber-security.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in:

1.  Introducing a dedicated benchmark and associated methodology for evaluating LLMs in the context of cryptanalysis.
2.  Highlighting the vulnerabilities and security implications of LLMs' partial comprehension of encrypted texts.
3.  Providing a systematic evaluation across diverse text types and encryption schemes.

The significance of the research stems from its contribution to:

1.  A more comprehensive understanding of LLMs' capabilities and limitations in security-related tasks.
2.  Raising awareness of the potential misuse of LLMs for malicious purposes.
3.  Informing the development of LLM safety safeguards and mitigation strategies.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a valuable benchmark and evaluation methodology for LLM cryptanalysis. While there are some limitations, the research is well-executed, addresses a critical security gap, and has the potential to influence future work in AI safety and security. The study is well written and has a significant impact in the field of cybersecurity and AI safety.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper's strengths in benchmark creation, thorough experimentation, and addressing a security gap outweigh its limitations in the scope of encryption algorithms and lack of a jailbreaking section. The potential for future research to build upon this work and further refine the benchmark justifies a relatively high score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Random Rule Forest (RRF): Interpretable Ensembles of LLM-Generated Questions for Predicting Startup Success
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24622v1)
- **Authors**: Ben Griffin, Joseph Ternasky, Fuat Alican, Yigit Ihlamur
- **Abstract**: Predicting startup success requires models that are both accurate and interpretable. We present a lightweight ensemble framework that combines YES/NO questions generated by large language models (LLMs), forming a transparent decision-making system. Each question acts as a weak heuristic, and by filtering, ranking, and aggregating them through a threshold-based voting mechanism, we construct a strong ensemble predictor. On a test set where 10% of startups are classified as successful, our approach achieves a precision rate of 50%, representing a 5x improvement over random selection, while remaining fully transparent. When we incorporate expert-guided heuristics into the generation process, performance improves further to 54% precision. These results highlight the value of combining LLM reasoning with human insight and demonstrate that simple, interpretable ensembles can support high-stakes decisions in domains such as venture capital (VC).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Random Rule Forest (RRF), a novel, lightweight, interpretable ensemble framework for predicting startup success. RRF leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate simple, interpretable YES/NO questions that serve as weak heuristics. These questions are filtered, ranked, and aggregated using a threshold-based voting mechanism to construct a transparent ensemble predictor.  The paper presents an iterative feedback-driven pipeline for refining question quality using real startup founder data and incorporates expert domain knowledge to improve question generation. Experiments show RRF achieves significantly higher precision compared to random selection in predicting startup success.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in combining LLM-generated interpretable questions into an ensemble for high-stakes prediction, emphasizing transparency and human understandability. While LLMs have been used for startup prediction and analysis, the focus on *creating an ensemble of human-interpretable rules* and the specific iterative feedback loop is relatively novel. The expert-in-the-loop component also contributes to the originality by integrating human knowledge for improved quality of questions. However, the individual components, such as ensemble methods and using LLMs for question generation, are not entirely novel.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is primarily in demonstrating a practical, interpretable alternative to black-box models for a high-stakes decision-making domain like venture capital. By providing a transparent system, RRF enables users to understand *why* a prediction is made, which is crucial for building trust and facilitating informed decision-making. The reported 5x improvement in precision over random selection is substantial in this domain, considering the low base rate of success and the significant financial implications of each investment decision.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Interpretability:** The core strength is its commitment to model interpretability. The use of simple YES/NO questions directly allows for understanding the reasoning behind the predictions.
    *   **Iterative Refinement:** The feedback-driven pipeline systematically improves question quality through data and expert inputs.
    *   **Empirical Results:** The experimental results demonstrate a clear performance improvement over a random baseline and showcase the potential of the expert-in-the-loop approach.
    *   **Practicality:** The design emphasizes practical adaptability for real-world decision-making in VC, making it a more immediately useful method than some purely theoretical approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset limitations:** The results are based on a moderately enriched subset of founder data, which might not fully generalize to datasets with lower success rates. The dependence on data quality and founder profile completeness is also a concern.
    *   **LLM dependence:** The reliance on LLMs introduces a level of stochasticity and dependence on the LLM's capabilities and biases. While GPT-40 mini is chosen, results might differ with other LLMs or different prompts.
    *   **Scope for Improvement:** The method still leaves considerable room for improvements in question generation strategies, the integration of more advanced LLM models, and further refinement of the ensemble optimization process.
    *   **Limited recall analysis**: the paper primarily focuses on increasing precision. This is understandable, but it may come at the cost of missing out on some successful start-up predictions.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence research in interpretable machine learning, particularly for domains requiring high levels of transparency and accountability. It provides a blueprint for combining the power of LLMs with the interpretability of rule-based systems. It may also influence how VC firms and other investors use AI in their decision-making processes, encouraging the adoption of more transparent and explainable models.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by effectively combining LLMs with an interpretable ensemble structure to address a high-stakes problem. While the core components are not entirely novel, the way they are integrated and refined, particularly with the iterative feedback loop and the expert-in-the-loop extension, is innovative. The empirical results and demonstration of a significant precision boost are compelling. The paper's emphasis on practicality and transparency is particularly relevant in the VC domain.

However, the limitations related to the dataset, LLM dependence, and scope for further improvement warrant a slightly lower score. The reliance on a particular LLM model and the lack of extensive analysis in more realistic settings slightly temper the potential influence.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning from Videos for 3D World: Enhancing MLLMs with 3D Vision Geometry Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24625v1)
- **Authors**: Duo Zheng, Shijia Huang, Yanyang Li, Liwei Wang
- **Abstract**: Previous research has investigated the application of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in understanding 3D scenes by interpreting them as videos. These approaches generally depend on comprehensive 3D data inputs, such as point clouds or reconstructed Bird's-Eye View (BEV) maps. In our research, we advance this field by enhancing the capability of MLLMs to understand and reason in 3D spaces directly from video data, without the need for additional 3D input. We propose a novel and efficient method, the Video-3D Geometry Large Language Model (VG LLM). Our approach employs a 3D visual geometry encoder that extracts 3D prior information from video sequences. This information is integrated with visual tokens and fed into the MLLM. Extensive experiments have shown that our method has achieved substantial improvements in various tasks related to 3D scene understanding and spatial reasoning, all directly learned from video sources. Impressively, our 4B model, which does not rely on explicit 3D data inputs, achieves competitive results compared to existing state-of-the-art methods, and even surpasses the Gemini-1.5-Pro in the VSI-Bench evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Video-3D Geometry Large Language Model (VG LLM), a method to enhance Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for 3D scene understanding directly from video data.  VG LLM uses a 3D visual geometry encoder to extract 3D prior information from video sequences, which is then integrated with visual tokens and fed into the MLLM. The authors demonstrate that this approach improves performance in 3D scene understanding and spatial reasoning tasks, achieving competitive results compared to methods relying on explicit 3D data inputs. Impressively, the 4B model size even surpasses Gemini 1.5 Pro in the VSI-Bench evalutation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating a 3D visual geometry encoder into an MLLM to extract 3D priors from video sequences is reasonably novel.  While previous work has used 3D data or engineered BEV maps for MLLMs, VG LLM is novel in its claim of being able to achieve comparable results, or even surpass it in certain evaluations, without any explicit 3D inputs. The choice of VGGT is well-motivated since it already extracts inter-frame correspondences, and is superior in 3D reconstruction tasks.

*   **Significance:** If the claims hold true (and the experimental section suggests they do), the potential impact is substantial.  The ability to perform 3D scene understanding directly from video, without reliance on potentially noisy and often unavailable 3D data, broadens the applicability of MLLMs to real-world scenarios. The method enables a pure-vision solution which is a significant advantage in many applications. Moreover, the fact that the 4B model size surpassed Gemini 1.5 Pro is extremely impressive, which shows the effectiveness and efficiency of the architecture design.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong experimental results:** The experiments demonstrate clear improvements on various 3D scene understanding and spatial reasoning tasks.  The comparison with existing methods and the ablation studies provide evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Efficient Model:** The model is efficient due to its small model size. This model can provide 3D geometric understanding without any additional 3D data, which is a significant advantage.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** the paper does a good job of analyzing results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset limitations:** The paper depends on synthetic datasets, so it is unclear how the proposed model will perform on more complex, real-world scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence the development of MLLMs for robotics, autonomous navigation, and AR/VR applications. The emphasis on incorporating geometric priors from video streams could become a standard practice in these domains.

*   **Areas for Improvement:** The study is fairly strong, and the improvements are evident on VSI-Bench. More analysis on the failures of the current approach in different tasks can be helpful for improvements.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach for enhancing MLLMs with 3D geometric understanding from video. The results are compelling, demonstrating substantial improvements on several tasks and even surpassing a proprietary model.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### The Hallucination Dilemma: Factuality-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24630v1)
- **Authors**: Junyi Li, Hwee Tou Ng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have significantly advanced in reasoning tasks through reinforcement learning (RL) optimization, achieving impressive capabilities across various challenging benchmarks. However, our empirical analysis reveals a critical drawback: reasoning-oriented RL fine-tuning significantly increases the prevalence of hallucinations. We theoretically analyze the RL training dynamics, identifying high-variance gradient, entropy-induced randomness, and susceptibility to spurious local optima as key factors leading to hallucinations. To address this drawback, we propose Factuality-aware Step-wise Policy Optimization (FSPO), an innovative RL fine-tuning algorithm incorporating explicit factuality verification at each reasoning step. FSPO leverages automated verification against given evidence to dynamically adjust token-level advantage values, incentivizing factual correctness throughout the reasoning process. Experiments across mathematical reasoning and hallucination benchmarks using Qwen2.5 and Llama models demonstrate that FSPO effectively reduces hallucinations while enhancing reasoning accuracy, substantially improving both reliability and performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "The Hallucination Dilemma: Factuality-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Large Reasoning Models" investigates how reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning, intended to improve reasoning abilities in large language models (LLMs), can inadvertently increase factual inaccuracies (hallucinations).  The authors theoretically analyze the RL training dynamics and identify high-variance gradients, entropy-induced randomness, and susceptibility to spurious local optima as contributing factors. To combat this, they propose Factuality-aware Step-wise Policy Optimization (FSPO). FSPO incorporates explicit factuality verification at each step of the reasoning process using automated evidence verification to dynamically adjust token-level advantage values during RL training. The experiments, conducted with Qwen2.5 and Llama models on mathematical reasoning and hallucination benchmarks, demonstrate that FSPO reduces hallucinations and enhances reasoning accuracy compared to standard RL fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel approach, FSPO, which directly addresses the problem of increased hallucinations stemming from RL-based fine-tuning for reasoning. While the individual components – RL fine-tuning, factuality verification, and advantage adjustment – are not entirely new, the combination and application to this specific problem are innovative. The theoretical analysis, though based on established RL principles, provides a clear and plausible explanation for the observed phenomenon. However, the idea of incorporating factuality checks during reasoning is not entirely new, but the implementation within an RL framework is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they highlight a potentially serious pitfall of RL fine-tuning for reasoning-oriented LLMs. Simply optimizing for correct final answers can inadvertently degrade factual correctness. The FSPO method presents a practical solution to mitigate this issue, improving the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs. The results demonstrated in the experimental evaluations present an important step in advancing toward reliable reasoning models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear and well-structured analysis of the problem.
    *   The theoretical justification for FSPO is sound.
    *   The proposed method is well-motivated and intuitively appealing.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, showing consistent improvements in both reasoning accuracy and hallucination reduction across different models and benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies demonstrate the importance of each component of FSPO.
    *   Detailed analysis of different types of hallucination and efforts in improving the results for all types of errors

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental setup, while strong, could benefit from more diverse datasets and larger model sizes. While the authors explore base and instruct models, it would be nice to see a range of sizes with corresponding datasets with each of the architectures.
    *   The factuality verification component relies on an automated verifier (HHEM-2.1), which is itself imperfect and could introduce biases or errors. The impact of verifier accuracy on FSPO's performance should be explored further. There may be limitations when evaluating for new environments, with questions or environments where information or data is limited.
    *   The method may be more computationally expensive than standard RL fine-tuning due to the added factuality verification step, the computational overhead is not explicitly discussed.
    *   The paper does not explore the potential of FSPO for other tasks, such as code generation or dialogue systems.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in RL fine-tuning for LLMs. It underscores the importance of considering factuality when optimizing reasoning abilities and provides a concrete framework for addressing this challenge. The FSPO algorithm could become a standard technique for training reliable reasoning models. Furthermore, the theoretical analysis encourages future research to investigate and mitigate the causes of hallucinations during RL training.

*   **Rigorous Justification:** The paper's strength resides in its thorough examination of the problem and the effectiveness of the suggested solution. The theoretical framework and the experimental findings both confirm the effectiveness of FSPO. Even if, other techniques might prove superior in the future, this work offers a foundational starting point and a valuable illustration of the trade-offs involved in RL fine-tuning for reasoning. It directly contributes to the community's capacity to develop more trustworthy and reliable models by focusing on factuality.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** This paper makes a substantial contribution to the field. It identifies a crucial issue with a widely used technique (RL fine-tuning), provides a compelling theoretical explanation, and introduces a practical solution (FSPO) that demonstrates significant improvements. While there are some limitations in terms of dataset diversity and computational cost analysis, the overall impact of the paper is high, making it a strong candidate for influencing future research and development in reliable LLM training.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Disentangling Language and Culture for Evaluating Multilingual Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24635v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahao Ying, Wei Tang, Yiran Zhao, Yixin Cao, Yu Rong, Wenxuan Zhang
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces a Dual Evaluation Framework to comprehensively assess the multilingual capabilities of LLMs. By decomposing the evaluation along the dimensions of linguistic medium and cultural context, this framework enables a nuanced analysis of LLMs' ability to process questions within both native and cross-cultural contexts cross-lingually. Extensive evaluations are conducted on a wide range of models, revealing a notable "CulturalLinguistic Synergy" phenomenon, where models exhibit better performance when questions are culturally aligned with the language. This phenomenon is further explored through interpretability probing, which shows that a higher proportion of specific neurons are activated in a language's cultural context. This activation proportion could serve as a potential indicator for evaluating multilingual performance during model training. Our findings challenge the prevailing notion that LLMs, primarily trained on English data, perform uniformly across languages and highlight the necessity of culturally and linguistically model evaluations. Our code can be found at https://yingjiahao14. github.io/Dual-Evaluation/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "Dual Evaluation Framework" to assess multilingual LLMs more comprehensively.  It decomposes the evaluation along two dimensions: linguistic medium (language used) and cultural context (regional/cultural knowledge tested). This framework generates four evaluation scenarios for each question template: native cultural-linguistic alignment, cross-lingual understanding, cross-cultural ability, and native English. The authors conduct extensive evaluations across a range of models and languages, identifying a "Cultural-Linguistic Synergy" phenomenon where models perform better when questions align culturally with the language. Interpretability probing reveals that a higher proportion of specific neurons activate in a language's cultural context, suggesting a link between neuron activation and multilingual performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its structured decoupling of language and culture in multilingual evaluation.  Existing works often evaluate translation or focus on culture-specific benchmarks independently. The Dual Evaluation Framework is a valuable contribution as it provides a more nuanced approach to understanding how LLMs handle questions across these dimensions. The identification of the "Cultural-Linguistic Synergy" is a novel and interesting finding. The attempt to link this synergy to specific neuron activation is a promising avenue for future research, although the current evidence is preliminary.

*   **Significance:**  The paper's significance stems from highlighting the limitations of solely relying on translated English-centric benchmarks for multilingual LLM evaluation. The "Cultural-Linguistic Synergy" emphasizes the importance of considering cultural context and language alignment. The findings have implications for model training, suggesting that focusing on better alignment with cultural contexts could improve performance. The neuron activation analysis offers a potential interpretable metric for gauging multilingual capabilities during model training.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The framework is well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   The experimental setup is reasonably comprehensive, covering a decent range of models and languages.
    *   The interpretability probing provides valuable insights into the underlying mechanisms of the Cultural-Linguistic Synergy.
    *   The paper clearly articulates its findings and their implications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the neuron activation analysis is promising, the link between specific neurons and language/culture is still somewhat speculative. The authors acknowledge that there's no definitive way to measure the importance of neurons and use an indirect approach based on total number of neurons activated which could be improved with a more precise method.
    *   The dataset extension using GPT-4 could introduce biases, even with human evaluation.
    *   The analysis focuses on relatively high-resource languages; extending to very low-resource languages could reveal different patterns.
    *   The paper doesn't thoroughly explore alternative explanations for the observed "Synergy," such as inherent differences in question difficulty between languages, even after careful dataset construction.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in multilingual LLM evaluation. The Dual Evaluation Framework could become a standard approach, encouraging more culturally and linguistically sensitive evaluations. The neuron activation analysis could inspire further interpretability studies, leading to better understanding and control of multilingual models.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper offers a valuable contribution by formally decoupling language and culture dimensions in the evaluation of multilingual LLMs. This approach addresses a significant limitation in current evaluation methods and introduces a promising new direction for future research. The discovery of Cultural-Linguistic Synergy and subsequent probing of neuron activation have implications for model training and understanding. While the neuron activation analysis is still relatively preliminary, the study provides valuable insights and highlights avenues for further investigation. Considering both the novelty and significance of the findings, the paper is a valuable contribution to the field.

### Efficient Text Encoders for Labor Market Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24640v1)
- **Authors**: Jens-Joris Decorte, Jeroen Van Hautte, Chris Develder, Thomas Demeester
- **Abstract**: Labor market analysis relies on extracting insights from job advertisements, which provide valuable yet unstructured information on job titles and corresponding skill requirements. While state-of-the-art methods for skill extraction achieve strong performance, they depend on large language models (LLMs), which are computationally expensive and slow. In this paper, we propose \textbf{ConTeXT-match}, a novel contrastive learning approach with token-level attention that is well-suited for the extreme multi-label classification task of skill classification. \textbf{ConTeXT-match} significantly improves skill extraction efficiency and performance, achieving state-of-the-art results with a lightweight bi-encoder model. To support robust evaluation, we introduce \textbf{Skill-XL}, a new benchmark with exhaustive, sentence-level skill annotations that explicitly address the redundancy in the large label space. Finally, we present \textbf{JobBERT V2}, an improved job title normalization model that leverages extracted skills to produce high-quality job title representations. Experiments demonstrate that our models are efficient, accurate, and scalable, making them ideal for large-scale, real-time labor market analysis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical need for efficient and scalable methods for labor market analysis, focusing on skill extraction and job title normalization from job advertisements. The authors propose ConTeXT-match, a novel contrastive learning approach with token-level attention for skill classification. They introduce Skill-XL, a new benchmark for evaluating skill extraction models that explicitly addresses redundancy in skill labels. Finally, they present JobBERT V2, an improved job title normalization model leveraging extracted skills. Experimental results demonstrate that their models are efficient, accurate, and scalable, making them suitable for large-scale, real-time labor market analysis. The paper also claims state-of-the-art performance with lightweight bi-encoder models, surpassing LLM-based methods in efficiency and achieving comparable results in accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel contributions:
    *   **ConTeXT-match:** The introduction of token-level attention in a contrastive learning framework for skill extraction is a significant methodological contribution. It addresses the limitations of sentence-level averaging by allowing the model to focus on the most relevant parts of a sentence for skill identification. This adds explainability as well.
    *   **Skill-XL Benchmark:** The creation of Skill-XL is a valuable contribution. Addressing the limitations of existing benchmarks by providing exhaustive skill annotations and explicitly coding redundancy in the labels enhances the robustness and accuracy of skill extraction model evaluation. The inter-annotator agreement, while reasonable, could be a potential area for improvement.

    *   **JobBERT V2:** Improving job title normalization with explicit extracted skills is a solid contribution. This makes JobBERT v2 able to match corresponding skills and job titles.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a highly relevant and practically important problem. Efficient labor market analysis has significant implications for workforce planning, policymaking, and HR applications. The proposed models and benchmark offer tangible benefits:
    *   **Scalability and Efficiency:** The emphasis on lightweight models addresses the limitations of computationally expensive LLMs, making real-time analysis feasible.
    *   **Improved Accuracy:** The claim of state-of-the-art results with ConTeXT-match, especially surpassing previous encoder-based methods, is substantial. However, the significance depends on the specific performance gains and the robustness of the evaluation across different datasets and scenarios.
    *   **Transparency and Interpretability:** The explainable component via token level extraction helps human-machine collaboration with the results.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem statement and clear objectives.
    *   Methodological innovation with ConTeXT-match.
    *   Creation of a valuable new benchmark dataset.
    *   Experimental validation demonstrating efficiency and accuracy.
    *   Open-sourcing of models and datasets promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on synthetic training data might limit the model's generalization to real-world job advertisements with diverse language and styles. While the synthetic data strategy is common, its impact on real-world performance needs further scrutiny.
    *   While the paper claims state-of-the-art performance, a more detailed analysis of the trade-offs between the proposed models and LLM-based approaches (considering both accuracy and efficiency) would strengthen the argument.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Providing a practical and efficient solution for large-scale labor market analysis.
    *   Setting a new benchmark for skill extraction model evaluation.
    *   Inspiring further research on contrastive learning and token-level attention mechanisms for text classification.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the ConTeXT-match approach, the significance of the problem being addressed, the creation of the Skill-XL benchmark, and the reported state-of-the-art results, the paper represents a valuable contribution to the field. However, the limitations related to synthetic data and the need for a more detailed comparison with LLM-based methods prevent a higher score. The impact of improved efficiency cannot be overstated, leading to an increase in accessibility of the model.

### Adaptable Cardiovascular Disease Risk Prediction from Heterogeneous Data using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24655v1)
- **Authors**: Frederike Lübeck, Jonas Wildberger, Frederik Träuble, Maximilian Mordig, Sergios Gatidis, Andreas Krause, Bernhard Schölkopf
- **Abstract**: Cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk prediction models are essential for identifying high-risk individuals and guiding preventive actions. However, existing models struggle with the challenges of real-world clinical practice as they oversimplify patient profiles, rely on rigid input schemas, and are sensitive to distribution shifts. We developed AdaCVD, an adaptable CVD risk prediction framework built on large language models extensively fine-tuned on over half a million participants from the UK Biobank. In benchmark comparisons, AdaCVD surpasses established risk scores and standard machine learning approaches, achieving state-of-the-art performance. Crucially, for the first time, it addresses key clinical challenges across three dimensions: it flexibly incorporates comprehensive yet variable patient information; it seamlessly integrates both structured data and unstructured text; and it rapidly adapts to new patient populations using minimal additional data. In stratified analyses, it demonstrates robust performance across demographic, socioeconomic, and clinical subgroups, including underrepresented cohorts. AdaCVD offers a promising path toward more flexible, AI-driven clinical decision support tools suited to the realities of heterogeneous and dynamic healthcare environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ADACVD, an adaptable cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk prediction framework built upon large language models (LLMs).  It addresses limitations of existing risk prediction models by:

1.  **Handling Heterogeneous Data:** ADACVD flexibly incorporates variable patient information, including structured data and unstructured text (clinical notes).
2.  **Adapting to Distribution Shifts:** It quickly adapts to new patient populations with minimal additional data, overcoming generalizability issues.
3.  **Achieving State-of-the-Art Performance:** Through extensive fine-tuning on UK Biobank data, ADACVD surpasses traditional risk scores and standard machine learning approaches in benchmark comparisons.  It also exhibits robust performance across diverse demographic and clinical subgroups.
The paper demonstrates the model's adaptability across different data formats and populations via transfer learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the application of LLMs to CVD risk prediction while explicitly addressing the challenges of real-world clinical data:  heterogeneity, unstructured inputs, and distribution shifts. While LLMs have been used in medical contexts before, the comprehensive approach to adaptability is a significant contribution.  Specifically, the demonstrated ability to fine-tune from structured data to unstructured clinical notes with high data efficiency is noteworthy. The three adaptation directions (varying patient information, unstructured data, and distribution shifts) are well-defined and addressed.
*   **Significance:** CVD risk prediction is a crucial area, and improved models have the potential to significantly impact public health by enabling more effective preventative interventions. ADACVD’s flexibility makes it potentially more practical and deployable in diverse clinical settings compared to traditional, rigid models. The robust performance across various subgroups helps to mitigate bias concerns. This adaptability tackles major real-world limitations currently plaguing clinical decision support systems. The model could facilitate more personalized risk assessment.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation against relevant baselines, across multiple dimensions of adaptability, and in various subgroups. This is essential to demonstrating the model's robustness.
    *   **Data Efficiency:** The model's ability to adapt to new data formats or populations with limited additional training data (data-efficient transfer learning) is a key strength, making it practical for real-world implementation.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The challenges of using predictive models in the real world are clearly articulated.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide a link to the source code.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Clinical Notes:** The use of LLMs to generate synthetic clinical notes is a limitation. While the authors argue that these synthetic notes mimic the characteristics of real clinical data, validation with real-world clinical notes would significantly strengthen the results.  The dependence on LLMs for data generation introduces a potential source of bias and limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Geographic Diversity:** The training data is primarily from the UK Biobank. Although evaluation on the Framingham cohort is included, further validation across diverse healthcare systems (different countries, hospitals with different data collection standards) is needed. It is also limited to one cohort per geographical area: US and UK.
    *   **Explainability:**  While the paper demonstrates strong predictive performance, the explainability of LLM-based risk predictions is not explicitly addressed. Understanding the reasoning behind the model's predictions is crucial for clinical acceptance and trust.
    *   **Focus on Prediction Over Action:** The paper focuses on risk *prediction*.  An essential next step is to investigate how ADACVD’s predictions can translate into *actionable* insights and influence clinical decision-making, ultimately improving patient outcomes.
    *   **Evaluation is entirely retrospective.** There are no real-world clinical trials or even piloting.
    *   **LLM Scaling:** While the use of smaller LLMs is practical from a deployment perspective, it would be interesting to see how performance scales with larger, more powerful models (within reasonable resource constraints).

*   **Potential Influence:** ADACVD has the potential to influence the design of future clinical decision support systems by demonstrating the feasibility and benefits of incorporating LLMs to address the challenges of data heterogeneity and distribution shifts. This could lead to more flexible and adaptable models that are easier to deploy in diverse clinical settings. It pushes the field towards models more suited for real world deployment, by making them robust to missing data and different data distributions.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:** ADACVD presents a significant advancement in CVD risk prediction, primarily driven by its comprehensive approach to addressing real-world clinical data challenges. The LLM adaptability is a major strength, however, synthetic clinical notes and limited geographical diversity hinder a higher score. Furthermore, it is unclear how the model will perform in a real-world clinical setting, or whether there will be any patient outcomes influenced. This is a well-executed research project, but there is much more work required before clinical translation.

### Can LLMs and humans be friends? Uncovering factors affecting human-AI intimacy formation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24658v1)
- **Authors**: Yeseon Hong, Junhyuk Choi, Minju Kim, Bugeun Kim
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being used in conversational roles, yet little is known about how intimacy emerges in human-LLM interactions. Although previous work emphasized the importance of self-disclosure in human-chatbot interaction, it is questionable whether gradual and reciprocal self-disclosure is also helpful in human-LLM interaction. Thus, this study examined three possible aspects contributing to intimacy formation: gradual self-disclosure, reciprocity, and naturalness. Study 1 explored the impact of mutual, gradual self-disclosure with 29 users and a vanilla LLM. Study 2 adopted self-criticism methods for more natural responses and conducted a similar experiment with 53 users. Results indicate that gradual self-disclosure significantly enhances perceived social intimacy, regardless of persona reciprocity. Moreover, participants perceived utterances generated with self-criticism as more natural compared to those of vanilla LLMs; self-criticism fostered higher intimacy in early stages. Also, we observed that excessive empathetic expressions occasionally disrupted immersion, pointing to the importance of response calibration during intimacy formation.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Can LLMs and humans be friends? Uncovering factors affecting human-AI intimacy formation" investigates the factors influencing the formation of intimacy in human-Large Language Model (LLM) interactions. It examines the impact of gradual self-disclosure, reciprocity, and response naturalness on perceived social intimacy. Two experiments were conducted where participants interacted with LLMs with varying levels of self-disclosure, persona similarity, and naturalness (through the use of a self-criticism mechanism to improve response quality).  The results suggest that gradual self-disclosure significantly enhances perceived intimacy, regardless of persona reciprocity.  Furthermore, using a self-criticism method in LLMs generates more natural responses and fosters higher intimacy, especially in the initial stages of the interaction. The study also identifies a potential trade-off where excessive empathetic expressions can disrupt immersion, highlighting the need for response calibration. The paper concludes with design guidelines for creating LLM-based conversational agents that foster intimacy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a relevant and timely question:** The increasing use of LLMs in conversational roles makes understanding intimacy formation crucial for designing effective and engaging AI companions.
    *   **Well-designed experiments:** The study employs a clear experimental design with controlled conditions (persona similarity, gradual disclosure) to isolate the effects of the factors being investigated.
    *   **Use of established psychological frameworks:** Grounding the research in Social Penetration Theory provides a strong theoretical foundation.
    *   **Qualitative and Quantitative Analysis:** Combining quantitative measures of intimacy (SCI, IJS) with qualitative analysis of participant experiences provides a rich and nuanced understanding of the phenomena.
    *   **Practical Design Guidelines:** The derived design guidelines offer concrete recommendations for developing more engaging LLM-based conversational agents.
    *   **Addresses Limitations of Prior Work:** The research explicitly addresses the limitations of previous HCI research by examining gradual self-disclosure and reciprocity dynamically, rather than relying on static designs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Population:** The study involves a relatively small sample of young, South Korean adults. This limits the generalizability of the findings to other demographics and cultures.
    *   **Short Interaction Duration:**  The experiments were conducted over a relatively short time. It's unclear if the observed effects persist over longer interactions or how intimacy evolves over time.
    *   **Potential for Demand Characteristics:** Although the study attempted to blind participants, it's possible that some participants may have suspected they were interacting with an LLM, potentially influencing their responses.
    *   **Reliance on Self-Reported Intimacy:** The study relies on self-reported measures of intimacy (SCI, IJS).  These measures are subjective and may not fully capture the complexities of interpersonal closeness.
    *   **Focus on Early-Stage Intimacy:** The study primarily focuses on the initial stages of intimacy formation. Further research is needed to understand how intimacy can be sustained and deepened in longer-term human-AI relationships.
    *   **Limited Generalizability of Self-Criticism:** The specific self-criticism technique used might not be universally effective across all LLMs and conversational contexts.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper makes a valuable contribution by empirically investigating the factors influencing intimacy in human-LLM interactions, which remains a relatively underexplored area.
    *   The finding that gradual self-disclosure is key to fostering intimacy regardless of persona similarity is novel and significant. It suggests that the *process* of disclosure is more important than *matching* personalities.
    *   The identification of a trade-off between enhanced empathetic responses and perceived naturalness is an important insight. This points to the need for nuanced calibration of empathy in LLM design.
    *   While previous work has explored self-disclosure in human-chatbot interactions, this paper extends these investigations to the more advanced and contextually aware domain of LLMs.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper addresses a relevant and timely question with a strong experimental design and a clear theoretical framework.  The findings offer valuable insights for designing more engaging and human-like LLM-based conversational agents.  While the study has some limitations regarding generalizability and interaction duration, it provides a solid foundation for future research in this area. The study provides significant insights, goes beyond simply testing existing theories, and creates a new understanding of the factors behind intimacy in human-LLM interactions.
