### Multiple LLM Agents Debate for Equitable Cultural Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24671v1)
- **Authors**: Dayeon Ki, Rachel Rudinger, Tianyi Zhou, Marine Carpuat
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) need to adapt their predictions to diverse cultural contexts to benefit diverse communities across the world. While previous efforts have focused on single-LLM, single-turn approaches, we propose to exploit the complementary strengths of multiple LLMs to promote cultural adaptability. We introduce a Multi-Agent Debate framework, where two LLM-based agents debate over a cultural scenario and collaboratively reach a final decision. We propose two variants: one where either LLM agents exclusively debate and another where they dynamically choose between self-reflection and debate during their turns. We evaluate these approaches on 7 open-weight LLMs (and 21 LLM combinations) using the NormAd-ETI benchmark for social etiquette norms in 75 countries. Experiments show that debate improves both overall accuracy and cultural group parity over single-LLM baselines. Notably, multi-agent debate enables relatively small LLMs (7-9B) to achieve accuracies comparable to that of a much larger model (27B parameters).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Multi-Agent Debate framework to improve the cultural adaptability of Large Language Models (LLMs). Unlike prior work that focuses on single LLM, single-turn approaches, this framework leverages the complementary strengths of multiple LLMs through debate.  Two variants are proposed: Debate-Only, where LLMs exclusively debate, and Self-Reflect+Debate, where LLMs dynamically choose between self-reflection and debate. The framework is evaluated on the NORMAD-ETI benchmark, assessing social etiquette norms across 75 countries.  The results demonstrate that debate improves both overall accuracy and cultural group parity compared to single-LLM baselines, enabling smaller LLMs to achieve accuracy comparable to much larger models.

**Rigorous Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in explicitly using a multi-agent debate framework to tackle the problem of cultural alignment in LLMs.  While multi-agent systems and debate have been explored in other contexts (e.g., factuality, reasoning), its application to the specific challenge of cultural norms is a worthwhile contribution. The distinction between Debate-Only and Self-Reflect+Debate adds a layer of methodological innovation. However, it is important to acknowledge that the idea of using multiple models to compensate for limitations of a single model is not entirely new.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the increasing need for LLMs to be sensitive to diverse cultural contexts. Demonstrating that debate can improve both overall accuracy and, more importantly, parity across cultural groups has substantial societal implications.  The finding that smaller models can achieve comparable accuracy to larger models through debate is valuable from a resource perspective. The results offer a cost-effective way to improve LLM cultural sensitivity.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Framework:** The Multi-Agent Debate framework and its variants are well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The evaluation leverages a benchmark specifically designed for cultural alignment (NORMAD-ETI) and includes a reasonable number of LLMs. The consideration of 75 countries further strengthens the empirical foundation.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper goes beyond reporting overall accuracy and provides insightful analysis of decision dynamics, including how predictions evolve through debate and the impact on different cultural groups.
    *   **Addresses an Important Problem:** Cultural alignment is a crucial aspect of responsible AI development.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The framework shows how we can use currently existing (and smaller) LLMs and combine them effectively, achieving improvements without having to develop entirely new models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Cultural Context:**  The NORMAD-ETI benchmark, while a good starting point, may not capture the full nuance and complexity of cultural norms across all 75 countries. Its relatively limited stories per country limit generalization. Furthermore, it uses a ternary classification task ("Yes", "No", or "Neither"), which doesn't fully capture more nuanced cultural differences.
    *   **Reliance on Specific LLMs:** The selection of LLMs might influence the results, and it's possible that different LLM combinations would yield different outcomes.  More justification for why *these* specific LLMs were selected for each role would have strengthened the paper.  The choice of GEMMA-2-27B as the judge LLM, while understandable due to accuracy and speed, could still introduce bias.  A more robust analysis of the judge's impact (perhaps with multiple judges) would be beneficial.
    *   **Simplified Debate Structure:** The debate structure is relatively basic (single round exchange).  While the authors explore more rounds, the findings suggest diminishing returns. Still, alternative debate strategies (e.g., different feedback mechanisms, argumentation styles) could be explored.
    *   **Adjudication Strategy:** The debate process is adjudicated using a single LLM. The choice is understandable, but does not remove bias. Moreover, the paper notes that the debate process primarily improves the prediction of individual models, rather than simply exploiting the strength of the larger judge LLM. It indicates that adjudication strategies deserve further investigation to reliably combine individual model predictions.
    *   **Limited exploration of prompt engineering:** The prompt design for the LLMs could be investigated more closely. A sensitivity analysis around how prompt variations could improve performance (especially in cases where both LLMs make the same error) could significantly bolster the paper's contribution.
    *   **Societal Considerations:** The discussions on societal considerations could benefit from more detail, specifically around potential biases that could be introduced by the Multi-Agent Debate Framework itself.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in cultural alignment, multi-agent systems for LLMs, and resource-efficient AI.  The framework can serve as a foundation for future studies exploring more sophisticated debate strategies and diverse cultural benchmarks.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. The paper presents a novel and well-executed application of a multi-agent debate framework to a crucial problem in responsible AI. The results are promising and offer practical implications. However, the limited scope of the cultural context and some choices in LLM selection/debate structure limit the overall impact. Further investigation into adjudication strategies and additional investigation could raise this ranking significantly.

### TRIDENT: Enhancing Large Language Model Safety with Tri-Dimensional Diversified Red-Teaming Data Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24672v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaorui Wu, Xiaofeng Mao, Fei Li, Xin Zhang, Xuanhong Li, Chong Teng, Donghong Ji, Zhuang Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel in various natural language processing tasks but remain vulnerable to generating harmful content or being exploited for malicious purposes. Although safety alignment datasets have been introduced to mitigate such risks through supervised fine-tuning (SFT), these datasets often lack comprehensive risk coverage. Most existing datasets focus primarily on lexical diversity while neglecting other critical dimensions. To address this limitation, we propose a novel analysis framework to systematically measure the risk coverage of alignment datasets across three essential dimensions: Lexical Diversity, Malicious Intent, and Jailbreak Tactics. We further introduce TRIDENT, an automated pipeline that leverages persona-based, zero-shot LLM generation to produce diverse and comprehensive instructions spanning these dimensions. Each harmful instruction is paired with an ethically aligned response, resulting in two datasets: TRIDENT-Core, comprising 26,311 examples, and TRIDENT-Edge, with 18,773 examples. Fine-tuning Llama 3.1-8B on TRIDENT-Edge demonstrates substantial improvements, achieving an average 14.29% reduction in Harm Score, and a 20% decrease in Attack Success Rate compared to the best-performing baseline model fine-tuned on the WildBreak dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TRIDENT: Enhancing Large Language Model Safety with Tri-Dimensional Diversified Red-Teaming Data Synthesis" addresses the critical issue of safety in Large Language Models (LLMs). It argues that existing safety alignment datasets often lack comprehensive risk coverage, primarily focusing on lexical diversity while neglecting malicious intent and jailbreak tactics. To overcome this, the authors propose a novel analysis framework that measures risk coverage across these three essential dimensions. They introduce TRIDENT, an automated pipeline that leverages persona-based, zero-shot LLM generation to produce diverse and comprehensive instructions spanning these dimensions. The process results in two datasets, TRIDENT-CORE and TRIDENT-EDGE, comprising harmful instructions paired with ethically aligned responses. Fine-tuning a META-LLAMA-3.1-8B model on TRIDENT-EDGE demonstrates substantial improvements in safety benchmarks compared to state-of-the-art baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a notable contribution through its tri-dimensional analysis framework and automated red-teaming data synthesis pipeline.  Existing work tends to focus on single dimensions like lexical diversity or adversarial attacks. Integrating all three dimensions and providing a systematic way to measure and improve upon dataset creation for safety alignment is a strong point of novelty. Persona-based zero-shot generation isn't entirely new, but its application in this context, combined with the targeted inclusion of jailbreak tactics, offers a unique approach.

*   **Significance:** Enhancing LLM safety is paramount for their widespread deployment. The paper's demonstrated improvement in harm scores and attack success rates has direct implications for the trustworthiness and reliability of these models.  The public release of TRIDENT-CORE and TRIDENT-EDGE datasets provides a valuable resource for the research community, enabling further exploration of safety alignment strategies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The tri-dimensional framework provides a structured way to analyze and improve the coverage of safety alignment datasets.
    *   **Automated Pipeline:** TRIDENT offers a scalable and efficient solution for generating diverse red-teaming data.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments demonstrate significant improvements in safety benchmarks, validating the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Dataset Release:**  Sharing TRIDENT-CORE and TRIDENT-EDGE fosters further research and development in LLM safety.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Dependency:** The reliance on LLMs (especially Llama-3 and GPT-4o) for both generation and evaluation introduces potential biases. The quality of the generated data is inherently limited by the capabilities and biases of the LLMs used in the pipeline. While GPT-4o is used, other models like Gemini could be explored for comparative robustness.
    *   **Benchmark Limitations:** While standard benchmarks are used, their comprehensiveness is always a concern. New attacks are constantly being developed, so dataset evaluations are always retrospective.  It would have been interesting to see if the method has any generalization to more recent attack strategies that aren't already present in the datasets used in the benchmarks.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the paper addresses three critical dimensions of risk, other aspects, such as bias in generated responses, are not explicitly addressed. The definition of malicious intent could be broadened beyond the 14 initial categories.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust safety alignment strategies for LLMs. It provides a framework and resources that can be used by researchers and practitioners to create more comprehensive and effective safety datasets. The focus on multiple dimensions of diversity is likely to become a standard practice in the field.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of LLM safety by introducing a novel framework and an automated data generation pipeline. The experimental results are convincing, and the release of the datasets is a valuable contribution to the community. The limitations of relying on LLMs for generation and evaluation are acknowledged but could be mitigated with more diverse or human evaluations. Considering these factors, a score of 7 reflects the paper's meaningful contribution while recognizing areas for future improvement.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Simple Linear Patch Revives Layer-Pruned Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24680v1)
- **Authors**: Xinrui Chen, Haoli Bai, Tao Yuan, Ruikang Liu, Kang Zhao, Xianzhi Yu, Lu Hou, Tian Guan, Yonghong He, Chun Yuan
- **Abstract**: Layer pruning has become a popular technique for compressing large language models (LLMs) due to its simplicity. However, existing layer pruning methods often suffer from significant performance drops. We identify that this degradation stems from the mismatch of activation magnitudes across layers and tokens at the pruning interface. To address this, we propose LinearPatch, a simple plug-and-play technique to revive the layer-pruned LLMs. The proposed method adopts Hadamard transformation to suppress massive outliers in particular tokens, and channel-wise scaling to align the activation magnitudes. These operations can be fused into a single matrix, which functions as a patch to bridge the pruning interface with negligible inference overhead. LinearPatch retains up to 94.15% performance of the original model when pruning 5 layers of LLaMA-3-8B on the question answering benchmark, surpassing existing state-of-the-art methods by 4%. In addition, the patch matrix can be further optimized with memory efficient offline knowledge distillation. With only 5K samples, the retained performance of LinearPatch can be further boosted to 95.16% within 30 minutes on a single computing card.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "A Simple Linear Patch Revives Layer-Pruned Large Language Models" addresses the performance degradation observed in layer-pruned LLMs.  The authors identify that a mismatch in activation magnitudes across layers and tokens at the pruning interface is the root cause. They propose LINEARPATCH, a plug-and-play technique that uses Hadamard transformation and channel-wise scaling (fused into a single matrix) to mitigate this mismatch.  Experimental results demonstrate that LINEARPATCH significantly improves the performance of layer-pruned LLMs across various models and tasks, exceeding existing state-of-the-art methods. The authors also explore a memory-efficient knowledge distillation approach to further enhance the patched model's performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its identification of the activation magnitude mismatch problem at the pruning interface and the proposed LINEARPATCH solution. While Hadamard transformations and channel-wise scaling are not individually new techniques, their specific application to this problem and the fusion into a single, efficient "patch" matrix demonstrates a practical contribution. The memory-efficient distillation approach is also a valuable addition.

*   **Significance:** Layer pruning is a widely used compression technique for LLMs, making this problem relevant to the broader community. The performance gains achieved by LINEARPATCH are significant, particularly in retaining a higher percentage of the original model's accuracy after pruning, and surpassing recent methods like LLM-Streamline. The simplicity and plug-and-play nature of the proposed solution make it highly accessible and potentially widely adopted. The reduced memory footprint of distillation also makes it very valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper provides a concise and easily understandable explanation of the activation magnitude mismatch issue.
    *   Simple and effective solution: The proposed LINEARPATCH is easy to implement and integrates well with existing layer pruning methods.
    *   Strong empirical results: The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across diverse LLMs, tasks, and pruning ratios, demonstrating the effectiveness of LINEARPATCH. The method is benchmarked properly in comparison with recent methods.
    *   Memory-efficient distillation: The proposed offline distillation technique minimizes the memory overhead associated with knowledge distillation.
    * Well-written and structured: The paper is well-organized and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited Theoretical Analysis:  While the empirical results are strong, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of why Hadamard transformation and channel-wise scaling are effective in addressing the activation magnitude mismatch.  Why does the Hadamard work for outliers specifically? This is more empirical than rigorously justified.
    *   Pruning Metric Dependency: Though the paper claims agnosticism to pruning metrics, all the experiments are primarily done with a cosine-similarity metric. Some of the comparisons are done with ShortGPT and LLM-Streamline which themselves use CosSim. Further experiments and insights would be helpful to show more reliable comparisons.
    *   Scope of Evaluation: While the experimental setup covers QA and perplexity evaluations, it could benefit from a more extensive evaluation on other tasks such as generation and summarization, as mentioned in the NeurIPS checklist regarding broader impact and task robustness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM compression. The LINEARPATCH technique could become a standard component of layer pruning pipelines, enabling more efficient and accurate deployment of LLMs.

*   **Score Rationale:**  The paper presents a valuable contribution to LLM compression. While the core techniques used in LINEARPATCH are not groundbreaking, their combination and application to the activation mismatch problem is novel and effective. The strong empirical results and ease of implementation contribute to the paper's significance. The paper, however, lacks a bit in theoretical depth and wider application scope, as well as dependency and comparison to a specific CosSim based pruning metric.
        While the proposed technique has the potential to become widely adopted, the aforementioned shortcomings slightly temper the enthusiasm.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Soft Reasoning: Navigating Solution Spaces in Large Language Models through Controlled Embedding Exploration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24688v1)
- **Authors**: Qinglin Zhu, Runcong Zhao, Hanqi Yan, Yulan He, Yudong Chen, Lin Gui
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) struggle with complex reasoning due to limited diversity and inefficient search. We propose Soft Reasoning, an embedding-based search framework that optimises the embedding of the first token to guide generation. It combines (1) embedding perturbation for controlled exploration and (2) Bayesian optimisation to refine embeddings via a verifier-guided objective, balancing exploration and exploitation. This approach improves reasoning accuracy and coherence while avoiding reliance on heuristic search. Experiments demonstrate superior correctness with minimal computation, making it a scalable, model-agnostic solution.
- **Summary**: The paper "Soft Reasoning: Navigating Solution Spaces in Large Language Models through Controlled Embedding Exploration" proposes a novel method to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is to guide LLM generation by optimizing the embedding of the first token in the generated sequence. This is achieved through a combination of (1) Gaussian embedding perturbation for controlled exploration of the embedding space, and (2) Bayesian optimization to refine the embeddings based on a verifier-guided objective function, balancing exploration and exploitation. The authors demonstrate that their approach, "Soft Reasoning," improves reasoning accuracy and coherence without relying on heuristic search. The method is presented as model-agnostic and scalable, requiring minimal computation. Experimental results on various LLMs and reasoning tasks are used to showcase the superior performance of Soft Reasoning compared to existing decoding strategies.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its approach of directly manipulating the embedding space to influence LLM generation. While techniques like temperature scaling and sampling methods have explored generation diversity at the token probability level, "Soft Reasoning" takes a step further by operating in the embedding space. Combining embedding perturbation with Bayesian optimization for verifier-guided search is also a novel combination of techniques.  However, it is important to acknowledge related work in controllable generation that also manipulates embeddings (e.g., for style transfer) - the distinction here is the *purpose*, which is reasoning and problem-solving.  The exploration of how embedding perturbation affects neuron activation is also a positive.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial challenge in LLMs: their struggle with complex reasoning. By enhancing generation diversity in a controlled and efficient manner, the authors contribute a promising solution. The model-agnostic nature of the approach and its low computational overhead make it highly practical and scalable, increasing its potential for adoption.  The fact that no explicit instruction tuning/training/prompt engineering is necessary to generate the diverse space, but instead a direct manipulation of the latent space, is a strong argument in the paper's favor. The ablation studies are convincing in demonstrating the importance of both the verifier and the coherence terms.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dependence on a "verifier" is a potential limitation. Although the authors mention the verifier can be the same model as the generator, the performance of the verifier will influence the effectiveness of the "Soft Reasoning" technique. This requires careful consideration of verifier design and potential biases it might introduce. A more thorough analysis of the robustness of the method under different verifier performances would be valuable.
    *   While the method is presented as computationally efficient, the Bayesian optimization process and the multiple evaluations of candidate answers still contribute to the overall computational cost. A more detailed analysis of the trade-off between performance improvement and computational overhead is needed, specifically compared to simpler diversification techniques.
    *   The explanation of why manipulating the *first* token embedding is sufficient is somewhat lacking. More justification for this choice, potentially drawing on insights about the importance of initial context in LLM generation, would strengthen the paper. It would be worthwhile to discuss why manipulating the embedding of the first *N* tokens wouldn't have an increase in performance.
    *   The theoretical grounding for the choice of specific hyperparameters (e.g., the exploration exploitation ratio, the dimension reduction factor) could be stronger.
    *   Clarity on how neuron activations are quantified, and what exactly are 'neurons' is essential to reproduce results and to fully accept the analysis.
    *   The paper makes a comparison against Chain of Thought Reasoning (CoT), however CoT has been extended with various techniques (e.g., Tree-of-Thoughts). It is essential to benchmark against the latest extensions.

*   **Potential Impact:** If successful, "Soft Reasoning" has the potential to significantly improve the reliability and accuracy of LLMs in various reasoning tasks. The scalability and model-agnostic nature of the approach could enable its widespread adoption, leading to more intelligent and robust AI systems. The core idea of embedding-based exploration could inspire new research directions in controllable generation and optimization of LLMs.

Score: 7

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a novel approach with strong empirical results. However, the reliance on a verifier and the need for careful hyperparameter tuning, and the choice of only perturbing a single token's embedding space limit its current impact. The paper would be strengthened by a more robust theoretical analysis and an exploration of different verifier designs. Nevertheless, the core idea of using controlled embedding exploration for reasoning holds considerable promise and warrants further investigation. While the work is innovative, the aforementioned weaknesses, including its reliance on a verifier and lacking thorough comparison with state of the art CoT solutions, limit its overall impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### BPE Stays on SCRIPT: Structured Encoding for Robust Multilingual Pretokenization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24689v1)
- **Authors**: Sander Land, Catherine Arnett
- **Abstract**: Byte Pair Encoding (BPE) tokenizers, widely used in Large Language Models, face challenges in multilingual settings, including penalization of non-Western scripts and the creation of tokens with partial UTF-8 sequences. Pretokenization, often reliant on complex regular expressions, can also introduce fragility and unexpected edge cases. We propose SCRIPT (Script Category Representation in PreTokenization), a novel encoding scheme that bypasses UTF-8 byte conversion by using initial tokens based on Unicode script and category properties. This approach enables a simple, rule-based pretokenization strategy that respects script boundaries, offering a robust alternative to pretokenization strategies based on regular expressions. We also introduce and validate a constrained BPE merging strategy that enforces character integrity, applicable to both SCRIPT-BPE and byte-based BPE. Our experiments demonstrate that SCRIPT-BPE achieves competitive compression while eliminating encoding-based penalties for non-Latin-script languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "BPE Stays on SCRIPT: Structured Encoding for Robust Multilingual Pretokenization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCRIPT-BPE, a novel encoding and pretokenization scheme designed to address challenges faced by byte-level BPE tokenizers in multilingual settings. SCRIPT-BPE leverages Unicode script and category properties to create initial tokens, bypassing UTF-8 byte conversion and mitigating byte premium effects that penalize non-Latin scripts.  The authors propose a rule-based pretokenization strategy built on this encoding and a constrained BPE merging strategy that enforces character integrity, preventing merges that produce partial UTF-8 sequences. Experiments demonstrate that SCRIPT-BPE achieves competitive compression while eliminating encoding-based penalties for non-Latin scripts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to pretokenization. Using Unicode script and category properties as the basis for initial token creation is a creative departure from relying on regular expressions or raw byte sequences. The constrained BPE merging strategy also adds a valuable layer of control over the tokenization process. The *concept* of leveraging Unicode properties is not entirely new, but the specific implementation, the combination with constrained merging, and the focus on resolving the problems of byte-premium effects makes this paper unique.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential for SCRIPT-BPE to create more equitable and robust multilingual language models. By addressing the byte premium effect, the approach promotes fairer representation of different scripts. The elimination of partial UTF-8 sequences can lead to more semantically meaningful tokens and potentially improve model performance and stability. The rule-based pretokenization offers a more interpretable and maintainable alternative to complex regular expressions, reducing fragility and the risk of unexpected edge cases. The improved compression rates shown are also notable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-defined problem statement.
    *   Novel and creative solution.
    *   Thorough evaluation with experiments comparing against strong baselines.
    *   Demonstrated benefits for multilingual scenarios.
    *   Provides code for reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The experiments primarily focus on compression ratios. While a good proxy, downstream task performance evaluation would significantly strengthen the claims. This limits the ability to evaluate model performance.
    *   While training time is reported, inference time/memory usage is not, which could be a consideration.
    *   The paper would benefit from more in-depth qualitative analysis of the learned tokens, specifically showing the impact of constrained merging beyond reduced partial UTF-8 sequences.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   Could influence the design of future tokenization schemes, particularly for multilingual models.
    *   The constrained merging strategy could be adopted in other BPE implementations.
    *   May lead to more fair and robust language models with improved performance across different languages.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated approach to addressing a significant challenge in multilingual language modeling. The benefits are clearly demonstrated through experiments. While lacking downstream task performance evaluation, the strong compression results and elimination of undesirable token properties justify a positive assessment.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Speech-to-Text Translation with Phoneme-Augmented CoT: Enhancing Cross-Lingual Transfer in Low-Resource Scenarios
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24691v1)
- **Authors**: Gerard I. Gállego, Oriol Pareras, Martí Cortada Garcia, Lucas Takanori, Javier Hernando
- **Abstract**: We propose a Speech-to-Text Translation (S2TT) approach that integrates phoneme representations into a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) framework to improve translation in low-resource and zero-resource settings. By introducing phoneme recognition as an intermediate step, we enhance cross-lingual transfer, enabling translation even for languages with no labeled speech data. Our system builds on a multilingual LLM, which we extend to process speech and phonemes. Training follows a curriculum learning strategy that progressively introduces more complex tasks. Experiments on multilingual S2TT benchmarks show that phoneme-augmented CoT improves translation quality in low-resource conditions and enables zero-resource translation, while slightly impacting high-resource performance. Despite this trade-off, our findings demonstrate that phoneme-based CoT is a promising step toward making S2TT more accessible across diverse languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a Speech-to-Text Translation (S2TT) system called SALAMANDRA-ST that uses a chain-of-thought (CoT) framework augmented with phoneme recognition. The core idea is to enhance cross-lingual transfer and improve translation quality, particularly in low-resource and zero-resource scenarios. The system uses a multilingual LLM extended to process speech tokens and phonemes. It's trained using a curriculum learning strategy. Experiments on multilingual benchmarks demonstrate that phoneme-augmented CoT improves translation, especially in data-scarce situations.  The paper also explores data augmentation and dual prompting strategies to further improve performance and robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the integration of phoneme recognition within a CoT framework for S2TT, especially for cross-lingual transfer. While CoT for S2TT isn't entirely new, the addition of phonemes as an intermediate step, combined with curriculum learning and exploration of data augmentation and dual prompting, represents a worthwhile contribution. Prior works have mostly focused on textual CoT or simple ASR integration. This work tackles the specific challenge of low-resource scenarios.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to enable S2TT in languages with limited or no labeled speech data. This expands the accessibility of S2TT technology to a broader range of languages, which is important for inclusivity. The paper convincingly demonstrates improvements in low-resource settings, suggesting a practical application. The ablation study of phoneme usage is particularly insightful.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of S2TT in low-resource settings and motivates the proposed solution effectively.
    *   **Well-defined methodology:** The architecture, training procedure, and experimental setup are well-described, making the work reproducible.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper includes extensive experiments across multiple languages and resource conditions. The use of both BLEU and COMET metrics is a good practice.
    *   **Insightful analysis:** The paper offers a thorough analysis of the results, discussing the impact of different components and the role of language proximity. The inclusion of PDA and DPS is a good idea.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **High-Resource Degradation:** The observed performance degradation in high-resource scenarios is a concern. While the paper addresses this with techniques like data augmentation, a more thorough investigation into the causes and potential solutions would be beneficial.
    *   **Error Propagation:** Although the paper mentions mitigating error propagation, a more detailed analysis of how errors propagate through the CoT pipeline (phoneme recognition, transcription, translation) would strengthen the work. What types of phoneme errors are most detrimental? Is the model robust to variations in accent? These questions could have been addressed more explicitly.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The focus is heavily on European languages, and the conclusions may not directly generalize to languages with very different phonetic systems or writing styles.
    *   **Ablation limitations:** The ablation study is not complete. What happens when the S2TT model does *not* include ASR data? What happens when one source language does *not* use its high-resource speech hours? This would have helped explain cross-lingual performance better.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence S2TT research by providing a practical and effective approach for low-resource scenarios. Other researchers can build upon this work by exploring different phoneme representations, CoT strategies, and training techniques.
*   **Clarity:** The writing is generally clear and well-organized.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant approach to S2TT, particularly for low-resource languages. The integration of phoneme recognition within a CoT framework is a compelling idea, and the experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness. The comprehensive analysis and exploration of data augmentation and dual prompting strategies further enhance the value of the work. The limitations, primarily the degradation in high-resource scenarios, lack of completeness of the ablation study, and focus on European languages, prevent it from achieving a higher score. However, it is still a valuable contribution with the potential to influence future research in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-Domain ABSA Conversation Dataset Generation via LLMs for Real-World Evaluation and Model Comparison
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24701v1)
- **Authors**: Tejul Pandit, Meet Raval, Dhvani Upadhyay
- **Abstract**: Aspect-Based Sentiment Analysis (ABSA) offers granular insights into opinions but often suffers from the scarcity of diverse, labeled datasets that reflect real-world conversational nuances. This paper presents an approach for generating synthetic ABSA data using Large Language Models (LLMs) to address this gap. We detail the generation process aimed at producing data with consistent topic and sentiment distributions across multiple domains using GPT-4o. The quality and utility of the generated data were evaluated by assessing the performance of three state-of-the-art LLMs (Gemini 1.5 Pro, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, and DeepSeek-R1) on topic and sentiment classification tasks. Our results demonstrate the effectiveness of the synthetic data, revealing distinct performance trade-offs among the models: DeepSeekR1 showed higher precision, Gemini 1.5 Pro and Claude 3.5 Sonnet exhibited strong recall, and Gemini 1.5 Pro offered significantly faster inference. We conclude that LLM-based synthetic data generation is a viable and flexible method for creating valuable ABSA resources, facilitating research and model evaluation without reliance on limited or inaccessible real-world labeled data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the scarcity of diverse, labeled datasets for Aspect-Based Sentiment Analysis (ABSA) in real-world conversational settings. It proposes a method for generating synthetic ABSA data using Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically GPT-40. The authors detail the generation process, focusing on creating data with consistent topic and sentiment distributions across multiple domains (Technology, Healthcare, Finance, Legal). They then evaluate the quality and utility of the generated data by assessing the performance of three state-of-the-art LLMs (Gemini 1.5 Pro, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, and DeepSeek-R1) on topic and sentiment classification tasks. The results show the effectiveness of the synthetic data, revealing distinct performance trade-offs among the tested LLMs (DeepSeek-R1 exhibiting higher precision; Gemini 1.5 Pro and Claude 3.5 Sonnet demonstrating strong recall; and Gemini 1.5 Pro offering faster inference speed). The authors conclude that LLM-based synthetic data generation is a viable and flexible approach for creating valuable ABSA resources, facilitating research and model evaluation without reliance on limited real-world data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the concept of using LLMs for synthetic data generation isn't entirely new, the paper distinguishes itself by specifically tailoring the generation process for the ABSA task with multi-turn conversational scenarios and controlled sentiment/topic distributions across diverse domains. The controlled dataset creation process is an added novelty. However, it builds upon existing work in synthetic data generation, rather than introducing a groundbreaking paradigm shift. The idea of using LLMs to generate data and then use other LLMs to evaluate has been explored before.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial issue in ABSA research: the lack of labeled data that captures the complexity of real-world conversations. By providing a method for generating synthetic data, the authors offer a potential solution for researchers and practitioners who are constrained by limited or inaccessible labeled data. The comparative evaluation of different LLMs on the generated dataset provides valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses for ABSA tasks, informing model selection and future research directions. The focus on multi-domain data generation is also a significant contribution, as it addresses the domain skew present in many existing ABSA datasets. However, one key limitation is the reliance on LLMs to *evaluate* the data that was *generated* by LLMs, it doesn't measure the true ability to generalize on real world data and relies on the correctness of other LLMs. Also, more detail on filtering of the raw data after creation could be beneficial.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined generation process with clear steps and justifications.
    *   Focus on generating data that reflects real-world conversational nuances.
    *   Comparative evaluation of multiple state-of-the-art LLMs.
    *   Demonstration of the effectiveness of the synthetic data for ABSA tasks.
    *   The structured evaluation methodology, using a range of standard metrics to assess performance.
    *   Thorough statistical analysis of the created datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation primarily relies on the performance of LLMs on the synthetic data. A more rigorous evaluation would involve comparing the performance of models trained on the synthetic data with those trained on real-world data (if available) or evaluating the models on a held-out set of real-world data.
    *   The generated data might be biased towards the knowledge and biases of the LLM used for generation (GPT-40). It doesn't necessarily ensure generalization to unseen real world data.
    *   The details of the prompt engineering used for data generation could be expanded. The quality of the generated data is highly dependent on the prompts, and more transparency would be beneficial. The data filtering and validation section could also be more detailed (Section 3.2).
    * The generated dataset may not encompass the long tail aspects of real world data.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and accessible method for generating ABSA data. It could encourage researchers to explore synthetic data generation as a way to overcome data scarcity issues and to develop more robust and generalizable ABSA models. The comparative evaluation of LLMs could also inform model selection and guide future research directions. The provided dataset is also a plus.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by addressing the data scarcity problem in ABSA and providing a method for generating synthetic data that reflects real-world conversational nuances. However, the lack of evaluation on real-world data and the potential biases of the LLM-generated data limit its impact. While the method is promising, more rigorous validation is needed to demonstrate its effectiveness and generalizability. The novelty is incremental rather than transformative, building upon existing synthetic data techniques. The assigned score reflects these strengths and weaknesses, indicating a useful contribution with room for further improvement. The dataset also has the added benefit of potentially aiding smaller organizations that do not have the budget to create large amounts of labeled data.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Causal-aware Large Language Models: Enhancing Decision-Making Through Learning, Adapting and Acting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24710v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Chen, Jiahao Zhang, Haipeng Zhu, Boyan Xu, Zhifeng Hao, Keli Zhang, Junjian Ye, Ruichu Cai
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown great potential in decision-making due to the vast amount of knowledge stored within the models. However, these pre-trained models are prone to lack reasoning abilities and are difficult to adapt to new environments, further hindering their application to complex real-world tasks. To address these challenges, inspired by the human cognitive process, we propose Causal-aware LLMs, which integrate the structural causal model (SCM) into the decision-making process to model, update, and utilize structured knowledge of the environment in a ``learning-adapting-acting" paradigm. Specifically, in the learning stage, we first utilize an LLM to extract the environment-specific causal entities and their causal relations to initialize a structured causal model of the environment. Subsequently,in the adapting stage, we update the structured causal model through external feedback about the environment, via an idea of causal intervention. Finally, in the acting stage, Causal-aware LLMs exploit structured causal knowledge for more efficient policy-making through the reinforcement learning agent. The above processes are performed iteratively to learn causal knowledge, ultimately enabling the causal-aware LLMs to achieve a more accurate understanding of the environment and make more efficient decisions. Experimental results across 22 diverse tasks within the open-world game ``Crafter" validate the effectiveness of our proposed method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Causal-aware LLMs," a framework that integrates Structural Causal Models (SCMs) into Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve decision-making in dynamic environments. The framework operates in three stages: learning (extracting causal knowledge to build an initial SCM), adapting (updating the SCM through causal intervention based on environmental feedback), and acting (using the SCM to guide reinforcement learning agents' policy-making). This iterative process allows the LLM to learn causal knowledge, understand the environment better, and make more efficient decisions.  The authors validate their method on the "Crafter" open-world game, demonstrating improved performance across various tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of combining LLMs with SCMs for improved decision-making is reasonably novel. While existing work has explored LLMs for RL, reward shaping, and fine-tuning, the explicit integration of causal intervention and SCMs to address LLM hallucinations and improve environmental understanding adds a unique perspective.  The "learning-adapting-acting" paradigm, although conceptually straightforward, provides a concrete structure for integrating causal knowledge into the decision-making loop. The use of causal intervention to refine the causal graph generated by the LLM is also a strong point. The ablation studies clearly demonstrate that each of the three stages learning, adapting, and acting contribute to the overall success of the method.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to address a key limitation of LLMs in real-world applications: their lack of reasoning abilities and difficulty adapting to new environments. By incorporating causal knowledge, the framework enhances LLM reasoning and decision-making capabilities, making them more suitable for complex tasks.  The "Crafter" experiments demonstrate tangible performance improvements, indicating the practical value of the approach. The work also makes a contribution to the field of LLM's causal reasoning and addresses the challenge of LLM hallucinations by refining causal relations with casual intervention.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing LLM-based approaches for decision-making.
    * **Novel Approach:** The integration of SCMs and causal intervention is a fresh perspective.
    * **Well-Defined Framework:** The "learning-adapting-acting" paradigm provides a structured approach.
    * **Empirical Validation:** Experiments on "Crafter" demonstrate performance improvements.
    * **Ablation Studies:** Ablation experiments prove the importance of each stage.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Environment Limitations:** "Crafter," while complex, is still a simplified environment. Demonstrating the framework's effectiveness in more complex and realistic scenarios is needed.
    * **LLM Hallucination:** LLM hallucination is a critical problem that the paper addresses. While using Causal intervention can help to refine the initial causal relations, how does the approach deal with situations where hallucinations remain after causal intervention, or if causal intervention isn't able to address the hallucination?
    * **Implementation Details:** While the overall approach is well-described, more details about the prompt engineering used for LLMs and the specific reward functions used in the RL agent would be beneficial for reproducibility.
    * **Scalability Issues:** The computational complexity of SCM learning and updating, especially in environments with many variables, isn't thoroughly addressed.  The approach's scalability to more complex environments needs further consideration.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging further research into integrating causal reasoning into LLMs for decision-making. The "learning-adapting-acting" paradigm could be a useful template for future work.  The idea of using causal intervention to refine LLM-generated knowledge has broad applicability.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach to improve LLM decision-making by integrating causal knowledge. While the framework demonstrates solid results in a simulated environment and addresses a key limitation of LLMs, there are some limitations concerning environment complexity, and the scalability of the proposed approach needs further evaluation in more complex and realistic scenarios. Taking these factors into account, a score of 7 seems appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### HESEIA: A community-based dataset for evaluating social biases in large language models, co-designed in real school settings in Latin America
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24712v1)
- **Authors**: Guido Ivetta, Marcos J. Gomez, Sofía Martinelli, Pietro Palombini, M. Emilia Echeveste, Nair Carolina Mazzeo, Beatriz Busaniche, Luciana Benotti
- **Abstract**: Most resources for evaluating social biases in Large Language Models are developed without co-design from the communities affected by these biases, and rarely involve participatory approaches. We introduce HESEIA, a dataset of 46,499 sentences created in a professional development course. The course involved 370 high-school teachers and 5,370 students from 189 Latin-American schools. Unlike existing benchmarks, HESEIA captures intersectional biases across multiple demographic axes and school subjects. It reflects local contexts through the lived experience and pedagogical expertise of educators. Teachers used minimal pairs to create sentences that express stereotypes relevant to their school subjects and communities. We show the dataset diversity in term of demographic axes represented and also in terms of the knowledge areas included. We demonstrate that the dataset contains more stereotypes unrecognized by current LLMs than previous datasets. HESEIA is available to support bias assessments grounded in educational communities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HESEIA, a novel dataset for evaluating social biases in Large Language Models (LLMs), created in a community-based and participatory manner.  Unlike existing benchmarks, HESEIA was co-designed by 370 high-school teachers and 5,370 students from 189 Latin American schools.  The dataset comprises 46,499 sentences reflecting local contexts and capturing intersectional biases across multiple demographic axes and school subjects.  Teachers, drawing on their lived experiences and pedagogical expertise, used minimal pairs to generate examples of stereotypes relevant to their communities and subjects. The paper presents the methodology for dataset creation through a professional development course for teachers and demonstrates that HESEIA contains stereotypes that are harder for LLMs to recognize compared to existing datasets.  The authors also evaluate the impact of the course on teachers' perceptions of AI bias.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *methodology* of dataset creation: a participatory, community-based approach involving teachers rather than crowdworkers. This is a significant departure from the standard practice of creating bias datasets.  HESEIA also claims to capture intersectional biases and locally relevant stereotypes that are often missed by datasets constructed in and for Western, dominant cultures. The focus on the Latin American context is valuable given the relative lack of resources for bias assessment in non-English languages and non-Western contexts.

* **Significance:** The potential significance is high if HESEIA can be shown to improve the *fairness* of LLMs when applied in Latin American educational settings.  The paper presents initial evidence suggesting that the dataset is more challenging for LLMs to handle, suggesting that it may capture nuances that are currently ignored. The description of the professional development course and its impact on teachers' perceptions contributes to the ongoing discussion about responsible AI education and how to empower educators in the AI era.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Participatory Methodology:** The involvement of teachers and students grounds the dataset in real-world educational contexts and lived experiences.
    * **Intersectional Bias Capture:**  The dataset explicitly targets intersectional biases, which are often underrepresented in existing resources.
    * **Contextual Relevance:** HESEIA reflects the specific cultural and regional contexts of Latin America.
    * **Professional Development Integration:** The paper describes how bias assessment was integrated into a professional development program for teachers.
    * **Thorough Documentation:** The paper provides detailed information about the dataset creation process, demographics of participants, and the content of the professional development course.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Lack of Comparative Evaluation (Beyond Recognition Rates):** The paper focuses on recognition/rejection rates for stereotypes, but it lacks a comprehensive evaluation showing how using HESEIA leads to demonstrably *fairer* LLM behavior in downstream tasks. For example, it would be beneficial to show improved outcomes on educational applications when using LLMs fine-tuned or evaluated with HESEIA.
    * **Dataset Availability is Assumed:** It should be clarified if the dataset will be released with a license that permits reuse and modification.
    * **Generalizability of Methodology:** While the Latin American focus is a strength, the question of whether this specific methodology can be easily transferred to *other* cultural or linguistic contexts needs more discussion. Are there unique characteristics of the Latin American educational system or cultural norms that make this approach particularly well-suited?
    * **Subjectivity of "Stereotype" Labelling:** Despite teacher involvement, there is an inherent subjectivity in labeling a statement as a stereotype. While triangulation within the immediate social circles of the teachers was performed, a more rigorous annotation scheme or external validation might be considered.
    * **Limited LLM Evaluation:** Only 4 LLMs are assessed.
    * **The study may not reflect how all teachers think.**

* **Potential Influence:** HESEIA can serve as a model for creating more culturally sensitive and contextually appropriate bias datasets in other regions and languages. It also contributes to the growing literature on participatory AI development and responsible AI education. The integration of bias assessment into professional development provides a practical template for educators.

* **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a valuable resource and a promising methodology for addressing bias in LLMs. The participatory approach and focus on intersectionality are particularly noteworthy. However, the lack of a more comprehensive evaluation demonstrating improved fairness in downstream tasks and the limited transferability analysis of the methodology reduces its overall impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FinMME: Benchmark Dataset for Financial Multi-Modal Reasoning Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24714v1)
- **Authors**: Junyu Luo, Zhizhuo Kou, Liming Yang, Xiao Luo, Jinsheng Huang, Zhiping Xiao, Jingshu Peng, Chengzhong Liu, Jiaming Ji, Xuanzhe Liu, Sirui Han, Ming Zhang, Yike Guo
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have experienced rapid development in recent years. However, in the financial domain, there is a notable lack of effective and specialized multimodal evaluation datasets. To advance the development of MLLMs in the finance domain, we introduce FinMME, encompassing more than 11,000 high-quality financial research samples across 18 financial domains and 6 asset classes, featuring 10 major chart types and 21 subtypes. We ensure data quality through 20 annotators and carefully designed validation mechanisms. Additionally, we develop FinScore, an evaluation system incorporating hallucination penalties and multi-dimensional capability assessment to provide an unbiased evaluation. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that even state-of-the-art models like GPT-4o exhibit unsatisfactory performance on FinMME, highlighting its challenging nature. The benchmark exhibits high robustness with prediction variations under different prompts remaining below 1%, demonstrating superior reliability compared to existing datasets. Our dataset and evaluation protocol are available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/luojunyu/FinMME and https://github.com/luo-junyu/FinMME.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FINMME, a new benchmark dataset for evaluating financial multi-modal reasoning in large language models (MLLMs). The dataset contains over 11,000 high-quality financial research samples spanning 18 domains and 6 asset classes, featuring various chart types and hierarchical metadata. The authors emphasize data quality through a rigorous annotation process with multiple annotators, LLM consistency checks, and expert validation. They also propose FinScore, an evaluation metric that incorporates hallucination penalties and domain-normalized scoring to address the challenges of financial data. The paper presents experimental results showing that even state-of-the-art MLLMs struggle on FINMME, highlighting the need for specialized financial datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper directly tackles a recognized gap in the MLLM evaluation landscape: the lack of specialized, high-quality datasets for the financial domain. Existing datasets are either general-purpose or lack the scale, quality, or domain coverage needed for robust financial reasoning evaluation.
    *   **Rigorous Data Collection and Annotation:** The authors detail a comprehensive data collection and annotation process, including expert consultation, LLM consistency checks, and thorough quality control measures. This is critical in the financial domain, where accuracy and reliability are paramount. The hallucination penalty is a particularly valuable addition, recognizing the real-world risks of incorrect financial information.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** FINMME offers impressive breadth in terms of financial domains, asset classes, and chart types. This allows for more nuanced and comprehensive evaluation of MLLM capabilities.
    *   **Evaluation Metric:** The FinScore metric is a well-considered attempt to address the specific challenges of evaluating MLLMs in the financial domain. The domain-normalized scoring promotes fairer comparisons, and the hallucination penalty is essential.
    *   **Benchmark Results:**  The demonstration that even strong MLLMs like GPT-4o perform relatively poorly on FINMME is compelling. This convincingly demonstrates the dataset's challenging nature and its value for driving future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Evaluation Tasks:**  While the dataset itself is novel, the evaluation methodology relies primarily on multiple-choice questions and calculations. These are standard tasks in MLLM evaluation, and the paper doesn't introduce fundamentally new task types that are specific to financial reasoning.  The authors could have explored more complex, open-ended reasoning scenarios that are prevalent in financial analysis.
    *   **Focus on Accuracy over Broader Skills:** The emphasis on accuracy and hallucination penalties, while justifiable, potentially overlooks other important aspects of financial reasoning, such as creative insights, scenario planning, and risk assessment under uncertainty. A more holistic evaluation framework might be considered in future work.
    *   **Lack of Qualitative Analysis:** The paper primarily focuses on quantitative performance metrics.  A qualitative analysis of MLLM errors and reasoning patterns on FINMME would provide valuable insights into the specific challenges that MLLMs face in the financial domain.
    *   **Limited Comparison to Existing Financial QA Datasets:** While Table 1 provides an overview of related datasets, Appendix E should have included a more comprehensive comparison.
    *   **Risk of Overfitting to FinScore:** While FinScore's hallucination penalty is well-intended, training models to maximize this metric alone could lead to models that are overly conservative and avoid making novel, potentially correct but uncertain predictions.

*   **Significance:**

    *   FINMME has the potential to become a valuable benchmark for the financial MLLM community. It will likely spur research on improving MLLM performance on financial tasks.
    *   The rigorous data collection and annotation process sets a high standard for future datasets in specialized domains.
    *   The FinScore metric provides a useful starting point for developing more sophisticated evaluation metrics that account for the specific challenges of financial reasoning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by providing a much-needed, high-quality dataset for evaluating MLLMs in finance. However, while it addresses a significant gap, the evaluation tasks and metrics are not fundamentally novel. More innovative tasks, a deeper qualitative analysis, and improved comparisons to similar finance focused datasets would have significantly enhanced the contribution. The risk of overfitting to FinScore and potential overlooking of more nuanced financial tasks also contribute to the score.

### Towards Scalable Schema Mapping using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24716v1)
- **Authors**: Christopher Buss, Mahdis Safari, Arash Termehchy, Stefan Lee, David Maier
- **Abstract**: The growing need to integrate information from a large number of diverse sources poses significant scalability challenges for data integration systems. These systems often rely on manually written schema mappings, which are complex, source-specific, and costly to maintain as sources evolve. While recent advances suggest that large language models (LLMs) can assist in automating schema matching by leveraging both structural and natural language cues, key challenges remain. In this paper, we identify three core issues with using LLMs for schema mapping: (1) inconsistent outputs due to sensitivity to input phrasing and structure, which we propose methods to address through sampling and aggregation techniques; (2) the need for more expressive mappings (e.g., GLaV), which strain the limited context windows of LLMs; and (3) the computational cost of repeated LLM calls, which we propose to mitigate through strategies like data type prefiltering.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize the paper, provide a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, and assign a score with a detailed justification.

**Summary:**

This paper explores the challenges and potential solutions for scalable schema mapping using Large Language Models (LLMs).  It identifies three core challenges: inconsistent outputs from LLMs due to input sensitivity, the need for more expressive mapping representations (GLaV) exceeding LLM context windows, and the computational cost of repeated LLM calls. To address these, the paper proposes techniques like sampling and aggregation to mitigate inconsistent outputs, discusses the complexities of supporting GLaV mappings within context limits, and suggests data type prefiltering to reduce the computational burden of LLM calls. The paper includes preliminary experimental results using an open-source LLM, showing that the proposed prompting strategies and bidirectional schema matching approach can achieve competitive performance compared to proprietary models. It investigates the challenges of generating more complex mappings and evaluates techniques for improving their efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its practical exploration of the challenges inherent in applying LLMs to schema mapping, particularly with a focus on scalability and expressiveness. While using LLMs for schema matching isn't entirely new, the specific focus on:

*   **Addressing Inconsistent Outputs:** The proposed methods of sampling and aggregation to handle the LLM output variability are a valuable contribution. While the idea of prompting multiple times isn't novel on its own, the paper's structured approach of employing symmetric transformations of the input is a useful exploration that provides new insight into how to tackle LLM sensitivity.
*   **FRD Mappings:** The explicit focus on addressing FRD mappings is a valuable addition as existing LLM approaches focus primarily on schema alignment without full mapping support. The discussion of how SQL is unable to generate GLAV rules is important.
*   **Emphasis on Scalability:** The challenges with large input and the computational resource needs is an important dimension of investigation.
*   **Open-Source Focus:** The research using a large open source LLM is beneficial given the ethical and legal concerns of commercial models.

The main question is whether the approaches are sufficiently novel, with most aspects appearing to be "sensible extensions" rather than paradigm shifts.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work lies in its contribution to the practical applicability of LLMs in data integration. The identified challenges are genuine roadblocks to wider adoption, and the proposed solutions offer tangible steps towards overcoming them.

*   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on real-world datasets (MIMIC-OMOP, Synthea-OMOP, and Amalgam) makes the findings more relevant to practitioners.
*   **Improved Efficiency:** By addressing computational costs and output consistency, the research contributes to making LLM-based schema mapping more feasible for large-scale data integration projects.
*   **Direction for Future Research:** The identified limitations and the discussions of open problems (e.g., filtering input schemas, scalable representation for FRD rules, constraint-based filtering, model-based filtering) provide valuable directions for future research in the field.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear problem definition and articulation of challenges.
*   Well-structured presentation of proposed solutions.
*   Experimental evaluation using established datasets.
*   Valuable insights into the practical considerations of using LLMs for schema mapping.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Depth of Evaluation:** The preliminary nature of the experimental results means that the effectiveness of the proposed solutions hasn't been definitively proven. A more thorough and extensive evaluation would be beneficial.
*   **Incremental Novelty:** Some of the proposed solutions appear to be incremental improvements over existing techniques.
*   **Lack of direct comparative results:** Many approaches are referenced (Parciak et al, MatchMaker), but direct performance comparison is limited. This makes the gains of the presented approach more difficult to isolate.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the factors above, I assign a score of **6** to this paper.

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a clear and relevant problem (scalability of schema mapping using LLMs) and identifies key challenges. It proposes reasonable solutions with preliminary supporting evidence using LLMs. The paper offers clear ideas of how to improve data integration, and explicitly acknowledges the challenges related to them. The paper makes a contribution to the understanding of LLM schema mapping and potential remedies. The paper has clear strengths related to data integration.

However, there are weaknesses. The novelty, though present, is not particularly groundbreaking, as many of the solutions build upon existing techniques. A more rigorous comparison to existing LLM methods would strengthen the arguments, and most techniques for filtering still required manual specification or intervention. The preliminary results need to be significantly extended.

### PDE-Transformer: Efficient and Versatile Transformers for Physics Simulations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24717v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Holzschuh, Qiang Liu, Georg Kohl, Nils Thuerey
- **Abstract**: We introduce PDE-Transformer, an improved transformer-based architecture for surrogate modeling of physics simulations on regular grids. We combine recent architectural improvements of diffusion transformers with adjustments specific for large-scale simulations to yield a more scalable and versatile general-purpose transformer architecture, which can be used as the backbone for building large-scale foundation models in physical sciences. We demonstrate that our proposed architecture outperforms state-of-the-art transformer architectures for computer vision on a large dataset of 16 different types of PDEs. We propose to embed different physical channels individually as spatio-temporal tokens, which interact via channel-wise self-attention. This helps to maintain a consistent information density of tokens when learning multiple types of PDEs simultaneously. We demonstrate that our pre-trained models achieve improved performance on several challenging downstream tasks compared to training from scratch and also beat other foundation model architectures for physics simulations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PDE-Transformer paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PDE-Transformer, a transformer-based architecture for surrogate modeling of physics simulations on regular grids. It combines architectural improvements from diffusion transformers with adjustments tailored for large-scale simulations. Key contributions include: (1) Token down/upsampling and shifted window attention for multi-scale modeling and improved scaling to high-resolution data. (2) A modified attention operation that decouples interactions between spatio-temporal axes and physical channel axes. (3) Demonstrating improved performance compared to state-of-the-art transformer architectures for computer vision on a dataset of 16 different PDEs, and showcasing improved downstream task performance compared to training from scratch and other foundation models. The paper also includes detailed ablation studies and explores pre-training strategies. The model is available as open source.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a well-engineered solution, but the architectural components are largely adapted from existing works. The core novelty lies in the specific *combination* of these components and their tailoring for the domain of physics simulation. The decoupling of channel and spatio-temporal attention, while an intuitive and useful idea, is not a fundamentally new attention mechanism. Token up/downsampling in the context of U-nets has been long explored, and while the authors adapt it, they don't revolutionize it. Therefore, the novelty is incremental, being a specific implementation that leverages the best of existing tools.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate, and stems from showing how transformer architectures can be effectively adapted to physics simulations. This is an important application area. The demonstration of generalization across a reasonably wide set of PDEs with a *single* architecture is valuable, suggesting that the model captures some underlying structure or principles relevant to this class of problems. The comparison against existing models on downstream tasks is also a strong point.  Furthermore, the release of open source code is a plus for reproducibility and follow-up work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear presentation and well-structured experiments.
    *   Comprehensive ablation study exploring design choices.
    *   Demonstrated superior performance compared to existing models in specific tasks.
    *   Addresses an important problem with high practical relevance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited architectural novelty. The building blocks are mostly adapted from existing literature.
    *   The results are limited to 2D regular grids which limits real-world applicability in more complex simulations.
    *   Limited exploration in what other ways can this architecture handle new tasks in physics simulations. The authors state inverse problems and handling noisy data would be good future directions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can be seen as a catalyst for further research in physics-informed machine learning and foundation models for scientific computing. It provides a practical and well-documented example of how to adapt transformer architectures to a specific scientific domain. It is a practical study from the field of scientific machine learning, and so it has some impact for that community.

*   **Score Rationale:** The paper provides a reasonable and practical approach. The ideas are not truly groundbreaking, but it is a good piece of engineering and implementation, and shows a practical application of transformer models in an important field. Due to this it's impact is considered higher than more blue-sky research, as other scientists will readily take up this model.
