### Reinforcing Video Reasoning with Focused Thinking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24718v1)
- **Authors**: Jisheng Dang, Jingze Wu, Teng Wang, Xuanhui Lin, Nannan Zhu, Hongbo Chen, Wei-Shi Zheng, Meng Wang, Tat-Seng Chua
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in reinforcement learning, particularly through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), have significantly improved multimodal large language models for complex reasoning tasks. However, two critical limitations persist: 1) they often produce unfocused, verbose reasoning chains that obscure salient spatiotemporal cues and 2) binary rewarding fails to account for partially correct answers, resulting in high reward variance and inefficient learning. In this paper, we propose TW-GRPO, a novel framework that enhances visual reasoning with focused thinking and dense reward granularity. Specifically, we employs a token weighting mechanism that prioritizes tokens with high informational density (estimated by intra-group variance), suppressing redundant tokens like generic reasoning prefixes. Furthermore, we reformulate RL training by shifting from single-choice to multi-choice QA tasks, where soft rewards enable finer-grained gradient estimation by distinguishing partial correctness. Additionally, we propose question-answer inversion, a data augmentation strategy to generate diverse multi-choice samples from existing benchmarks. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on several video reasoning and general understanding benchmarks. Notably, TW-GRPO achieves 50.4\% accuracy on CLEVRER (18.8\% improvement over Video-R1) and 65.8\% on MMVU. Our codes are available at \href{https://github.com/longmalongma/TW-GRPO}{https://github.com/longmalongma/TW-GRPO}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TW-GRPO, a novel reinforcement learning framework designed to improve video reasoning capabilities in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). TW-GRPO addresses two key limitations of existing methods: the production of unfocused, verbose reasoning chains and the use of sparse, binary rewards that don't account for partial correctness. The framework incorporates a token weighting mechanism that prioritizes informative tokens based on intra-group variance, suppressing redundant tokens. It also reformulates RL training using a multi-choice QA task format with soft rewards, enabling finer-grained gradient estimation by distinguishing partial correctness.  Finally, a Question-Answer Inversion (QAI) data augmentation strategy is proposed to generate diverse multi-choice samples. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on video reasoning and general understanding benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents two primary novelties: (1) a token weighting scheme based on intra-group variance to focus reasoning, and (2) the reformulation of the reinforcement learning objective to use multi-choice QA with soft rewards. While using soft rewards in RL is not entirely new (as the authors acknowledge with VideoChat-R1), its application to multi-choice Video-QA and the combination with token weighting represent a novel contribution. The Question-Answer Inversion is a useful but relatively straightforward data augmentation technique. Token weighting using variance as a proxy for importance has connections to work in interpretability and active learning, but its application in the context of GRPO seems novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements over existing methods (including Video-R1 and VideoChat-R1) on established video reasoning benchmarks. The performance boost on CLEVRER is particularly noteworthy. The ablation studies provide some evidence that the token weighting and soft reward mechanisms contribute to these improvements. Qualitatively, the focused reasoning chains generated by TW-GRPO are more concise and relevant. This can potentially improve the reliability of reasoning chains.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Demonstrated state-of-the-art performance on several benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies to analyze the contribution of individual components.
    *   Qualitative analysis illustrating the benefits of focused reasoning.
    *   Addresses a concrete problem of the verbosity of MLLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Ablation granularity:** While ablation studies are included, it's difficult to fully disentangle the effects of different components. For example, how much is the increase coming from the new token weighting, and how much is coming from the multi-choice reward structure itself. A full factorial study would strengthen claims but would be very costly to perform.
    *   **Dependency on assumption B.1:** The analysis relies heavily on assumption B.1 which posits output stability which can make training very dependent on proper hyperparameter settings.
    *   **Oversimplification of grounding task:** While this paper improved QA tasks, other grounding issues not directly addressed still persist in MLLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of video reasoning with MLLMs by introducing more effective training techniques. The ideas of token weighting and soft rewards could be adopted in other RL-based approaches. The QAI data augmentation strategy could also be beneficial for other tasks where labeled data is scarce.

**Justification for the score:**

The paper addresses an important problem in MLLMs and presents a novel and effective framework for improving video reasoning. The performance improvements are significant, and the ablation studies provide some insight into the contribution of different components. However, some limitations in ablation granularity and theoretical analysis prevent a higher score. The overall impact is solid.

- **Score**: 7/10

### HELM: Hyperbolic Large Language Models via Mixture-of-Curvature Experts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24722v1)
- **Authors**: Neil He, Rishabh Anand, Hiren Madhu, Ali Maatouk, Smita Krishnaswamy, Leandros Tassiulas, Menglin Yang, Rex Ying
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown great success in text modeling tasks across domains. However, natural language exhibits inherent semantic hierarchies and nuanced geometric structure, which current LLMs do not capture completely owing to their reliance on Euclidean operations. Recent studies have also shown that not respecting the geometry of token embeddings leads to training instabilities and degradation of generative capabilities. These findings suggest that shifting to non-Euclidean geometries can better align language models with the underlying geometry of text. We thus propose to operate fully in Hyperbolic space, known for its expansive, scale-free, and low-distortion properties. We thus introduce HELM, a family of HypErbolic Large Language Models, offering a geometric rethinking of the Transformer-based LLM that addresses the representational inflexibility, missing set of necessary operations, and poor scalability of existing hyperbolic LMs. We additionally introduce a Mixture-of-Curvature Experts model, HELM-MICE, where each expert operates in a distinct curvature space to encode more fine-grained geometric structure from text, as well as a dense model, HELM-D. For HELM-MICE, we further develop hyperbolic Multi-Head Latent Attention (HMLA) for efficient, reduced-KV-cache training and inference. For both models, we develop essential hyperbolic equivalents of rotary positional encodings and RMS normalization. We are the first to train fully hyperbolic LLMs at billion-parameter scale, and evaluate them on well-known benchmarks such as MMLU and ARC, spanning STEM problem-solving, general knowledge, and commonsense reasoning. Our results show consistent gains from our HELM architectures -- up to 4% -- over popular Euclidean architectures used in LLaMA and DeepSeek, highlighting the efficacy and enhanced reasoning afforded by hyperbolic geometry in large-scale LM pretraining.
- **Summary**: The paper "HELM: Hyperbolic Large Language Models via Mixture-of-Curvature Experts" introduces a family of large language models (LLMs) that operate entirely within hyperbolic space. This contrasts with contemporary LLMs that rely on Euclidean operations. The authors argue that hyperbolic space, with its expansive, scale-free, and low-distortion properties, is better suited for capturing the inherent semantic hierarchies and geometric structure of natural language. To this end, they propose several novel hyperbolic modules, including Hyperbolic Rotary Positional Encodings (HOPE) and Hyperbolic RMSNorm. They also introduce a Mixture-of-Curvature Experts (MICE) model, HELM-MICE, where each expert operates in a distinct curvature space to encode more fine-grained geometric structure. They further develop hyperbolic Multi-Head Latent Attention (HMLA) for efficient training and inference with reduced KV-cache. The authors train these models at billion-parameter scale and evaluate them on benchmarks such as MMLU and ARC, demonstrating consistent gains over popular Euclidean architectures like LLaMA and DeepSeek.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components. The most prominent is the HELM architecture itself: a fully hyperbolic LLM. While hyperbolic geometry has been explored in NLP previously, scaling this to billion-parameter LLMs is a non-trivial undertaking and represents a significant engineering effort. The MICE component adds another layer of novelty by allowing experts to specialize in different curvatures, potentially allowing the model to better capture the varying hyperbolicity of different parts of the input. The HOPE and HMLA components, while potentially more incremental, are essential for bridging the gap between hyperbolic geometry and modern LLM architectures. The theoretical analysis of HOPE is a solid contribution.

**Significance:** The findings suggest that hyperbolic geometry can indeed improve the performance of LLMs on tasks requiring reasoning and knowledge understanding. The consistent gains over Euclidean architectures are promising, indicating that the shift to non-Euclidean geometries is more than a theoretical curiosity. The efficiency improvements offered by HMLA are critical for scaling hyperbolic LLMs to even larger sizes.  This work has the potential to shift the community towards exploring alternative geometries for LLMs.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper addresses several key limitations of previous hyperbolic Transformers, including representational inflexibility, lack of essential operations, and poor scalability.
*   **Novel Modules:** The introduction of HOPE, HMLA, and MICE provides concrete building blocks for constructing hyperbolic LLMs.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their models through extensive experiments on well-established benchmarks.
*   **Theoretical Analysis:** The paper includes theoretical analysis of the HOPE module, providing justification for its design choices.
*   **Scalability:** The demonstration of a billion-parameter hyperbolic LLM is a significant achievement.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Data Comparison:** The training data used for the HELM models (Wikipedia) is smaller compared to the datasets used to train commercial LLMs. While the authors acknowledge this, it limits a direct, apples-to-apples comparison with state-of-the-art models trained on significantly larger datasets.  This makes it difficult to truly isolate the effect of hyperbolic geometry.
*   **Runtime Overhead:**  The increased training time of HELM models is a significant drawback. The paper only mentions a factor of ~1.5-1.8x increase, it does not delve into the reasons behind this increased runtime and offer potential solutions.
*   **Incremental Gains:** While the gains are consistent, the relative improvements (up to 4%) are modest and might not justify the increased complexity and computational cost. The gains are especially marginal at 1B parameters. It's crucial to note that even the best performing models can still be substantially outperformed by larger, commercially developed Euclidean models.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence future research in geometric deep learning and LLMs. It provides a roadmap for building hyperbolic LLMs and demonstrates the potential benefits of using non-Euclidean geometries. Further research is needed to address the limitations of the current work and to explore other non-Euclidean geometries that might be even better suited for language modeling.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:** The paper presents a significant engineering effort and provides convincing evidence that hyperbolic geometry can improve LLM performance. The introduction of novel modules and the scaling to billion-parameter sizes are notable achievements. However, the modest performance gains, limited data comparison, and increased computational cost temper the overall impact. The paper opens up a promising direction for future research, but further work is needed to fully realize the potential of hyperbolic LLMs. The primary challenge is that the core architectural changes are unlikely to translate into models significantly outperforming well-funded, heavily engineered Euclidean LLMs trained on significantly larger datasets. The work still shows promising results for a specific niche within the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reflect, Retry, Reward: Self-Improving LLMs via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24726v1)
- **Authors**: Shelly Bensal, Umar Jamil, Christopher Bryant, Melisa Russak, Kiran Kamble, Dmytro Mozolevskyi, Muayad Ali, Waseem AlShikh
- **Abstract**: We explore a method for improving the performance of large language models through self-reflection and reinforcement learning. By incentivizing the model to generate better self-reflections when it answers incorrectly, we demonstrate that a model's ability to solve complex, verifiable tasks can be enhanced even when generating synthetic data is infeasible and only binary feedback is available. Our framework operates in two stages: first, upon failing a given task, the model generates a self-reflective commentary analyzing its previous attempt; second, the model is given another attempt at the task with the self-reflection in context. If the subsequent attempt succeeds, the tokens generated during the self-reflection phase are rewarded. Our experimental results show substantial performance gains across a variety of model architectures, as high as 34.7% improvement at math equation writing and 18.1% improvement at function calling. Notably, smaller fine-tuned models (1.5 billion to 7 billion parameters) outperform models in the same family that are 10 times larger. Our novel paradigm is thus an exciting pathway to more useful and reliable language models that can self-improve on challenging tasks with limited external feedback.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel "Reflect, Retry, Reward" framework designed to improve the performance of large language models (LLMs) on complex tasks with limited external feedback. The core idea is to incentivize the model to generate better self-reflections when it fails to complete a task. The framework operates in two stages: (1) upon failure, the model generates a self-reflective commentary, and (2) the model attempts the task again with the self-reflection as context.  If the second attempt succeeds, the tokens generated during the self-reflection phase are rewarded using reinforcement learning (specifically, Group Relative Policy Optimization - GRPO). This approach aims to improve task performance without relying on large, task-specific datasets or synthetic data generation, instead focusing on optimizing the model's ability to learn from its mistakes. The authors demonstrate the framework's effectiveness on function calling and math equation tasks, achieving substantial performance gains and outperforming larger, untrained models in some cases. They also highlight the potential for more generalizable self-reflection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a reasonably novel approach. While self-reflection and RL are not individually new concepts in the LLM space, the combination of incentivizing better self-reflections through reinforcement learning *specifically* when a task *fails* and *only rewarding the self-reflection tokens* (rather than the whole output) is a clear and well-defined contribution. This is notably different from typical RL fine-tuning, which often targets general improvement. The emphasis on a binary success/failure signal is also a practical and valuable aspect. This approach moves beyond just prompting for self-reflection and creates a mechanism for the model to *learn* how to reflect effectively.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to improve LLM performance on challenging tasks with limited supervision. The method is task-agnostic in that it improves the model's ability to reflect on mistakes rather than specializing in one specific skill. It is important that this method improves task performance without large amounts of specific data or external LLMs. Overcoming the data bottleneck is a critical issue, particularly for niche or complex tasks where creating large, high-quality datasets is expensive or infeasible. The demonstrated performance improvements, especially the outperformance of larger models, suggest this approach is promising. The finding that the model benefits even when succeeding at the first attempt after training suggests some degree of generalization in reasoning skills. It potentially enhances robustness and helps overcome blind spots in reasoning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The "Reflect, Retry, Reward" framework is clearly described and easy to understand.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides solid experimental evidence on two distinct tasks (function calling and math equations) and across various model architectures. The performance gains are significant and consistently demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework. Error analyses provide details regarding improvements.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The approach uses only binary feedback signals, which makes it applicable to a wide range of tasks where success can be easily verified. The method doesn't require synthetic data. This makes it scalable and practical for real-world applications.
    *   **Identified Generalization Benefits:** The observation that self-reflection improves task performance even when the reflection is not utilized (due to first-try success) suggests a valuable side effect of improved reasoning skills.
    *   **Identified a task specific need:** The paper identifies a setting in which more concise feedback is desired compared to more verbose feedback.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the two tasks are relevant, it would be beneficial to demonstrate the approach on a wider variety of more complex and diverse tasks to further validate its general applicability.
    *   **Reliance on Binary Feedback:** The reliance on a binary success/failure validator could be a limitation for tasks where success is more nuanced or subjective. The authors do acknowledge that a large LLM can be used as the judge in such scenarios, however this may introduce additional biases.
    *   **Model Limitations:** The authors noted that low capability models were unable to perform well. This means that this method can't act as a bootstrap for weak models, but rather as a tool to further enhance mid and high tier LLMs.
    *   **Incremental Improvement over existing methods:** The method is built on previously existing concepts like reinforcement learning and prompting techniques like chain-of-thought.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence research directions in several ways:
    *   **More efficient fine-tuning:**  It encourages researchers to explore methods for fine-tuning LLMs more efficiently, focusing on learning from mistakes rather than relying solely on massive datasets.
    *   **Self-reflection as a training objective:** It highlights the importance of self-reflection as a valuable training objective and provides a practical framework for achieving it.
    *   **Development of better evaluation metrics:** The focus on binary feedback could spur the development of more robust and scalable evaluation metrics for complex tasks.
    *   **Better generalist models:** Improves the general reasoning capability of models.
    *   **More general models through a task agnostic method:** Improves models without specializing in a particular task.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a significant, innovative, and practical approach to improving LLM performance. The "Reflect, Retry, Reward" framework is well-defined, theoretically sound, and empirically validated. While the scope of evaluation could be broader and the reliance on binary feedback is a potential limitation, the paper presents a compelling case for the effectiveness of the proposed method. The clear methodology, strong results, and generalizable benefits outweigh the weaknesses. The potential influence on research directions in LLM training and evaluation makes this a valuable contribution to the field. This method may not be applicable to all scenarios, but the simplicity and effectiveness make this a novel and significant contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SUMO: Subspace-Aware Moment-Orthogonalization for Accelerating Memory-Efficient LLM Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24749v1)
- **Authors**: Yehonathan Refael, Guy Smorodinsky, Tom Tirer, Ofir Lindenbaum
- **Abstract**: Low-rank gradient-based optimization methods have significantly improved memory efficiency during the training of large language models (LLMs), enabling operations within constrained hardware without sacrificing performance. However, these methods primarily emphasize memory savings, often overlooking potential acceleration in convergence due to their reliance on standard isotropic steepest descent techniques, which can perform suboptimally in the highly anisotropic landscapes typical of deep networks, particularly LLMs. In this paper, we propose SUMO (Subspace-Aware Moment-Orthogonalization), an optimizer that employs exact singular value decomposition (SVD) for moment orthogonalization within a dynamically adapted low-dimensional subspace, enabling norm-inducing steepest descent optimization steps. By explicitly aligning optimization steps with the spectral characteristics of the loss landscape, SUMO effectively mitigates approximation errors associated with commonly used methods like Newton-Schulz orthogonalization approximation. We theoretically establish an upper bound on these approximation errors, proving their dependence on the condition numbers of moments, conditions we analytically demonstrate are encountered during LLM training. Furthermore, we both theoretically and empirically illustrate that exact orthogonalization via SVD substantially improves convergence rates while reducing overall complexity. Empirical evaluations confirm that SUMO accelerates convergence, enhances stability, improves performance, and reduces memory requirements by up to 20% compared to state-of-the-art methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SUMO (Subspace-Aware Moment-Orthogonalization), a new optimizer designed to accelerate memory-efficient training of large language models (LLMs). SUMO leverages the low-rank structure of gradients, a property commonly observed during LLM training, to perform moment orthogonalization within a dynamically adapted low-dimensional subspace using exact Singular Value Decomposition (SVD).  The authors argue that existing low-rank optimizers often rely on standard steepest descent methods which can be suboptimal in the anisotropic loss landscapes of LLMs. SUMO aims to address this by aligning optimization steps with the spectral characteristics of the loss landscape. The paper provides theoretical analysis, demonstrating that approximating moment orthogonalization with methods like Newton-Schulz can introduce errors that depend on the condition number of the moment matrix (a situation common in LLM training). The authors empirically demonstrate that SUMO accelerates convergence, enhances stability, improves performance, and reduces memory compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the combination of exact SVD-based moment orthogonalization with adaptive low-rank subspace optimization for LLMs. While low-rank optimization and gradient orthogonalization are not entirely new concepts in deep learning, the paper convincingly argues and demonstrates that applying exact SVD within an adaptively chosen low-rank subspace offers practical benefits over approximate orthogonalization methods (like Newton-Schulz) that are computationally cheaper but can accumulate errors, especially in ill-conditioned scenarios. The theoretical error bound on Newton-Schulz approximation provides a solid analytical foundation.

*   **Significance:**  The significance stems from the potential to improve the training efficiency and stability of LLMs, especially in resource-constrained environments. The demonstrated reduction in memory requirements, coupled with accelerated convergence, makes LLM training more accessible and potentially faster. This could have a significant impact on the development and deployment of LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a clear theoretical analysis of the limitations of approximate moment orthogonalization methods, motivating the use of exact SVD.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The experimental results demonstrate the practical benefits of SUMO across various LLM tasks, including fine-tuning and pre-training.
    *   **Addresses a Practical Problem:**  The optimizer directly addresses a key challenge in LLM training: the need for efficient and stable optimization, particularly when memory is a bottleneck.
    *   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is well-written and clearly explains the algorithm and its theoretical underpinnings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Complexity:**  While the paper argues for the feasibility of SVD in low-rank subspaces, the computational cost of SVD remains a concern, particularly as model and subspace sizes increase. Further analysis of the scalability of SUMO is needed.  While they mentioned Randomized-SVD, some experiments with different values of `r` and K (subspace update frequency) and profiling might provide more comprehensive evidence and better guidance for choosing `r` and `K`
    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** Although the paper includes evaluations on GLUE and pre-training, more extensive experiments across a wider range of model sizes, datasets, and hardware configurations would strengthen the empirical validation.
    *   **Comparison to Other Recent Methods:** The field of memory-efficient LLM training is rapidly evolving. The paper would benefit from a more detailed comparison to other very recent methods, especially those that also consider geometric optimization.

*   **Potential Influence:**  If the claims hold up under broader scrutiny and further scalability studies, SUMO could become a valuable tool for LLM practitioners, contributing to faster and more efficient training.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper presents a solid and novel approach to LLM optimization. The theoretical analysis provides a strong justification for the proposed method, and the empirical results demonstrate its practical advantages. While the computational cost of SVD and the limited scope of experiments are weaknesses, the potential for SUMO to improve LLM training efficiency and stability warrants a relatively high score.  The weaknesses are significant but not fatal flaws. With further refinement and more extensive validation, this work could have a lasting impact.

- **Score**: 7/10

### LGAR: Zero-Shot LLM-Guided Neural Ranking for Abstract Screening in Systematic Literature Reviews
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24757v1)
- **Authors**: Christian Jaumann, Andreas Wiedholz, Annemarie Friedrich
- **Abstract**: The scientific literature is growing rapidly, making it hard to keep track of the state-of-the-art. Systematic literature reviews (SLRs) aim to identify and evaluate all relevant papers on a topic. After retrieving a set of candidate papers, the abstract screening phase determines initial relevance. To date, abstract screening methods using large language models (LLMs) focus on binary classification settings; existing question answering (QA) based ranking approaches suffer from error propagation. LLMs offer a unique opportunity to evaluate the SLR's inclusion and exclusion criteria, yet, existing benchmarks do not provide them exhaustively. We manually extract these criteria as well as research questions for 57 SLRs, mostly in the medical domain, enabling principled comparisons between approaches. Moreover, we propose LGAR, a zero-shot LLM Guided Abstract Ranker composed of an LLM based graded relevance scorer and a dense re-ranker. Our extensive experiments show that LGAR outperforms existing QA-based methods by 5-10 pp. in mean average precision. Our code and data is publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LGAR: Zero-Shot LLM-Guided Neural Ranking for Abstract Screening in Systematic Literature Reviews":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LGAR, a novel zero-shot Large Language Model (LLM)-Guided Abstract Ranker designed to improve the abstract screening phase of Systematic Literature Reviews (SLRs).  LGAR employs a two-stage ranking approach:  first, an LLM assigns graded relevance scores based on the SLR's inclusion/exclusion criteria and research questions. Second, a dense neural re-ranker orders papers within each score group to produce a final ranked list. The authors also contribute a manually curated dataset extension of 57 SLRs with exhaustive inclusion/exclusion criteria and research questions, addressing a gap in existing benchmarks. Experimental results demonstrate that LGAR outperforms existing Question Answering (QA)-based ranking methods by 5-10 percentage points in mean average precision (MAP).

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach, especially in the context of systematic literature review automation.  While LLMs have been applied to abstract screening before, the two-stage architecture of LGAR, combining graded relevance scoring with dense re-ranking, is a unique contribution. The manual creation and validation of the comprehensive inclusion/exclusion criteria and research question dataset is another significant contribution. This dataset addresses a crucial limitation of existing SLR benchmarks, enabling more realistic and rigorous evaluation of LLM-based ranking methods.  The discovery that larger relevance scales (up to 20 options) are more effective for this task than general ranking tasks is a novel finding.

**Significance:**

The potential impact of LGAR on the efficiency and effectiveness of SLRs is considerable. Abstract screening is a time-consuming and resource-intensive process. By automating this phase, LGAR could significantly reduce the workload for researchers, allowing them to focus on other critical aspects of the review.  The zero-shot nature of the method is another strength. It removes the need for task-specific fine-tuning, allowing the model to be applied to new SLRs without requiring labeled training data. However, this also means it is susceptible to biases present in the pre-trained LLM.

**Strengths:**

*   **Two-Stage Architecture:** The combination of graded relevance scoring and dense re-ranking is well-motivated and demonstrates strong empirical performance.
*   **Comprehensive Dataset Extension:** The manual extraction and validation of inclusion/exclusion criteria and research questions address a critical gap in existing SLR benchmarks, enabling more realistic and rigorous evaluation.
*   **Zero-Shot Approach:**  The model requires no task-specific fine-tuning, making it readily applicable to new SLRs.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** LGAR consistently outperforms existing QA-based methods.
*   **Thorough Ablation Study:** The authors perform a detailed ablation study, exploring the impact of different model components, prompting techniques, and scale sizes. This helps to understand the inner workings of the model and identify the most important factors.
*   **Focus on Open-Weight Models:** By primarily using open-weight LLMs, the authors ensure that the work is reproducible and accessible.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Domain Diversity:** The evaluation is primarily focused on SLRs in the medical domain. While medicine is a major application area for SLRs, it would be beneficial to evaluate the model on SLRs in other fields.
*   **Potential for LLM Bias:** The zero-shot nature of the model means it is susceptible to biases present in the pre-trained LLM. Further investigation into potential biases and mitigation strategies would be beneficial.
*   **Reliance on LLM Quality:** The performance of LGAR is heavily dependent on the quality of the underlying LLM. Any limitations or biases in the LLM will likely be reflected in the performance of LGAR.
*   **Abstract-only screening:** The model makes decisions based on the abstract, but it still needs to be validated with the full paper which could be time-consuming.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence future research in SLR automation. The LGAR architecture provides a promising framework for integrating LLMs into the abstract screening process. The dataset extension will likely become a valuable resource for the community, enabling more rigorous and realistic evaluation of LLM-based ranking methods.  The finding that larger relevance scales are beneficial for abstract screening may also inform future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the identified limitations, the paper presents a novel approach with significant potential to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of SLRs.  The curated dataset and thorough experimental results strengthen the paper's contribution. The weakness regarding the domain focus is less critical, and the zero-shot element outweighs the LLM bias to some extent.

**Score: 7.5**

This score reflects the paper's novelty and practical significance, while acknowledging the limitations mentioned above. It constitutes a solid contribution to the field of information retrieval and systematic review automation.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A survey of using EHR as real-world evidence for discovering and validating new drug indications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24767v1)
- **Authors**: Nabasmita Talukdar, Xiaodan Zhang, Shreya Paithankar, Hui Wang, Bin Chen
- **Abstract**: Electronic Health Records (EHRs) have been increasingly used as real-world evidence (RWE) to support the discovery and validation of new drug indications. This paper surveys current approaches to EHR-based drug repurposing, covering data sources, processing methodologies, and representation techniques. It discusses study designs and statistical frameworks for evaluating drug efficacy. Key challenges in validation are discussed, with emphasis on the role of large language models (LLMs) and target trial emulation. By synthesizing recent developments and methodological advances, this work provides a foundational resource for researchers aiming to translate real-world data into actionable drug-repurposing evidence.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided text, "A survey of using EHR as real-world evidence for discovering and validating new drug indications."

**Summary:**

The paper presents a survey of current approaches for using Electronic Health Records (EHRs) as real-world evidence (RWE) in drug repurposing. It covers a range of topics, including data sources, preprocessing methodologies, representation techniques, study designs, and statistical frameworks for evaluating drug efficacy. It also addresses key challenges in validation, with a particular focus on the role of large language models (LLMs) and target trial emulation. The authors aim to provide a comprehensive resource for researchers looking to translate real-world data into actionable drug repurposing evidence. The paper discusses various databases, data standards, NLP techniques, ML techniques for phenotyping and statistical methods commonly applied in this space. It concludes with perspectives on the future of the field with a special emphasis on leveraging LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The paper provides a fairly broad overview of the drug repurposing pipeline, covering a wide range of topics from data acquisition and preprocessing to statistical analysis and advanced techniques like LLMs. It serves as a good starting point for researchers entering the field or those looking for a refresher on different aspects.
    *   **Practical Focus:** By focusing on EHR-based drug repurposing, the survey directly addresses a timely and relevant area of research with the increasing availability of real-world data and the ongoing quest for cheaper drug development.
    *   **Emphasis on Challenges:** The paper acknowledges the challenges associated with using EHR data, including data quality issues, the presence of confounders, and limitations in generalizability. This provides a more realistic perspective on the promises and limitations of the RWE-based approach.
    *   **LLM and Emulation Studies Discussion:** The emphasis on LLMs and emulation studies highlights emerging trends that could significantly impact the field. However, it remains conceptual and doesn't offer concrete, proven solutions, pointing to areas of future research.
    *   Clear and structured writing; provides a good overview of the field to researchers and practitioners alike.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Significant Novelty:** While the paper is a useful survey, it does not present substantially new or original research. It mainly synthesizes existing knowledge.
    *   **Depth vs. Breadth Trade-off:** The breadth of coverage means that certain topics might be treated superficially. More in-depth discussions on specific statistical methods or NLP techniques would have been beneficial.
    *   **Limited Critical Analysis:** Although the paper mentions challenges, it could benefit from a more rigorous and critical analysis of the strengths and weaknesses of different methodologies and databases. For instance, a head-to-head comparison of specific databases with clear metrics would improve the utility of the survey.
    *   **Over-optimistic on LLMs:** The paper expresses optimism about the potential of LLMs without fully acknowledging the significant issues of biases, hallucinations, and computational resources required for these models to be effectively employed in clinical research.
    *   **Limited examples**: The paper is a survey, but it could be strengthened with additional concrete examples of how LLMs or the discussed methodologies have been successfully implemented in real-world drug repurposing efforts.
    *   Over-reliance on commercial/private sources

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper could serve as a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners interested in using EHRs for drug repurposing. It provides a good overview of the field and highlights key challenges and opportunities.
    *   By emphasizing emerging techniques like LLMs and target trial emulation, it might encourage further research and development in these areas.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is valuable as a survey and provides a helpful overview of the drug repurposing field, particularly its application to EHR data. However, it lacks significant novelty and critical depth. While it highlights emerging trends and challenges, it doesn't provide groundbreaking insights or solutions.

**Score: 6/10**

- **Classification**: stat.AP
- **Score**: 6/10

### Generalization Dynamics of Linear Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24769v1)
- **Authors**: Claudia Merger, Sebastian Goldt
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models trained on finite datasets with $N$ samples from a target distribution exhibit a transition from memorisation, where the model reproduces training examples, to generalisation, where it produces novel samples that reflect the underlying data distribution. Understanding this transition is key to characterising the sample efficiency and reliability of generative models, but our theoretical understanding of this transition is incomplete. Here, we analytically study the memorisation-to-generalisation transition in a simple model using linear denoisers, which allow explicit computation of test errors, sampling distributions, and Kullback-Leibler divergences between samples and target distribution. Using these measures, we predict that this transition occurs roughly when $N \asymp d$, the dimension of the inputs. When $N$ is smaller than the dimension of the inputs $d$, so that only a fraction of relevant directions of variation are present in the training data, we demonstrate how both regularization and early stopping help to prevent overfitting. For $N > d$, we find that the sampling distributions of linear diffusion models approach their optimum (measured by the Kullback-Leibler divergence) linearly with $d/N$, independent of the specifics of the data distribution. Our work clarifies how sample complexity governs generalisation in a simple model of diffusion-based generative models and provides insight into the training dynamics of linear denoisers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper analytically studies the memorization-to-generalization transition in diffusion models using a simplified model with linear denoisers.  The authors derive test and training losses, and Kullback-Leibler divergence between the sampling distribution and the target distribution.  They find that overfitting is most prominent when the number of training samples (N) is less than the input dimension (d). In this N<d regime, early stopping and regularization are shown to be effective at preventing overfitting.  For N > d, the Kullback-Leibler divergence decays linearly with d/N, regardless of the data distribution specifics. The paper also investigates how well linear denoisers approximate non-linear denoisers and finds similarities across various diffusion times and data covariance eigenmodes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper provides a solid theoretical analysis of the memorization-generalization transition in diffusion models, focusing on the tractable case of linear denoisers.  While linear network analyses are established in machine learning, its application to diffusion models in such detail concerning the transition from memorization to generalization offers novelty. Specifically, deriving closed-form expressions for test error, sampling distributions, and Kullback-Leibler divergences and connecting these to regularization and early stopping strategies is significant. The finding about the independence of the KL divergence decay (with d/N) from data distribution particulars in the N>d regime is also noteworthy. The observation that linear models have similar behaviour to the highly complex non-linear U-nets for some diffusion times is also interesting.

*   **Significance:** Understanding sample complexity is critical for building efficient and reliable generative models.  This work offers insight into how the training dynamics and architecture interact to affect model generalization in diffusion models. The analytical results provide a baseline against which more complex models can be compared. Connecting data structure to the overfitting regime is particularly relevant. This provides a theoretical rationale for practical strategies used in training diffusion models like regularization and early stopping.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Analytical Tractability:** The use of linear denoisers allows for explicit calculations and a more comprehensive theoretical understanding compared to analyzing directly complex, non-linear models.
    *   **Clear Insights:** The paper effectively relates sample complexity (N vs. d) to the emergence of overfitting and generalization. It highlights the role of regularization and early stopping in mitigating overfitting.
    *   **Connection to Empirical Observations:**  The paper's findings are tied back to empirical results, demonstrating consistency between the theoretical framework and practical phenomena observed in diffusion model training (e.g., Kadkhodaie et al.'s work).
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper studies memorization, generalization, Kullback-Leibler divergence, and alignment with reference models offering a holistic understanding of the training dynamics of diffusion models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplification:** The reliance on linear denoisers is a significant simplification that limits the direct applicability of the findings to state-of-the-art diffusion models that use complex, non-linear architectures. While the paper does touch upon similarities between linear and nonlinear models, this is a high-level comparison, not a rigorous analysis.
    *   **Limited Data Types:** The theory is primarily developed for unstructured data with some discussion on structured images, however, the types of data that are used are limited which reduces the generality of the results.
    *   **Gaussian Assumption:** The Gaussian assumption on the data distribution limits the generalizability of some results. While the paper addresses structured datasets and hierarchical structures, it would be beneficial to explore more complex data distributions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by:
    *   Providing a theoretical foundation for understanding and addressing overfitting in diffusion models.
    *   Guiding the design of more efficient training strategies (e.g., early stopping, regularization) based on the sample complexity regime.
    *   Encouraging further research into the generalization properties of more complex diffusion model architectures.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    The paper contributes non-trivial theoretical insights into the generalization properties of diffusion models, specifically by using linear denoisers. However, the simplified nature of the model represents a considerable limitation. The insights gained from analyzing linear denoisers are valuable as a stepping stone, but their direct applicability to the highly complex non-linear diffusion models commonly used is constrained. The theoretical findings regarding the impact of data structure in the memorization-generalization transition as well as the KL decay offer significant value to the generative modelling community.

### AFLoRA: Adaptive Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models with Resource-Aware Low-Rank Adaption
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24773v1)
- **Authors**: Yajie Zhou, Xiaoyi Pang, Zhibo Wang
- **Abstract**: Federated fine-tuning has emerged as a promising approach to adapt foundation models to downstream tasks using decentralized data. However, real-world deployment remains challenging due to the high computational and communication demands of fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) on clients with data and system resources that are heterogeneous and constrained. In such settings, the global model's performance is often bottlenecked by the weakest clients and further degraded by the non-IID nature of local data. Although existing methods leverage parameter-efficient techniques such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to reduce communication and computation overhead, they often fail to simultaneously ensure accurate aggregation of low-rank updates and maintain low system costs, thereby hindering overall performance. To address these challenges, we propose AFLoRA, an adaptive and lightweight federated fine-tuning framework for LLMs. AFLoRA decouples shared and client-specific updates to reduce overhead and improve aggregation accuracy, incorporates diagonal matrix-based rank pruning to better utilize local resources, and employs rank-aware aggregation with public data refinement to strengthen generalization under data heterogeneity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AFLoRA outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both accuracy and efficiency, providing a practical solution for efficient LLM adaptation in heterogeneous environments in the real world.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the AFLORA paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AFLORA, an adaptive federated fine-tuning framework for Large Language Models (LLMs) designed to address the challenges of resource heterogeneity and non-IID data distributions in federated learning environments. AFLORA employs a structurally decoupled and dynamically rank-aware Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) approach. Key features include:

*   **Decoupled Updates:** Separates the optimization of LoRA matrices, assigning the B matrix to client-side updates for personalized knowledge and the A matrix to server-side refinement for generalization.
*   **Diagonal-Based Adaptive Rank:** Inserts a learnable diagonal matrix between A and B to modulate directional information flow, enabling dynamic rank control and compression.
*   **Heterogeneous Aggregation with Refinement:** Aggregates LoRA modules using zero-padding and weighted aggregation, followed by public data refinement on the server to harmonize updates.

The authors present experimental results demonstrating that AFLORA outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of accuracy and efficiency in various federated learning scenarios with diverse datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper combines several existing ideas in a novel way. The use of LoRA in federated learning is well-established, but the specific decoupling of the A and B matrices and the introduction of the diagonal rank-adaptive mechanism are interesting modifications. The aggregation strategy, including the public data refinement, appears to be a useful addition to handle Non-IID data. The use of SVD-inspired components for rank adaption provides a theoretical basis for the method.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a very practical and important problem: enabling efficient and accurate fine-tuning of LLMs in resource-constrained and heterogeneous federated environments. The benefits of AFLORA for heterogeneous clients appears significant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a critical gap in the federated learning literature, specifically focusing on efficient LLM adaptation.
    *   The proposed framework is well-designed, with clear motivations for each component.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and settings demonstrates the effectiveness of AFLORA.
    *   The authors clearly articulate the challenges and solutions, providing a solid contribution to the field.
    *   Public refinement has been around for a while, but the way that this is introduced and used with other components is novel.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of the computational overhead of the diagonal rank mechanism, especially concerning its scalability to larger models.
    *   While experiments cover general and domain-specific datasets, more real-world scenarios with highly skewed Non-IID data could further validate the approach.
    *   The sensitivity of AFLORA to hyperparameters (e.g., *a* for fusion, *ß* for pruning) should be discussed in more detail.
    *   While the paper provides detailed experiments comparing to standard techniques, it does not provide an ablation study, meaning that it is unclear which pieces of AFLORA are most important.

*   **Potential Impact:** AFLORA has the potential to become a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners working on federated learning with LLMs. The adaptive and lightweight nature of the framework makes it suitable for deployment in various edge environments. The insights from this work could stimulate further research on adaptive and resource-efficient federated learning algorithms. It could contribute to the wider adoption of federated learning for LLMs in privacy-sensitive applications.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper combines existing ideas in a clever, original way, and is well-written and easy to understand. While it does not revolutionize the field, it offers a practical and well-validated solution to an important problem. The limitations are addressed above.
