### Vision LLMs Are Bad at Hierarchical Visual Understanding, and LLMs Are the Bottleneck
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24840v1)
- **Authors**: Yuwen Tan, Yuan Qing, Boqing Gong
- **Abstract**: This paper reveals that many state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) lack hierarchical knowledge about our visual world, unaware of even well-established biology taxonomies. This shortcoming makes LLMs a bottleneck for vision LLMs' hierarchical visual understanding (e.g., recognizing Anemone Fish but not Vertebrate). We arrive at these findings using about one million four-choice visual question answering (VQA) tasks constructed from six taxonomies and four image datasets. Interestingly, finetuning a vision LLM using our VQA tasks reaffirms LLMs' bottleneck effect to some extent because the VQA tasks improve the LLM's hierarchical consistency more than the vision LLM's. We conjecture that one cannot make vision LLMs understand visual concepts fully hierarchical until LLMs possess corresponding taxonomy knowledge.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll summarize the paper, critically evaluate its novelty and significance, and assign it a score with a well-reasoned justification.

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the hierarchical visual understanding capabilities of Vision Large Language Models (VLLMs). The authors find that many state-of-the-art VLLMs struggle with tasks requiring hierarchical knowledge, even on well-established taxonomies like biology classifications. They attribute this limitation to the underlying Large Language Models (LLMs) lacking sufficient knowledge of these taxonomies. They demonstrate this bottleneck effect through a series of experiments using four-choice visual question answering (VQA) tasks constructed from six taxonomies and four image datasets. Their experiments show that while VLLMs can extract discriminative visual features, the LLM component fails to consistently decode and apply hierarchical relationships. Further, they show that fine-tuning improves the LLM's textual hierarchy consistency *more* than the VLLM's visual consistency, reinforcing their argument that the LLM is the bottleneck.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper addresses a crucial aspect of visual understanding that has been relatively under-explored in the context of VLLMs: hierarchical consistency. While previous works have pointed out limitations in fine-grained classification and open-world capabilities, this paper focuses specifically on the ability of VLLMs to reason *hierarchically* across different levels of abstraction within a taxonomy. This is important because many real-world applications require systems to understand not just the specific identity of an object, but also its broader category and relationships to other objects.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of hierarchical visual understanding and explains its importance.
*   **Well-Designed Experiments:** The authors carefully construct VQA tasks and designed probing experiments to isolate the cause of VLLM failure. The experiments are rigorous and provide strong evidence for their claims. The creation of about a million four-choice questions across diverse taxonomic datasets is a significant contribution in itself.
*   **Convincing Argument:** The evidence presented strongly supports the claim that LLMs are the bottleneck for hierarchical visual understanding in VLLMs. They convincingly show that visual encoders can extract the necessary features, but the LLM component lacks sufficient taxonomic knowledge to reason correctly. The finetuning experiment, showing improved textual vs. visual consistency, provides particularly compelling support.
*   **Focus on Consistency:** The paper places appropriate emphasis on *hierarchical consistency* as the primary evaluation metric, rather than simply focusing on leaf-level accuracy, which can be misleading.
*   **Reproducibility:** The code and data will be released, ensuring that others can reproduce and build upon their findings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Modalities and Taxonomies**: The work primarily focuses on taxonomies of visual concepts, like biological categories and common artifacts. The extent to which their findings generalize to other forms of knowledge (e.g. complex event sequences, social hierarchies) or modalities (e.g., audio) remains unclear.
*   **Limited Model Diversity:** While a range of models is evaluated, a more extensive evaluation across *proprietary* LLMs (specifically analyzing their internal workings) would strengthen the conclusion that LLMs, in general, are the bottleneck. The limitation of only partially analyzing one proprietary LLM (GPT-4o) hurts the generalizability of the bottleneck finding across the field.
*   **Limited Exploration of Mitigation Strategies:** The paper demonstrates the problem effectively, but spends relatively little effort exploring or evaluating potential mitigation strategies *beyond* fine-tuning, which they acknowledge as likely insufficient. While this is understandable, more exploration in that direction would have increased the paper's impact.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it identifies a fundamental limitation in current VLLMs that needs to be addressed for them to achieve true general-purpose visual understanding. Their findings have implications for how VLLMs are pre-trained, fine-tuned, and evaluated. It highlights the need for more research into how to imbue LLMs with structured knowledge and the ability to reason hierarchically. Their observations also create research opportunities for test-time adaptation algorithms which improve performance by increasing consistency without further training.

**Justification for Score:**

I am giving this paper a score of **7**. While the findings are important and well-supported by the experiments, the limitations outlined above prevent it from being considered a truly exceptional contribution (9 or 10). The focus is relatively narrow and model diversity could be improved. It does provide a sound argument and a novel demonstration of this argument.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Chameleon: A Flexible Data-mixing Framework for Language Model Pretraining and Finetuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24844v1)
- **Authors**: Wanyun Xie, Francesco Tonin, Volkan Cevher
- **Abstract**: Training data mixtures greatly impact the generalization performance of large language models. Existing domain reweighting methods often rely on costly weight computations and require retraining when new data is introduced. To this end, we introduce a flexible and efficient data mixing framework, Chameleon, that employs leverage scores to quantify domain importance within a learned embedding space. We first construct a domain affinity matrix over domain embeddings. The induced leverage scores determine a mixture that upweights domains sharing common representations in embedding space. This formulation allows direct transfer to new data by computing the new domain embeddings. In experiments, we demonstrate improvements over three key scenarios: (i) our computed weights improve performance on pretraining domains with a fraction of the compute of existing methods; (ii) Chameleon can adapt to data changes without proxy retraining, boosting few-shot reasoning accuracies when transferred to new data; (iii) our method enables efficient domain reweighting in finetuning, consistently improving test perplexity on all finetuning domains over uniform mixture. Our code is available at https://github.com/LIONS-EPFL/Chameleon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CHAMELEON: A Flexible Data-mixing Framework for Language Model Pretraining and Finetuning."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CHAMELEON, a new framework for data mixing in language model pretraining and finetuning.  CHAMELEON leverages kernel ridge leverage scores (KRLS) to quantify the importance of different data domains within a learned embedding space. The framework constructs a domain affinity matrix based on domain embeddings and computes leverage scores to guide domain reweighting.  Key features of CHAMELEON include its efficiency (low computational cost), adaptability to new data without retraining proxy models, and applicability to both pretraining and finetuning.  Experiments demonstrate improved performance over existing methods like DoReMi and DoGE on pretraining and perplexity improvements during finetuning across various datasets. The authors claim CHAMELEON scales effectively to larger datasets and maintains stability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the use of KRLS on learned domain embeddings as a *direct* and efficient way to determine data domain weights for LLM training.  While domain reweighting itself is not novel (DoReMi, DoGE exist),  CHAMELEON's approach aims to decouple domain weight computation from iterative proxy model training, offering advantages in adaptability and efficiency. Applying KRLS to learned embeddings is a clever method for capturing domain relatedness directly in the learned representation space. It simplifies the weight calculation and offers a different perspective compared to optimization-based approaches like DoReMi or gradient-based approaches like DoGE. The explicit leveraging of inverse KRLS during pretraining for general knowledge and KRLS during finetuning for task-specificity seems a sensible adaptation.

**Significance:**

The potential significance stems from the claimed improvements in efficiency and scalability. If CHAMELEON can achieve comparable or better performance than existing methods with a significantly reduced computational cost, it could make advanced data mixing techniques more accessible to researchers with limited resources. The adaptability to new data without retraining proxy models is a significant advantage in dynamic data environments. Furthermore, CHAMELEON's application to both pretraining and finetuning provides a unified framework, which can be very practical. The paper also importantly shows CHAMELEON performs competitively to RegMix, while not being task aware.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing domain reweighting methods.
*   **Efficient Approach:** KRLS-based approach offers compelling potential for computational efficiency.
*   **Adaptability:**  The framework's ability to adapt to new data without retraining proxy models is a valuable contribution.
*   **Unified Framework:** Extends domain reweighting to both pretraining and finetuning stages.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental section demonstrates CHAMELEON's effectiveness on universal generalization and downstream tasks. It backs up all claims with substantial quantitative results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Proxy Model:** CHAMELEON still relies on a proxy model to generate embeddings. Although it avoids retraining this model for data modifications, the quality of the initial proxy model remains crucial. It would be helpful to have more discussion on the impact of the proxy model’s architecture and training.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper provides some theoretical motivation, a more in-depth analysis of the KRLS properties and their influence on domain reweighting would strengthen the theoretical foundation. The discussion about regularization needs expanding. The role of the regularization parameter requires a much more thorough motivation and explanation.
*   **Scalability beyond Pile:** The demonstration of scalability to "Pile" is a good start, but it should be viewed with some caution. While a large dataset, The Pile is still a fixed and well understood dataset. More strenuous stress tests would be more persuasive.
*   **Practicality of the approach:** The framework appears to have several parameters that would require expert knowledge. The temperature used in the softmax normalization step, the proxy size, the layer from which to obtain embeddings all add to the complexity of the approach, and make it more difficult to readily apply.

**Potential Influence:**

CHAMELEON's ability to reduce the computational burden associated with domain reweighting and scale to new data could potentially influence future research in large language model pretraining and finetuning, particularly for researchers and labs with limited computational resources. It also showcases the potential of spectral methods for data management in LLMs.

**Score Justification:**

I assign a score of **7**. While the paper tackles an important problem and presents a novel and efficient approach, there are some significant limitations. The reliance on the proxy model, the need for more theoretical understanding of KRLS-based weight determination, and limited stress-testing of scalability are all things holding it back. However, the results are clear and the claims are adequately backed up by experiments. The potential for significant computational savings and increased adaptability make this a valuable contribution to the field and justifies a score of 7.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MiCRo: Mixture Modeling and Context-aware Routing for Personalized Preference Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24846v1)
- **Authors**: Jingyan Shen, Jiarui Yao, Rui Yang, Yifan Sun, Feng Luo, Rui Pan, Tong Zhang, Han Zhao
- **Abstract**: Reward modeling is a key step in building safe foundation models when applying reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) to align Large Language Models (LLMs). However, reward modeling based on the Bradley-Terry (BT) model assumes a global reward function, failing to capture the inherently diverse and heterogeneous human preferences. Hence, such oversimplification limits LLMs from supporting personalization and pluralistic alignment. Theoretically, we show that when human preferences follow a mixture distribution of diverse subgroups, a single BT model has an irreducible error. While existing solutions, such as multi-objective learning with fine-grained annotations, help address this issue, they are costly and constrained by predefined attributes, failing to fully capture the richness of human values. In this work, we introduce MiCRo, a two-stage framework that enhances personalized preference learning by leveraging large-scale binary preference datasets without requiring explicit fine-grained annotations. In the first stage, MiCRo introduces context-aware mixture modeling approach to capture diverse human preferences. In the second stage, MiCRo integrates an online routing strategy that dynamically adapts mixture weights based on specific context to resolve ambiguity, allowing for efficient and scalable preference adaptation with minimal additional supervision. Experiments on multiple preference datasets demonstrate that MiCRo effectively captures diverse human preferences and significantly improves downstream personalization.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "MiCRo: Mixture Modeling and Context-aware Routing for Personalized Preference Learning" introduces a two-stage framework to improve personalization in reward modeling for Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Recognizing the limitations of a single global reward function due to diverse human preferences, the paper proposes MiCRo. The first stage uses context-aware mixture modeling to capture diverse preferences by learning multiple latent reward functions. The second stage employs an online routing strategy that dynamically adjusts the weights of these reward functions based on the context provided by the user. This enables efficient adaptation to individual preferences with minimal additional supervision. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of MiCRo through experiments on multiple preference datasets, showing that it captures diverse human preferences and enhances downstream personalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The novelty of the paper lies in its combination of context-aware mixture modeling and online routing for personalized preference learning.  While mixture modeling for preferences and online adaptation are not entirely new individually, the specific two-stage framework, the incorporation of context into the mixture model weighting, and the lightweight online routing strategy contribute to a unique approach. The theoretical result on the irreducible error of a single BT model under a mixture of preference distributions provides a strong justification for the approach. However, the approach is more incremental rather than revolutionary.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation of standard RLHF – the assumption of a homogenous user preference. Personalized preference learning is essential for creating more adaptable and user-centric AI systems. MiCRo offers a practical solution that can be implemented with existing binary preference datasets and requires limited additional supervision for adaptation. The results showing improved performance on multiple datasets with context awareness suggests potential real-world impact.  The experiments comparing MiCRo with existing methods (static mixtures, ensembles, full supervision) strengthen its claims. However, the contribution feels heavily incremental compared to HyRe and ARMO.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Theoretical Justification:** The theoretical analysis clearly demonstrates the limitations of using a single BT model for diverse populations and motivates the need for mixture modeling.
    * **Practical Approach:**  MiCRo is designed to work with widely available binary preference datasets, making it easier to adopt and scale. The online routing strategy minimizes the need for additional supervision.
    * **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of MiCRo in capturing diverse preferences and improving personalization. The ablation studies provide insights into the impact of key hyperparameters.
    * **Context-Awareness:** Introducing context-awareness significantly elevates the applicability of the approach, reflecting real-world scenarios where user preferences are highly conditional.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Binary Labels:**  While the paper highlights the advantage of using readily available binary preference datasets, the approach may not fully capture the nuances of human preferences compared to methods that utilize richer feedback signals.
    * **Context Quality:**  The effectiveness of the routing strategy depends on the quality and relevance of the context information. The paper acknowledges this limitation but does not thoroughly explore strategies for handling noisy or incomplete contextual signals.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While novel in its combination, the individual components of MiCRo (mixture models, online learning) are well-established.  The performance gains over state-of-the-art approaches seem incremental, and thus its clear that the method still relies heavily on prior information to generate diverse preferences.
    * **Experimental Setup:** The simulated context augmentation for HelpSteer2 introduces a level of artificiality to the experimental setup. A more direct evaluation with datasets explicitly designed for personalization would be more convincing.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper can influence future research on personalized preference learning in RLHF. The framework provides a solid foundation for developing more sophisticated methods for capturing and adapting to diverse human preferences.  The online routing strategy offers a practical approach to personalization that can be readily integrated into existing RLHF pipelines.

* **Score Justification:** The paper presents a well-motivated and validated approach to personalized preference learning. However, the novelty and impact are somewhat limited by the reliance on existing techniques and the reliance on labeled preference data. While the empirical results are positive, the magnitude of improvement is not overwhelming. Given these factors, I assign a score of 6.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Accelerated Sampling from Masked Diffusion Models via Entropy Bounded Unmasking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24857v1)
- **Authors**: Heli Ben-Hamu, Itai Gat, Daniel Severo, Niklas Nolte, Brian Karrer
- **Abstract**: Recent masked diffusion models (MDMs) have shown competitive performance compared to autoregressive models (ARMs) for language modeling. While most literature has focused on performance enhancing sampling procedures, efficient sampling from MDMs has been scarcely explored. We make the observation that often a given sequence of partially masked tokens determines the values of multiple unknown tokens deterministically, meaning that a single prediction of a masked model holds additional information unused by standard sampling procedures. Based on this observation, we introduce EB-Sampler, a simple drop-in replacement for existing samplers, utilizing an Entropy Bounded unmasking procedure that dynamically unmasks multiple tokens in one function evaluation with predefined approximate error tolerance. We formulate the EB-Sampler as part of a broad family of adaptive samplers for which we provide an error analysis that motivates our algorithmic choices. EB-Sampler accelerates sampling from current state of the art MDMs by roughly 2-3x on standard coding and math reasoning benchmarks without loss in performance. We also validate the same procedure works well on smaller reasoning tasks including maze navigation and Sudoku, tasks ARMs often struggle with.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "EB-Sampler," a novel sampling method for Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs). EB-Sampler is designed to improve sampling efficiency without sacrificing performance by dynamically unmasking multiple tokens at each step. It leverages two key observations: 1) model-determined unmasking orders tend to be more accurate and 2) multiple masked tokens are often predicted with high certainty (low entropy). EB-Sampler uses an entropy bound to control the joint dependence of unmasked tokens, allowing it to unmask multiple tokens when confidence is high and error is expected to be low. The authors demonstrate that EB-Sampler accelerates sampling from state-of-the-art MDMs on coding and math reasoning benchmarks, outperforming existing methods like Top-k sampling. The paper also provides a theoretical analysis, framing EB-Sampler within a broader family of adaptive multi-token samplers and deriving an error decomposition that motivates the algorithm.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adaptively unmasking tokens based on entropy is a good one and does offer a definite advantage over existing methods, particularly top-k sampling, which unmasks a fixed number of tokens. The theoretical justification, while building on existing error decomposition techniques, provides a useful framework for understanding the method's behavior. The drop-in replacement aspect is also a strong point.

*   **Significance:** Efficient sampling remains a practical problem for MDMs, which are gaining traction as competitors to autoregressive models. The potential for 2-3x speedup in sampling without sacrificing performance is definitely a valuable contribution. The fact that it works on existing models without retraining is a strong positive. The experiments conducted on several standard benchmarks, including maze navigation and Sudoku add credence to this.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical impact:** EB-Sampler is demonstrated to improve the speed of MDM sampling on challenging tasks.
    *   **Theoretical justification:** The error decomposition framework provides a principled basis for the algorithm's design.
    *   **Drop-in replacement:** EB-Sampler can be easily integrated into existing MDM pipelines.
    *   Good experimental validation on multiple benchmarks.
    *   The connection with rate distortion theory is interesting and insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper provides a solid theoretical grounding, the entropy bound may still require careful tuning. The sensitivity of the method to the threshold parameter γ could be a limitation.
    *   The speedups reported are task-dependent, and it is not clear how well EB-Sampler would generalize to other tasks or model architectures. The bias in NFE count is acknowledged but could be more thoroughly addressed. Semi-AR generation is a very narrow case and may not easily translate to other generation tasks that may require a wider context of the sequence.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on performance improvement. The interpretability of the unmasking process and what that entails could be explored further.
    *   More extensive comparison with other efficient sampling strategies for MDMs (if there are any) could strengthen the paper. This should compare the efficiency gains.
    *   The experiments with margin error proxies yielded inferior performances. It could be useful to understand the causes for these inferior performances and to include that information in the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** EB-Sampler has the potential to become a standard technique for accelerating MDM sampling, as it addresses a critical bottleneck in their deployment. The ideas explored in this paper could stimulate further research into adaptive sampling strategies for MDMs.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

EB-Sampler presents a solid, practical contribution with a clear theoretical foundation. The achieved speedups in sampling are noteworthy, and the drop-in replacement aspect makes it readily usable. However, the limitations related to parameter tuning, task dependence, and potentially limited comparisons with other efficient strategies warrant a more measured evaluation. While it's a significant advancement, further investigation of its robustness and broader applicability would be necessary to justify a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ProRL: Prolonged Reinforcement Learning Expands Reasoning Boundaries in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24864v1)
- **Authors**: Mingjie Liu, Shizhe Diao, Ximing Lu, Jian Hu, Xin Dong, Yejin Choi, Jan Kautz, Yi Dong
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in reasoning-centric language models have highlighted reinforcement learning (RL) as a promising method for aligning models with verifiable rewards. However, it remains contentious whether RL truly expands a model's reasoning capabilities or merely amplifies high-reward outputs already latent in the base model's distribution, and whether continually scaling up RL compute reliably leads to improved reasoning performance. In this work, we challenge prevailing assumptions by demonstrating that prolonged RL (ProRL) training can uncover novel reasoning strategies that are inaccessible to base models, even under extensive sampling. We introduce ProRL, a novel training methodology that incorporates KL divergence control, reference policy resetting, and a diverse suite of tasks. Our empirical analysis reveals that RL-trained models consistently outperform base models across a wide range of pass@k evaluations, including scenarios where base models fail entirely regardless of the number of attempts. We further show that reasoning boundary improvements correlates strongly with task competence of base model and training duration, suggesting that RL can explore and populate new regions of solution space over time. These findings offer new insights into the conditions under which RL meaningfully expands reasoning boundaries in language models and establish a foundation for future work on long-horizon RL for reasoning. We release model weights to support further research: https://huggingface.co/nvidia/Nemotron-Research-Reasoning-Qwen-1.5B
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ProRL: Prolonged Reinforcement Learning Expands Reasoning Boundaries in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ProRL, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) methodology designed to extend RL training periods for large language models (LLMs) and facilitate deeper exploration of reasoning strategies. ProRL incorporates KL divergence control, reference policy resetting, and a diverse task suite. The authors argue that by training for longer durations (2k+ steps) on a varied set of tasks, LLMs can uncover new reasoning strategies that are inaccessible to base models, even under extensive sampling. The developed model, Nemotron-Research-Reasoning-Qwen-1.5B, demonstrates improved performance across a range of benchmarks compared to the base model (DeepSeek-R1-1.5B) and sometimes even surpasses DeepSeek-R1-7B.  Crucially, the paper highlights cases where the base model fails entirely regardless of sampling efforts, while the RL-trained model achieves high success rates, indicating a genuine expansion of the reasoning boundary. The paper also quantifies the novelty of reasoning trajectories using the Creativity Index, finding that prolonged RL training leads to more novel patterns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper challenges the commonly held belief that RL in LLMs primarily amplifies existing capabilities rather than creating new ones. The core novelty lies in the demonstration that *prolonged* RL training, combined with specific techniques like KL divergence control and reference policy resets, is key to truly expanding the reasoning boundaries. While other works have explored RL for LLMs, this paper's emphasis on significantly extended training and the exploration of diverse tasks is a distinguishing factor. The use of the Creativity Index to quantify novelty is also a positive contribution.

*   **Significance:** If the claims hold up under further scrutiny, the paper's significance is substantial.  It suggests a pathway toward developing more capable and generalizable AI systems through sustained RL exploration. This could reshape how we approach aligning LLMs with verifiable rewards and potentially unlock new reasoning capabilities currently beyond reach. The release of model weights is helpful for reproducibility and further research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-motivated problem statement.
    *   Introduction of a practical and effective RL training methodology (ProRL).
    *   Empirical evidence supporting the claim of reasoning boundary expansion (demonstrated in failure case scenarios of base models).
    *   Quantification of reasoning novelty using the Creativity Index.
    *   Evaluation across a diverse set of tasks.
    *   Comparison against strong baselines (DeepSeek-R1-1.5B and 7B).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While diverse, the task suite is still limited compared to the breadth of human reasoning. Could further studies be performed with much more tasks or task types?
    *   Computational cost: The requirement of extensive computational resources for ProRL makes it less accessible to many researchers. The authors acknowledge this but it remains a significant hurdle for broader adoption. The exact cost is hard to determine. How sensitive the method is to hyper-parameters is unclear from the information given.
    *   Limited insights into *why* the specific ProRL components are crucial. While KL divergence control and reference policy resets are motivated, a deeper understanding of their interaction and individual contributions could further strengthen the work. For example: what happens without the use of the creativity index?
    *   The reliance on specific baselines like DeepSeek-R1 is a potential dependency. Generalizability to other base models should be demonstrated.
    *   The paper is "under review". This limits the ability to ensure the accuracy and veracity of these results. However, assuming the claims hold, the points above are valid.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence future research on:
    *   RL training methodologies for LLMs, specifically emphasizing the importance of prolonged training.
    *   Methods for quantifying and evaluating the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.
    *   Alignment strategies for LLMs that aim to expand, not just amplify, existing skills.
    *   Exploring new tasks or training schemes to promote creativity in models.
    * Further analysis on scaling laws and task competence, which could also help address some of the weaknesses listed above.

*   **Score Justification:**

I assign a score of **7.5** to this paper.

While the paper presents compelling evidence and a novel approach, the practical challenges associated with the computational cost and the need for deeper mechanistic understanding of ProRL's components prevents it from being in the top tier. There is also a need to verify that these results and the claims are accurate under further testing. The core idea of prolonged RL is valuable, but its wider applicability and the long-term consequences of such training strategies require further exploration.
Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TalkingHeadBench: A Multi-Modal Benchmark & Analysis of Talking-Head DeepFake Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24866v1)
- **Authors**: Xinqi Xiong, Prakrut Patel, Qingyuan Fan, Amisha Wadhwa, Sarathy Selvam, Xiao Guo, Luchao Qi, Xiaoming Liu, Roni Sengupta
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of talking-head deepfake generation fueled by advanced generative models has elevated the realism of synthetic videos to a level that poses substantial risks in domains such as media, politics, and finance. However, current benchmarks for deepfake talking-head detection fail to reflect this progress, relying on outdated generators and offering limited insight into model robustness and generalization. We introduce TalkingHeadBench, a comprehensive multi-model multi-generator benchmark and curated dataset designed to evaluate the performance of state-of-the-art detectors on the most advanced generators. Our dataset includes deepfakes synthesized by leading academic and commercial models and features carefully constructed protocols to assess generalization under distribution shifts in identity and generator characteristics. We benchmark a diverse set of existing detection methods, including CNNs, vision transformers, and temporal models, and analyze their robustness and generalization capabilities. In addition, we provide error analysis using Grad-CAM visualizations to expose common failure modes and detector biases. TalkingHeadBench is hosted on https://huggingface.co/datasets/luchaoqi/TalkingHeadBench with open access to all data splits and protocols. Our benchmark aims to accelerate research towards more robust and generalizable detection models in the face of rapidly evolving generative techniques.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TalkingHeadBench, a new benchmark dataset and evaluation protocol for talking-head deepfake detection. The benchmark addresses limitations in existing datasets by using state-of-the-art talking-head generators (both academic and commercial), curating high-quality synthetic videos, and providing controlled multi-modal evaluation settings. The paper also presents a comprehensive evaluation of several SOTA deepfake detectors on the benchmark, analyzing their robustness and generalization capabilities across identity and generator shifts. Furthermore, it includes an error analysis using Grad-CAM visualizations to identify failure modes and detector biases. The benchmark and code are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty in several aspects. First, the use of current, sophisticated talking-head generators is a major advancement, as existing benchmarks rely on older, easier-to-detect techniques. Second, the meticulous curation of the dataset, removing low-quality samples, ensures a more realistic and challenging evaluation. Third, the designed evaluation protocols, explicitly studying identity and generator shifts, provide more detailed insights into detector performance compared to standard aggregate metrics.  The integration of a commercial generator is also an important inclusion, reflecting real-world capabilities and the gap between academic research and commercial products. The use of Grad-CAM for error analysis contributes to understanding the limitations of current detectors.

*   **Significance:** The benchmark has the potential to significantly impact the field of deepfake detection.  It directly addresses a known problem: the inadequacy of existing datasets for evaluating detectors against modern deepfake techniques. By providing a more challenging and diverse benchmark, the paper can drive research towards more robust and generalizable detection models. The detailed analysis of detector performance, failure modes, and biases provides valuable guidance for future research directions. The public availability of the dataset and code will facilitate adoption and contribution by the research community.  The plan to actively maintain and update the benchmark further enhances its long-term value.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   State-of-the-art generators are used.
    *   High-quality curation process.
    *   Carefully designed evaluation protocols for assessing robustness and generalization.
    *   Inclusion of commercial generator.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of SOTA detectors with error analysis.
    *   Publicly available dataset and code.
    *   Plans for active maintenance and updates.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper predominantly uses open-source generators, with limited exploration of the commercial one, MAGI-1. While its inclusion is welcome, a deeper dive is limited, which could be valuable, given the differences observed.
    *   The analysis section could offer even deeper insight, for example by correlating artifact types to performance results.
    *   While the paper outlines limitations of SOTA detectors, a more in-depth discussion on potential mitigation strategies would be beneficial. The conclusion hints at domain adaptation, semantic attention, etc. but doesn't elaborate.

*   **Influence:** The benchmark is likely to become a standard resource in the deepfake detection community.  It provides a realistic and challenging testbed for evaluating new detection methods and for identifying areas where further research is needed.  It facilitates more accurate assessment of existing techniques and pushes forward improvements in both deepfake generation and detection.  This could help reduce harm by ensuring that generated media more closely aligns with reality.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper is very strong overall, it’s important to critically assess its actual impact *right now*, instead of its *potential* impact.  The identified weaknesses, particularly the shallow exploration of commercial generators and limited discussion of mitigation strategies, prevent it from receiving a truly exceptional score. However, the thorough methodology, comprehensive analysis, and commitment to maintaining the benchmark are compelling. It serves to move the field forwards substantially.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### SiLVR: A Simple Language-based Video Reasoning Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24869v1)
- **Authors**: Ce Zhang, Yan-Bo Lin, Ziyang Wang, Mohit Bansal, Gedas Bertasius
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in test-time optimization have led to remarkable reasoning capabilities in Large Language Models (LLMs), enabling them to solve highly complex problems in math and coding. However, the reasoning capabilities of multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) still significantly lag, especially for complex video-language tasks. To address this issue, we present SiLVR, a Simple Language-based Video Reasoning framework that decomposes complex video understanding into two stages. In the first stage, SiLVR transforms raw video into language-based representations using multisensory inputs, such as short clip captions and audio/speech subtitles. In the second stage, language descriptions are fed into a powerful reasoning LLM to solve complex video-language understanding tasks. To handle long-context multisensory inputs, we use an adaptive token reduction scheme, which dynamically determines the temporal granularity with which to sample the tokens. Our simple, modular, and training-free video reasoning framework achieves the best-reported results on Video-MME (long), Video-MMMU (comprehension), Video-MMLU, CGBench, and EgoLife. Furthermore, our empirical study focused on video reasoning capabilities shows that, despite not being explicitly trained on video, strong reasoning LLMs can effectively aggregate multisensory input information from video, speech, and audio for complex temporal, causal, long-context, and knowledge acquisition reasoning tasks in video. Code is available at https://github.com/CeeZh/SILVR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SiLVR, a simple and training-free framework for video reasoning tasks. It decomposes the task into two stages: (1) converting raw video into language-based representations by densely sampling short clips and using a pre-trained visual captioner, and extracting audio/speech subtitles, and (2) feeding these language descriptions into a reasoning LLM to answer questions. To handle long videos, SiLVR employs an adaptive token reduction scheme that dynamically adjusts the temporal granularity of sampled tokens. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art results on several video QA benchmarks, highlighting SiLVR's ability to integrate information from video, speech, and audio for complex reasoning tasks.  They emphasize the modularity and generalizability of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary contribution of this work lies in the architecture integrating established techniques in a very effective manner for strong performance. The approach of leveraging pre-trained visual captioning and speech recognition models in conjunction with a large language model for video reasoning is not entirely novel. However, the adaptive token reduction scheme, although simple, provides a practical solution to the long-context challenge in videos. The comprehensive experimental evaluation and the resulting state-of-the-art performance on several benchmarks contribute to the paper's novelty.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the practical and accessible nature of its approach. By demonstrating that strong reasoning performance can be achieved without extensive training or complex architectures, SiLVR lowers the barrier to entry for researchers in video-language understanding. The modularity of the framework enables researchers to easily swap out different components (e.g., captioning models, LLMs) to further improve performance. The strong results on challenging benchmarks demonstrate the potential of leveraging powerful reasoning LLMs for video understanding.

* **Strengths:**
    * Simplicity and Modularity: SiLVR's straightforward design makes it easy to understand, implement, and extend.
    * Training-Free: The absence of training requirements makes SiLVR computationally efficient and avoids the challenges associated with training large multimodal models.
    * Strong Performance: The state-of-the-art results on multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach.
    * Adaptive Token Reduction: The ability to handle long videos with limited context LLM is significant.
    * Comprehensive Evaluation: The paper includes thorough experiments and ablation studies, providing valuable insights into the framework's behavior.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * Reliance on Pre-trained Models:  SiLVR's performance is heavily dependent on the quality of the pre-trained visual captioning and speech recognition models.  If these models are inaccurate or biased, it can negatively impact the overall performance.
    * Limited Reasoning Ability of LLM in Isolation: The framework relies heavily on the reasoning capabilities of the LLM and while its effective, there are no specific strategies of improving this (short of using a larger LLM).

* **Impact:** SiLVR is likely to serve as a strong baseline for future research in video-language understanding, encouraging the development of more sophisticated reasoning strategies and more effective methods for integrating information from different modalities. The simplicity and modularity of the framework will facilitate further exploration and experimentation in this field.

**Justification for Score:**

While the individual components of SiLVR are not entirely new, their combination, along with the adaptive token reduction strategy, results in a highly effective and practical framework. The paper's significant contribution lies in demonstrating that strong reasoning performance can be achieved with a relatively simple and training-free approach. This simplifies video understanding tasks which is key for broader adoption. While reliance on pre-trained models and limited reasoning capability are concerns, the gains achieved in the paper outweigh these concerns. Thus, it is a valuable contribution.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### GenSpace: Benchmarking Spatially-Aware Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24870v1)
- **Authors**: Zehan Wang, Jiayang Xu, Ziang Zhang, Tianyu Pan, Chao Du, Hengshuang Zhao, Zhou Zhao
- **Abstract**: Humans can intuitively compose and arrange scenes in the 3D space for photography. However, can advanced AI image generators plan scenes with similar 3D spatial awareness when creating images from text or image prompts? We present GenSpace, a novel benchmark and evaluation pipeline to comprehensively assess the spatial awareness of current image generation models. Furthermore, standard evaluations using general Vision-Language Models (VLMs) frequently fail to capture the detailed spatial errors. To handle this challenge, we propose a specialized evaluation pipeline and metric, which reconstructs 3D scene geometry using multiple visual foundation models and provides a more accurate and human-aligned metric of spatial faithfulness. Our findings show that while AI models create visually appealing images and can follow general instructions, they struggle with specific 3D details like object placement, relationships, and measurements. We summarize three core limitations in the spatial perception of current state-of-the-art image generation models: 1) Object Perspective Understanding, 2) Egocentric-Allocentric Transformation and 3) Metric Measurement Adherence, highlighting possible directions for improving spatial intelligence in image generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GenSpace paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GenSpace, a novel benchmark and evaluation pipeline designed to assess the spatial awareness capabilities of image generation models. It tackles the limitations of existing benchmarks that rely on general-purpose Vision-Language Models (VLMs), which often fail to capture detailed spatial errors. GenSpace categorizes spatial awareness into three dimensions: Spatial Pose, Spatial Relation, and Spatial Measurement, further dividing these into nine sub-domains. It proposes a specialized evaluation pipeline that reconstructs 3D scene geometry using multiple visual foundation models to provide a more accurate, human-aligned metric of spatial faithfulness. The authors evaluate several leading image generation models (both open and closed source) and reveal that, despite creating visually appealing images, these models struggle with specific 3D details like object placement, relationships, and measurements. The paper identifies three core limitations of current models: Object Perspective Understanding, Egocentric-Allocentric Transformation, and Metric Measurement Adherence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution lies in its comprehensive benchmark, evaluation pipeline, and analysis of spatial awareness in image generation. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on image-text alignment or common-sense reasoning, but do not emphasize spatial fidelity in the way GenSpace does. The taxonomy of spatial awareness (pose, relation, measurement) provides a structured framework for analyzing model performance. The pipeline, by leveraging multiple visual foundation models for 3D reconstruction, offers a novel approach to evaluating spatial correctness beyond VLM-based methods.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of image generation models. While visual fidelity has improved dramatically, the ability to control and understand spatial relationships remains a challenge. The paper's findings highlight the limitations of current state-of-the-art models in precisely controlling 3D details and adhering to spatial measurements. By identifying these limitations, the paper points the way for future research aimed at improving spatial intelligence in image generation. The thorough evaluation of various models also provides a valuable resource for the research community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:**  GenSpace provides a well-structured benchmark covering a wide range of spatial reasoning tasks. The categorization into three dimensions is logical and allows for targeted evaluation. The curated prompts and instructions are well-designed.
    *   **Innovative Evaluation Pipeline:**  The use of multiple visual foundation models for 3D scene reconstruction is a significant improvement over relying solely on VLMs, which have demonstrated spatial reasoning limitations.  The evaluation metric's breakdown into object presence, spatial difference analysis, and quantitative score mapping is clearly defined.
    *   **Clear Identification of Limitations:** The paper's articulation of three core limitations (Object Perspective, Egocentric-Allocentric Transformation, Metric Measurement Adherence) provides specific targets for future research efforts.
    *   **Human Alignment of Metrics:** Testing alternative scoring methods with manual annotation data provides insight into model alignment with human perception.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Foundation Models:** While the approach of using multiple foundation models for evaluation is innovative, the performance of the pipeline is inherently dependent on the accuracy and robustness of these underlying models.  Errors or biases in these models could affect the evaluation results.
    *   **Scope of Spatial Awareness:** The taxonomy is good, but it is possible that more levels could be added such as temporal consistency for video generation.
    *   **Complexity:** The evaluation pipeline, although improved, can be challenging to replicate due to the involved visual foundation models and their dependencies.

*   **Potential Impact:**  GenSpace has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating spatial awareness in image generation models. It could drive the development of new models that are better at controlling and understanding spatial relationships, leading to improvements in controllable generation, artistic creation, and AR/VR applications. The identification of core limitations provides specific directions for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper doesn't present a revolutionary new technique in image generation itself, its contribution lies in the development of a crucial evaluation methodology. The benchmark, combined with the robust evaluation pipeline, makes a significant contribution to the field by highlighting the limitations of existing models and providing a clear path for future development.  The thoroughness of the evaluation and the clear articulation of core limitations are particularly valuable. The score reflects the strong methodology and potential impact, tempered by some limitations in the reliability and complexity of the evaluation.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### MoDoMoDo: Multi-Domain Data Mixtures for Multimodal LLM Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24871v1)
- **Authors**: Yiqing Liang, Jielin Qiu, Wenhao Ding, Zuxin Liu, James Tompkin, Mengdi Xu, Mengzhou Xia, Zhengzhong Tu, Laixi Shi, Jiacheng Zhu
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has recently emerged as a powerful paradigm for post-training large language models (LLMs), achieving state-of-the-art performance on tasks with structured, verifiable answers. Applying RLVR to Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) presents significant opportunities but is complicated by the broader, heterogeneous nature of vision-language tasks that demand nuanced visual, logical, and spatial capabilities. As such, training MLLMs using RLVR on multiple datasets could be beneficial but creates challenges with conflicting objectives from interaction among diverse datasets, highlighting the need for optimal dataset mixture strategies to improve generalization and reasoning. We introduce a systematic post-training framework for Multimodal LLM RLVR, featuring a rigorous data mixture problem formulation and benchmark implementation. Specifically, (1) We developed a multimodal RLVR framework for multi-dataset post-training by curating a dataset that contains different verifiable vision-language problems and enabling multi-domain online RL learning with different verifiable rewards; (2) We proposed a data mixture strategy that learns to predict the RL fine-tuning outcome from the data mixture distribution, and consequently optimizes the best mixture. Comprehensive experiments showcase that multi-domain RLVR training, when combined with mixture prediction strategies, can significantly boost MLLM general reasoning capacities. Our best mixture improves the post-trained model's accuracy on out-of-distribution benchmarks by an average of 5.24% compared to the same model post-trained with uniform data mixture, and by a total of 20.74% compared to the pre-finetuning baseline.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "MoDoMoDo: Multi-Domain Data Mixtures for Multimodal LLM Reinforcement Learning" addresses the challenge of training multimodal large language models (MLLMs) using Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) across diverse vision-language tasks.  The key idea is to create a framework where multiple datasets, each with its own verifiable reward signal, are mixed in an optimal way during RLVR fine-tuning.  The paper introduces a system that learns to predict the RL fine-tuning outcome from a given data mixture distribution and then optimizes the mixture based on this prediction.  Experiments demonstrate that this approach can significantly improve the general reasoning capacities of MLLMs, particularly on out-of-distribution benchmarks, compared to uniform or naive data mixtures. The method includes developing a multi-modal RLVR environment, formulating a data mixture optimization strategy, and demonstrating empirical results on MMMU, MathVista, InfoVQA, and ChartQA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic approach to data mixture optimization in the context of RLVR for MLLMs. While data mixture strategies are not entirely new in machine learning or even in pre-training LLMs, their application to RLVR for MLLMs is a relatively unexplored area. The idea of learning a surrogate model to predict RL fine-tuning outcomes given data mixture is innovative, particularly the use of multivariate quadratic functions to capture counterfactual interplay between datasets.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical challenge in scaling RLVR to MLLMs: the need for large amounts of labeled data across diverse modalities and tasks. By effectively mixing existing datasets with verifiable rewards, the approach can potentially improve the generalization and reasoning abilities of MLLMs without requiring extensive new data annotation. This is particularly important for tasks requiring nuanced visual, logical, and spatial capabilities. The empirical results, showing improvements on out-of-distribution benchmarks, support the practical significance of the proposed method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Formulation:** The paper provides a clear and rigorous problem formulation of the data mixture optimization challenge in MLLM RLVR.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The framework provides a systematic and well-defined approach that is helpful in further developing methods to address the complex training process.
    *   **Surrogate Modeling:** The innovative surrogate modeling approach, which is used to train the data-mixtures, is novel.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper is well supported by experimental results across diverse datasets (MMMU, MathVista, InfoVQA, ChartQA), including out-of-distribution scenarios.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The writing is clear and well organized, making the paper accessible.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Surrogate Model Complexity:** While the choice of a quadratic surrogate model is justified, the method would be improved if it provided further insight into the model's complexity. Also, it might be interesting to see if other non-parametric models could also be used.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** While the paper demonstrates the method's effectiveness, questions remain on how well the surrogate modeling approach scales to a much larger number of datasets or more complex task combinations. Also, more experimentation would be beneficial.
    *   **Dataset Choice:** While the datasets chosen are diverse, additional experiments with datasets that reflect more open-ended visual intelligence capabilities would strengthen the contribution.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in MLLM training by providing a practical framework for optimizing data mixtures in RLVR settings. The approach could be extended to other modalities (audio, video, etc.) and tasks. It could also inspire further work on developing more efficient and scalable surrogate modeling techniques for data mixture optimization.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign this paper a score of **7**.

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by addressing an important challenge in training MLLMs with RLVR. The systematic approach to data mixture optimization and the use of surrogate modeling are novel and potentially impactful. The empirical results are compelling and support the practical significance of the method. However, the limited exploration of surrogate model complexity and the questions regarding scalability temper the overall impact. Future research building upon this work could further strengthen its contribution and potentially raise the score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MiniMax-Remover: Taming Bad Noise Helps Video Object Removal
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24873v1)
- **Authors**: Bojia Zi, Weixuan Peng, Xianbiao Qi, Jianan Wang, Shihao Zhao, Rong Xiao, Kam-Fai Wong
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in video diffusion models have driven rapid progress in video editing techniques. However, video object removal, a critical subtask of video editing, remains challenging due to issues such as hallucinated objects and visual artifacts. Furthermore, existing methods often rely on computationally expensive sampling procedures and classifier-free guidance (CFG), resulting in slow inference. To address these limitations, we propose MiniMax-Remover, a novel two-stage video object removal approach. Motivated by the observation that text condition is not best suited for this task, we simplify the pretrained video generation model by removing textual input and cross-attention layers, resulting in a more lightweight and efficient model architecture in the first stage. In the second stage, we distilled our remover on successful videos produced by the stage-1 model and curated by human annotators, using a minimax optimization strategy to further improve editing quality and inference speed. Specifically, the inner maximization identifies adversarial input noise ("bad noise") that makes failure removals, while the outer minimization step trains the model to generate high-quality removal results even under such challenging conditions. As a result, our method achieves a state-of-the-art video object removal results with as few as 6 sampling steps and doesn't rely on CFG, significantly improving inference efficiency. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and superiority of MiniMax-Remover compared to existing methods. Codes and Videos are available at: https://minimax-remover.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "MiniMax-Remover: Taming Bad Noise Helps Video Object Removal" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MiniMax-Remover, a two-stage approach to video object removal designed to improve both visual quality and inference efficiency.  Stage 1 trains a lightweight DiT-based architecture, simplifying a pre-trained video generation model by removing textual input and cross-attention layers.  Crucially, this stage incorporates contrastive condition tokens to guide the inpainting process. Stage 2 distills the remover on high-quality object removal samples curated by human annotators, leveraging a minimax optimization strategy.  This strategy identifies adversarial input noise ("bad noise") leading to removal failures and trains the model to be robust under such conditions. The method achieves state-of-the-art video object removal results with significantly fewer sampling steps and without relying on classifier-free guidance (CFG), substantially improving inference speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel elements. The simplification of the DiT architecture by removing cross-attention layers and employing contrastive condition tokens for object removal is a unique approach. The minimax optimization strategy tailored for video object removal, especially the "bad noise" identification and robustness training, is innovative. The two-stage framework, which distills human-curated results to boost performance, is also a worthwhile contribution.  However, it builds heavily upon existing diffusion architectures and inpainting techniques. A fair amount of prior work has explored distillation methods as well as minimax based generative models.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses the critical challenge of video object removal, which is essential for video editing applications. The improvement in inference efficiency is substantial, making the method more practical for real-world use. The focus on robustness against adversarial noise is also significant, as it leads to more consistent and artifact-free results. The demonstrated improvements over existing methods on standard datasets validate the effectiveness of the approach. The human evaluation aspect is a positive addition, lending credibility to the visual quality assessment. While a good step forward, it is more incremental than revolutionary. It will likely not change how other researchers approach these problems, but rather will add to the available pool of ideas.

* **Strengths:**
    *   Significant improvement in inference speed compared to existing diffusion-based methods.
    *   Effective minimax optimization strategy that enhances robustness and visual quality.
    *   Human-guided curation of the training dataset, leading to better performance.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on standard benchmarks, including ablation studies.
    *   Clear and well-written paper with a detailed explanation of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on a human-curated dataset in Stage 2, which may limit scalability and introduce bias. The details of the human curation process and the criteria for "high-quality" removals could be further elaborated.
    * The paper builds upon and adapts existing architectures and techniques (Diffusion models, DiT, adversarial noise injection in GANs). The core is well-executed and brings good improvement, it remains somewhat incremental rather than disruptive.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence research in video editing and inpainting by demonstrating the effectiveness of lightweight architectures, contrastive condition tokens, and minimax optimization. The focus on inference efficiency is valuable, and the method could be incorporated into practical video editing tools.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper demonstrates important advancements in video object removal, enhancing efficiency and visual quality, several factors temper the evaluation. The reliance on manual curation could limit its practical application in large-scale automated scenarios. Further, while the adaptation of minimax strategies for robust training and the optimization of DiT networks are valuable, these contributions, while helpful, don't fundamentally reshape the landscape of video editing research. Therefore, the score reflects a recognition of solid, incremental progress rather than a breakthrough.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10
